#350. Steinmeier in China: A major state visit in uncertain times

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Frank-Walter Steinmeier is traveling to China on a state visit in a period of political and economic uncertainty. Germany is seeking alternative partners in the region but also wants good ties with Beijing.

Eight years have passed since Frank-Walter Steinmeier's last trip to China. Since then, the relationship between China and Europe has changed significantly. The German president plans to spend several days touring China before concluding his visit in Beijing, where he will meet with China's leaders.

His visit comes just as the Chinese Communist Party's Central Committee is convening for its plenary session, a major annual event that foreign dignitaries usually avoid when timing their visits.

The focus of the president's five-day trip will be "Germany's diverse and complex relations with China, as well as the challenges involved," his office said in a statement. In addition to sites of cultural exchange, the itinerary will also include a visit to a Volkswagen plant in Hefei.

The automotive industry, in particular, illustrates the dilemma facing Germany's China policy. China is one of the most important markets for German automotive and machinery manufacturers, as well as for the chemical industry. At the same time, state-supported overcapacity in China is putting pressure on European manufacturers.

Not only that. Germany and Europe are dependent on China for raw materials and technologies, which means they are increasingly seeking out alternative partnerships.

Steinmeier was once an advocate of the policy of "change through trade" — the idea that other countries could be persuaded to become more democratic with the leverage of global trade — which has few adherents left in Germany today.

Now he must now pursue a tougher European line in Beijing. Caught between partnership and competition, Europe leaders recently managed to reach an agreement: Imports of plug-in hybrid vehicles from China are to be scaled back.

"That was a concrete step, but it can only be the beginning," said Michael Laha, an expert on China at the German Council on Foreign Relations (DGAP) in Berlin.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Coinciding with Steinmeier's state visit to China, Chancellor Friedrich Merz is traveling to the Indo-Pacific with business representatives. His visit to Japan, Singapore and South Korea will focus on competitiveness, technological upheavals, defense cooperation and critical raw materials. Merz is visiting companies and attending important regional economic conferences in Tokyo and Seoul.

The dual trips are Berlin's attempt to send a twofold message: While the chancellor is "deliberately strengthening Germany's partnerships in the Indo-Pacific at a time of global upheaval," according to Laha, the president is seeking dialogue with Beijing at a time when Germany and Europe are recalibrating their relationship with China.

"This joint trip sends a very well-coordinated message to the Chinese that we remain committed to the partnership but at the same time are taking steps to reduce risk," said Laha, before adding that this is a difficult balancing act that also allows China to save face.

While Merz will be accompanied by a large economic delegation, Steinmeier will be traveling to China with representatives from German culture, politics, and academia. Mikko Huotari, director of the China Institute MERICS, is also expected to be part of the delegation — even though Beijing has imposed sanctions on the institute. "The fact that he is coming along sends a clear message to the Chinese side that we do not approve of the sanctions," said Laha.

In 2021, the EU imposed sanctions against several Chinese party and regional officials, as well as an organization accused of serious human rights violations and oppressing the Uyghur minority. It was the first time such sanctions had been imposed on China since the Tiananmen Square massacre in 1989, and Beijing did not take kindly to them. In response, the Chinese government imposed entry bans and other sanctions against European politicians, scholars, and institutions, including MERICS.

Laha did not expect human rights to play a major role in the German president's trip. Steinmeier is considered a pragmatist when it comes to China policy. As foreign minister, he distanced himself from his predecessor Joschka Fischer, a critic of China, and even criticized Chancellor Angela Merkel when she hosted the Dalai Lama.

On the subject of Ukraine, Steinmeier is likely to be more direct and raise the issue of China's role in the conflict. "Beijing is clearly on Moscow's side," said Laha. In Berlin, however, officials are convinced that China can do more to persuade Russia to engage in serious negotiations and find a solution to the conflict.

Steinmeier may make this clear in his speech at Peking University on Thursday. Germany has "long viewed China's emergence with respect and goodwill," read a statement from the president's office. Today, however, the focus is less on China's continued rise and more on how it uses its growing power.

"If the president points out that Russia used its power to attack a sovereign state, he could be alluding to another regional connection: Taiwan," said Laha. That could be seen as an appeal to Beijing to assume more responsibility for the regional and international order.

To conclude his visit, Steinmeier will meet with China's leadership in Beijing. Talks are planned with President Xi Jinping, whom he has known for nearly two decades, as well as with Premier Li Qiang and the chairman of the National People's Congress, Zhao Leji.

The trip marks the culmination of Steinmeier's focus on Asia during his second term in office. Since then, he has deliberately sought to forge close ties with partners in the region and has advocated for greater diversification. Now China, which is Germany's most important and most challenging partner, will come to its own conclusion.

This article was originally published in German.

#340. What Germany thinks of carmaker Audi's $4 billion deal with Trump

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The luxury carmaker is set to open its first factory in the United States — a move Trump credits to his tariffs. DW examines the German reaction amid a massive auto industry cull at home.

US President Donald Trump announced the investment on Saturday, during a campaign rally in Georgia, a key battleground state for his Republican Party in the midterm elections next week.

Audi quickly confirmed the plan, saying it would build its first US factory near Macon, Georgia, costing around $4 billion (€3.6 billion) and creating some 4,500 jobs.

The German automaker said construction would begin next year, with a planned annual production of up to 200,000 vehicles due to start in the early 2030s.

The firm plans to build premium SUVs for the US market at the plant, marking a "long-term commitment" to what it said was "one of the world's most important premium markets."

The US investment comes as German automakers face unprecedented pressure from China, rising costs and two conflicts.

Backed by huge government subsidies, Chinese rivals have stolen a march in the transition to electric vehicles (EVs), scaling up production of new models that are fast gaining market share in a crowded global auto market.

BMW, Mercedes-Benz and Audi's parent company, Volkswagen— the world's second-largest automaker — have all issued profit warnings in recent months as margins come under pressure.

They've also announced major cost-cutting. VW is preparing to axe up to 100,000 jobs by 2030, while Audi's factory in Neckarsulm, which has around 15,000 staff, is one of four plants at risk of closure.

Trump credited his 15% tariffs on European-made vehicles for Audi's investment, saying it "would not have happened without tariffs."

While the commitment does feed into Trump's plan to bring manufacturing back to the United States from abroad, Audi said it was making the huge outlay for growth and resilience.

“We have much greater ambitions for Audi in America," said the carmaker's CEO Gernot Döllner, who appeared alongside Trump at the Republican's campaign rally.

"The United States is one of the world's most important premium markets, and we have not yet realized our full potential. We are determined to change that ... This is about building a stronger Audi business in America — not only about building a factory," he said.

However, Trump's tariffs have undoubtedly added to the automaker's woes.

Audi, which currently exports to the US market from Europe and Mexico, said the additional costs amounted to about €1.2 billion ($1.3 billion) last year.

With more than 160,000 vehicles sold, Audi's US sales last year fell 16%, amid intense competition and after Trump scrapped subsidies on electric vehicles in its second-biggest market.

By building a US factory, Audi should be able to avoid or reduce Trump's tariffs, industry analysts say.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

There's been fierce criticism of the deal due to the severe pressure facing German automakers and because the new US investment could mean further job losses at home.

Much of the criticism focused on Döllner's rally appearance alongside Trump.

The center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD)'s transport policy spokesperson Isabel Cademartori described it as campaign assistance for an autocrat who is waging a trade war against Germany.

"This is a disgrace, especially considering the history of the Volkswagen Group," she stated, referring to the company's links with Adolf Hitler's Nazi party during World War II.

Christiane Benner, president of the powerful IG Metall union, labeled the rally appearance as "extremely embarrassing, politically unacceptable and not worthy of the company."

VW works council chief Daniela Cavallo went further, saying Döllner had let himself be turned "into a puppet in [Trump's] propaganda show."

Cavallo, however, noted that the US plant could make sense from a business perspective, as VW needs to grow its market share there.

An Audi spokesperson defended the US investment, saying it was "not a decision against Germany, but rather a step toward strengthening our global competitiveness."

Manfred Döss, chairman of Audi's supervisory board, insisted the automaker's future cannot be secured by cost-cutting and efficiency alone.

"We need the courage to invest, to seize new opportunities and to make decisions that will shape our company for decades to come," he added.

The announcement certainly appears timed to coincide with the US midterm elections on November 3.

Voters will decide whether the Republicans or Democrats hold the balance of power in Congress during Trump's last two years in office.

Holding the announcement in the key battleground state of Georgia is seen as a strategic move to boost the fortunes of local Republican candidates.

Since he returned to the White House last year, Trump has used tariffs and political pressure to force global automakers to relocate their factories to American soil.

Audi's investment follows moves by Stellantis and Japan's Toyota to expand US production, while Honda is in talks to build a new US plant.

US auto giants General Motors and Ford have also promised to increase domestic production.

While one investment announcement may not be enough to sway many voters, it serves one of Trump's main election promises — to revive industrial production in the US.

#275. Russia in Africa: What is Putin hoping to achieve?

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Following its invasion of Ukraine and the resulting rift with the West, Russia has declared that it intends to make a "historic return" to Africa. What have we seen of this so far?

The conflict with the West sparked by Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine  prompted Russian President Vladimir Putin to seek support from other parts of the world. Africa has become a focus, with foreign minister Sergei Lavrov visiting the continent increasingly often.

Russian foreign ministry spokesperson Maria Zakharova has described it as a "historic return," and declared no one and nothing could be compared to what Russia did for Africa in the 20th century.

A Russia-Africa summit has been held in Russia at roughly three-year intervals since 2019. The third takes place in Moscow on October 28 and 29. The program focusses on economic cooperation. Other topics range from politics and security to science and culture. But Russia is actively working with only a few African countries, mainly in the north of the continent, and this is reflected in the trade figures.

"South Africa is still Africa's largest national economy, but its volume of trade with Russia is small, far less than with Egypt and Algeria," says Ivan Klyszcz from the International Centre for Defence and Security (ICDS) in Tallinn, Estonia.

"Egypt and Algeria in particular are key partners for Russia. Egypt plays an important role in this regard. Russian diplomats and business representatives are trying to extend relationships with other countries, especially Tanzania and Ethiopia, but with no appreciable success so far."

Egypt is the second-largest economy in Africa, and the bilateral volume of trade with Russia has more than doubled since 2021; in 2025, according to Russia's Federal Customs Service, it was $10.5 billion (€9.4 billion). Consignments of Russian grain are the main drivers, followed by products of the metal industry. Egypt's exports to Russia are primarily fruit and vegetables.

There are investment projects, too. Russia is building a big nuclear power plant in the Egyptian region of El-Dabaa on the Mediterranean coast. Construction is being financed by a Russian loan of 25 billion US dollars, which covers around 85% of the costs.

Egypt is one of the few African countries with which Russia enjoys stable business relationships, as well as having extensive experience of cooperation, says Nikita Smagin, an expert on Russian policy in the Middle East. "In the long term, Egypt could become Moscow's gateway to Africa and to other markets," he says. "But Cairo is pursuing its own interests. It would be too optimistic to hope that Egypt is lending its support purely out of political solidarity."

According to the Russian customs service, around 39% of Russian–African trade in 2025 was with Egypt, and total sales between Russia and all 54 countries of the African continent came to around 27 billion US dollars. Africa's share of Russia's foreign trade is therefore very small (3.9% in 2025), and not at all commensurate with the attention Moscow is devoting to its relations with African countries.

Russia is not a significant trading partner for Africa, either. The most important partner for African countries is the European Union. In 2025, the volume of trade with the EU came to around 385 billion US dollars. African countries' statistics also show that other partners were well ahead of Russia. The volume of trade with China was in excess of 348 billion US dollars in 2025; with the United States it was almost 105 billion, and 103 billion with India.

Fidel Owusu, a Ghanaian expert with Geopolitical Intelligence Services (GIS) in Liechtenstein, comments that Russia's economic presence in Africa has not been as significant as that of China, the United States, India, or other traditional powers like France and Britain. "If you look at the volume of trade and the kind of investment," he says, "in recent times, some middle powers such as Turkey and the UAW are doing far better than Russia."

Beverly Ochieng, a senior analyst with Control Risks, a global risk and strategic intelligence consultancy in Senegal, observes: "Western countries continue to dominate in strategically important areas such as raw material extraction, finance, and the energy sector, even if their political presence in the region is declining." According to her, "Russia will compete more intensively for access to critical mineral resources and energy projects. We see this with lithium projects in Mali as well as with nuclear power initiatives across the continent."

Russia's presence in Africa is more obvious in the military sector than in the fields of trade and investment. Starting with the arrival of the Wagner Group, Russian mercenaries have been supporting African regimes, particularly those of the unstable countries in the Sahel region. In exchange, their associated subsidiaries are granted access to raw materials. Following the death of the Wagner Group leader, Yevgeny Prigozhin, most of these operations were taken over by the Africa Corps, a paramilitary group set up and controlled by Russia's defense ministry.

According to the Congressional Research Service, the think tank of the US Congress, the largest Russian military contingents, each comprising several thousand men, are in the Central African Republic, Mali, and Libya. The Africa Corps is also keen to extend its presence in Burkina Faso and Niger. Russian mercenaries have also been spotted in Equatorial Guinea.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Irina Filatova from the University of Cape Town stresses that there are several reasons why it's important for Russia to maintain a military presence in Africa. "The countries have sizeable deposits of minerals such as gold and uranium. Through them, it's possible to exert influence over Europe's southern borders, including in matters of migration and regional security. With regard to Libya, the country is an important logistical hub for connections with the Sahel and central Africa. Goods can easily be supplied via Libya to countries without access to the sea," Filatova says.

Ultimately, Russia is still one of the biggest suppliers of arms to African countries, even though Russian exports have fallen over the course of the war in Ukraine. The Stockholm International Peace Research Institute (SIPRI) estimates Moscow's share of the African market for the years 2021–2025 at 15%, which puts it in third place behind the United States (19%) and China (17%). Between 2020 and 2024, its share was even higher: It was in first place with 21%, followed by China with 18%, and the US with 16%.

Ivan Klyszcz from the ICDS says that while Russia is not a significant economic partner for Africa, the fact that it supplies food and fertilizer and has a military presence in some countries means that Moscow's influence in Africa is far greater than is suggested by the volume of trade and investment alone.

"I believe Russia sees the diplomatic weight of Africa," says Fidel Owusu. "When I talk of diplomatic weight, I mean numbers – the continent has the largest number of countries. Russia wants that kind of support in the UN General Assembly whenever votes are cast. In recent times it has done better than in earlier votes."

In recent years, Russia has been positioning itself on the international stage as representing the interests of the Global South.

"We want to work with Africa, we are working with Africa and we will continue to do so. But our aim is not, as it was for the colonial powers, to make money or steal something from Africa, but to develop together," Russian president Vladimir Putin said recently at a conference of the Moscow-based Valdai Discussion Club.

References to the colonial past appeal to some countries but not others, says Irina Filatova. "Africa is not a country, it's a continent. There are differences in the perception of Russia, as well as in the way Russia positions itself. Also, the views of the political elites are often substantially different from those of ordinary citizens. In South Africa, for instance, the Russian narrative is received very positively. An anti-colonial agenda is also popular in other countries, but it isn't necessarily associated with Russia."

Antonio Cascais contributed to this report. It was originally written in Russian.

#266. EU's 'Made in Europe' push unnerves global auto suppliers

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In shielding Europe's carmakers from Chinese rivals, the EU risks hurting close partners from Turkey and Morocco to Japan and South Korea. Billions in trade and hundreds of thousands of European jobs are at stake.

It is meant to save Europe's auto industry from China's unfair trade practices. The European Union's Industrial Accelerator Act (IAA), proposed in March, seeks to upgrade European car factories and only subsidize vehicles produced in Europe.

Yet, the EU's "Made in Europe" plan is causing deep concern among other allied carmaking nations — such as Turkey, Morocco, Japan and South Korea — all of them deeply embedded in European supply chains.

After months of deadlock over the IAA's potential impact on their own vehicle production, Germany and France earlier this month reached a key compromise that would allow suppliers from non-EU members like the United Kingdom and Norway to qualify for "European origin" status.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Other key players in the automotive sector, however, will be excluded — including Turkey, which has enjoyed seamless access to the EU market since the mid-1990s.

While the proposals still face major legislative hurdles in Brussels before becoming law, Ayhan Zeytinoglu, chairman of the Economic Development Foundation (IKV), thinks the Franco-German framework could be devastating for Turkey's auto sector.

"73% of Turkey’s total automotive exports go to the EU," Zeytinoglu told DW. "If we encounter a 'Made in Europe' barrier in the EU market, this will cause great damage to our trade with the EU and to our exports."

Excluding Turkey would also harm European automaking rather than strengthen competitiveness against China, he added.

Turkey's auto exporters association OIB calculated that the country's automotive exports to the EU totaled $30.1 billion (€26.8 billion) last year.

The IAA requires that 70% of the total value of a vehicle's components — excluding the battery — should be made in Europe.

However, the initial proposal only applies to battery-electric vehicles (EVs), plug-in hybrids and fuel-cell models, which last year made up just 27% of all new EU car registrations.

Tommaso Pardi, director of the international automotive research network GERPISA and a senior researcher at the French National Center for Scientific Research (CNRS), notes that the 70% target is remarkably generous.

"If you take an average European-made electric car and take away the battery, you would find that around 83% [of parts] come from Europe," Pardi told DW.

Under the revised German-French plan, some self-charging hybrids and petrol and diesel vehicles could still be sold after 2035 and would also be affected by the "Made in Europe" rules.

These cars contain a much lower proportion of European parts. Most of Turkey's nearly 800,000 vehicle exports to the EU last year would fall under this much larger umbrella.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Another key reason for excluding Turkey is that Chinese automakers are expanding their presence there, hoping to gain back-door access to the EU market, but face major roadblocks.

They include SWM Motors, whose factory in the northwestern city of Eskisehir closed within a year because the cars produced didn't meet EU safety standards.

BYD's plans to open a $1 billion plant in Manisa — a large industrial hub near the Aegean coast — have been put on hold.

Morocco, meanwhile, has received Chinese investments worth more than €10 billion ($11 billion) since the COVID-19 pandemic, according to consultancy AlixPartners, much of it in batteries and auto parts.

For France, the North African nation plays a similar role to China for Germany's auto sector — a low-cost production hub that helps French carmakers stay competitive. Budget-friendly French models would likely become more expensive if Morocco were excluded.

Japanese carmakers, which build several of their models in European factories, could also lose out. Cars imported from Japan would not qualify for EU subsidies, while models built in France, Portugal and Eastern Europe would benefit only if enough of their parts come from Europe.

South Korean automakers, meanwhile, could also be hurt. Hyundai and Kia build cars in the Czech Republic and Slovakia, but also ship models from Asia, which would not qualify. Korean battery plants in Poland and Hungary, however, would count as European.

Pardi notes how European car giants are actively lobbying to water down the IAA to protect their high profit margins.

"They want to limit the IAA to retain the biggest scope to still source from abroad, particularly from China," he said.

EU-based automakers warn that higher parts costs would put them at an even bigger disadvantage against Chinese rivals, while European battery supply is unlikely to meet demand anytime soon.

Ankara is also lobbying Brussels to be included in the "Made in Europe" program, while automakers are seeking a compromise that would allow Turkey to join at a later stage.

Pardi said this would be "only for European companies that are based there [Turkey and Morocco] ... while excluding all recent investments involving Chinese capital [in those countries]."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

European auto-parts suppliers are, however, on the opposite side of the argument, calling for further strengthening of IAA. They warn that up to 650,000 auto jobs in the region could be lost by 2035 without better protection for their members.

"A lost industrial job is very hard to win back, and the damage reaches well beyond large suppliers," Benjamin Krieger, Secretary General of the European Association of Automotive Suppliers (CLEPA), told DW.

Krieger thinks the IAA helps European industry to level the playing field against unfair competition from China, adding: "We must stop the bleeding."

With broad public support for measures that ensure that EU public funding "benefits value creation in Europe," CLEPA's Krieger thinks allies like Turkey, Morocco, South Korea and Japan may not lose out if they meet the EU's criteria.

"The EU should offer access to the IAA through an opt-in mechanism with objective criteria: regulatory alignment, social and environmental standards, no market distortion, respect for the rule of law and reciprocity, backed by anti-circumvention measures. Partners that meet them are welcome," he said.

#199. European Central Bank (ECB) head Christine Lagarde to step down in February, before her term ends

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

European Central Bank (ECB) President Christine Lagarde is to resign nine months ahead of the end of her 8-year term. European Council President Antonio Costa has said the process to find a successor is underway.

Christine Lagarde, who has headed the European Central Bank for seven years, has announced that she is stepping down prematurely from the position.

"European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde today informed the Governing Council and the president of the European Council Antonio Costa that she will step down from her position on 7 February 2027," the ECB said in a statement.

European Council President Antonio Costa said a process to find her successor would start immediately.

The announcement comes ahead of a key meeting of the bank this week that is likely to focus on France's deteriorating public finances.

No reason has so far been given for Lagarde's premature departure, but there has been speculation that she would leave early to enable EU leaders to choose her successor before French elections in the spring that could see a eurosceptic far-right victory influence the choice.

Her term, which began on November 1, 2019, would normally have ended at the end of October 2027.

Lagarde, a former French finance minister, was also head of the International Monetary Fund from 2011 to 2019.

Costa praised Lagarde's leadership during her seven-year stint at the ECB helm, a period in which the EU and the world faced great challenges.

"Through a global pandemic, a war in Europe, energy crises and inflation shocks, she has provided a steady and determined stewardship, helping to safeguard stability and the resilience of the euro area," Costa posted on social media.

"To ensure a smooth transition to the next leadership of the ECB, the process for the appointment of her successor will be initiated today and will proceed without delay, with a view to a timely decision," Costa said.

Lagarde herself has voiced satisfaction with her record.

"I leave this institution with a sense of good work done and an ECB that is fit for the future," Lagarde, a French economist, said in a short video shared on ​social media.

The ECB head is chosen by the leaders of the 21 eurozone countries.

The announcement of Lagarde's imminent departure comes as the ECB is to hold a meeting this week where it is expected to keep its ​deposit rate at 2.5%, while signaling that this is likely to rise again before the end of the year.

The bank has already raised interest rates twice since June in a bid to curb inflation fueled by the energy shock caused by the Iran war.

However, Thursday's meeting is also likely to focus on the fiscal and political crisis in France, Europe's second-biggest economy.

French bond yields, which reflect the cost of government borrowing, rose to their highest level in 25 years last week amid a deadlock over next year's budget.

Recent protests, at which many demonstrators were calling for more spending on public services, were also marred by considerable violence.

There has been speculation as to whether the ECB could, for the first time, use the Transmission Protection Instrument (TPI),  a crisis-fighting tool that allows the bank to buy bonds of a eurozone member if yields rise uncontrollably and there is a risk of financial stress spreading.

However, most observers believe the problems in France do not yet meet the strict conditions stipulated for its use.

#170. US moves to lift sanctions on Russia's shadow fleet could undermine Western pressure on Moscow and help fund its war in Ukraine.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The US is reportedly considering ways to convert "dark tankers" into legitimate vessels, allowing them to transport crude oil that is not subject to sanctions.

Since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, Moscow has increasingly relied on a so-called shadow fleet to bypass Western sanctions and transport its oil around the world.

Other heavily sanctioned countries, including Iran and Venezuela, have also been accused of using such vessels to export petroleum products.

These tankers often rely on deceptive practices like turning off their location transponders to evade detection and conceal the cargo's true origins or destinations.

The fleet, which consists largely of aging, uninsured and poorly maintained tankers, has raised widespread concern across the shipping industry. Yet it has expanded rapidly in recent years and now accounts for nearly 19% of global oil tanker capacity, according to estimates by financial intelligence firm S&P Global.

"The shadow fleet is a scourge," Elisabeth Braw, senior fellow at the think tank Atlantic Council, said, adding that "it is in nobody's interest" to have these ships sailing around in the world's oceans.

She pointed out that these tankers undermine the maritime order and increase the risk of accidents and environmental damage.

"Every country should use its leverage with Russia to ask it not to use the fleet," she told DW, noting that ultimately all countries suffer the consequences.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

To clamp down on the fleet and curb Russia's oil export revenues, the United States and European countries have stepped up efforts to intercept tankers accused of violating sanctions.

The Trump administration, however, is reportedly considering lifting sanctions on some shadow fleet vessels and allowing them to transport oil that is not subject to sanctions.

A US official told Reuters earlier this month that the move was intended to ease record-high global freight rates.

Lifting sanctions on the shadow fleet "would decrease the effectiveness of the sanctions regime overall," said Michael Carpenter, senior fellow for Transatlantic Affairs at the International Institute for Strategic Studies (IISS). Such a move "rewards the aggressor and helps Moscow gain revenues to continue attacking Ukraine," he told DW.

"This is the exact opposite of what the US should be doing if it wants to end the war."

Carpenter, who previously served as US ambassador to the Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe (OSCE), said any sanctions relief would also put the US and the EU "strategically at odds with each other, with the EU clearly backing Ukraine and the US increasingly supporting Russia's financial goals."

This view is shared by Ben Harris, vice president and director of Economic Studies at the Brookings Institution.

Lifting sanctions on shadow fleet tankers would "make it substantially easier for Russia to trade its oil at market rates," he said, pointing out that it would "improve its government finances since so much revenue is tied to energy markets."

US Energy Secretary Chris Wright, however, rejected suggestions that the US was removing sanctions from shadow fleet tankers.

"We are converting dark tankers to legitimate tankers," Bloomberg quoted Wright as saying last week. "There is no removing of sanctions," he stressed, adding that the US government was working with investors who buy idle sanctioned tankers, refurbish them and use them to transport oil that is not subject to sanctions.

"We're going to move some of the ships from what I call the 'bad-guy fleet' to the 'good-guy fleet,'" Wright said. "They will not carry sanctioned oil anymore."

The US government has yet to unveil a detailed plan for shadow fleet tankers.

The proposal follows a deal that US President Donald Trump struck with his Russian counterpart Vladimir Putin this month to allow sales of Russian diesel onto global markets.

The agreement was seen as part of Trump's efforts to lower domestic fuel prices ahead of the US midterm elections on November 3.

The US president also waived broad new sanctions on Russia authorized by ​a recent legislation, which targeted Moscow's energy revenues and threatened tariffs against the world's largest purchasers of Russian oil and gas.

The decision was sharply criticized by Ukraine and its European allies over concerns that it could undermine the credibility of sanctions and weaken Western economic pressure on Moscow.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Harris said that "the US sanctions carry outsized weight because of the threat [for countries and businesses] of being cut off from the US financial system."

If the US steps away from its sanctions on the shadow fleet, he argued, "the responsibility shifts to European policymakers." But they have "less effective options because of the diminished threat of severing financial ties."

Still, the expert stressed that Europe has some tools, pointing to the continent's key role in global shipping and insurance services.

"They can implement measures demanding that tankers carry legitimate and established insurance when traveling through their waters," he said, noting that it "would mean that many shadow fleet tankers could not in practice legally transport Russian oil."

Harris added that European policymakers could also pressure countries worldwide to better regulate the tankers flying their flags.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Carpenter said even if US eases some restrictions, the EU will have no choice but to continue enforcing its sanctions on these vessels.

"The vast majority of the shadow fleet traverses the Baltic Sea and exits it via the Danish Strait," he noted, adding that the EU is "uniquely positioned to board and inspect vessels moving through this key chokepoint."

But Russia has threatened to seize ships belonging to European countries in retaliation for interdicting and seizing Russian vessels suspected of being part of the shadow fleet.

And without US backing, Carpenter said, "it will be harder for EU countries to take on the burdens and risks of sanctions enforcement."

Braw echoed this view, saying that Ukraine's European allies will have to remain united and continue working closely with partners such as Japan, Australia and Canada. Nevertheless, she stressed that what they can achieve on their own "is far less than what they can achieve if the US is part of the team."

#158. Volkswagen logs Q3 operating loss of €5.86 billion on Porsche writedown

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Volkswagen Group has filed its worst quarterly performance since the height of the "Dieselgate" scandal in 2015. Share prices climbed in response, however, perhaps because the bad news was so well signposted.

The Volkswagen Group on Thursday posted its worst quarterly numbers in more than a decade.

The company reported an operating loss of €5.86 billion (roughly $6.53 billion) during July, August and September. One-off costs, not least a massive writedown in its stake in struggling sister company Porsche, explained the vast bulk of the losses.

The world's second-largest carmaker is battling mounting competition from and falling demand in the core Chinese market.

It is pursuing a major restructuring and downsizing program, trying to bolster falling profit margins, and executives said the difficulties highlighted the urgency of this process.

"The nine-month results make one thing clear: We cannot afford to lose any time. We must act quickly and decisively," chief financial officer Arno Antlitz said in a video statement.

The results were the latest reminder of the pressure on VW — and the wider German and Western automotive industry — to react to increasing competition in China, high costs and practical difficulties moving towards electric car production, and unpredictable and rising tariff burdens, thanks in no small part to US President Donald Trump.

And here are some of the key year-to-date 9-month figures:

VW shares were already at their lowest levels in well over 10 years prior to Thursday's announcements, after months of difficult headlines and the ongoing plans to shed tens of thousands of jobs and reduce production capacities.

Perhaps as a result of this, the stock dipped briefly as markets opened and the news was published, but then started to rally as the day progressed.

By midday in Frankfurt, VW shares had climbed by 2% compared to their prices at the start of trading on the DAX.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Multiple factors are contributing to this. They include increased competition from Chinese manufacturers, falling revenues in China, changeable US auto tariffs under President Donald Trump, different staffing requirements when manufacturing electric cars and difficulties moving towards making and selling more electric cars at the pace once designated by political leaders.

Up to four plants in Germany could face closure as part of the plan, which has met with considerable union resistance and could lead to strikes starting next year.

CFO Antlitz said the figures showed the urgency underlying these plans, saying the company needed to increase its profit margins by 2030 to solidify its finances.

Antlitz said the operating return on sales was only 3.8% in the first nine months of the year, even before the one-off writedowns hitting the bottom line.

"That is not enough in the long term," he said.

As part of its restructuring program, the VW Group aims to restore an operating profit margin of between 8-10% by 2030.

This year, amid one-off costs now totaling in the region of €10 billion, it expects a profit margin of no more than 1%, compared to prior forecasts in the region of 5%.

#147. Can EU and India work together to secure Indo-Pacific?

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

India and Europe are deepening defense ties, but their clashing perspectives on Russia and differing priorities on China are hampering their joint ambitions on maritime security.

The European Union is vying for a larger role in Indo-Pacific security as the US finds itself on the back foot in the Eastern Hemisphere.

India is among the regional powers looking for new allies. Its rising economy and increasingly global ambitions have pushed it into new forums and partnerships, including ever closer defense ties with the European bloc.

In January this year, India and the EU signed a Security and Defense Partnership, followed by their first formal Security and Defense Dialogue in March. More meetings followed throughout the year, including the fifth Maritime Security Dialogue in Brussels last month.

However, while both sides want to boost cooperation on maritime security, working together in the Indo-Pacific remains a challenge — India and European countries simply have different defense priorities.

"Europe can be a credible defense partner of India, especially in the maritime theater in general and Indo-Pacific in particular," Mohan Kumar, a former Indian ambassador to France, told DW.

But Europe's capacity to expand its role is constrained by other security crises.

"European distractions such as the conflict in Ukraine, which is of existential importance for it, do detract from this objective and can be a limiting factor," he added.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

France is a notable exception, given its established military presence in the Indian Ocean.

"France, with its presence in Reunion Island and Mayotte, is already cooperating with India in the Indian Ocean theater and this cooperation can be intensified," added Kumar.

Javed Ashraf, another Indian diplomat, said that France has been "a strong, trusted and consistent security partner across the full spectrum of defense cooperation."

"What makes the partnership easier is the acceptance that the two may not always agree or be in a position to cooperate," said Ashraf.

"France also stands out for India because [France] has currently no defense cooperation with Pakistan or with China."

While France remains the EU's only nuclear power and maintains its strongest military force, a cooperation between Paris and New Delhi does not automatically translate into a coherent European security role across the Indo-Pacific.

"Europe as an entity has very little role in the Indo-Pacific security despite the announcement of a strategy in 2025," said Tara Kartha, a former member of India's National Security Council Secretariat.

She pointed to political differences among EU members as an obstacle to a coherent security outlook.

Those differences were on display during the EU's October 15–16 summit in Brussels, when leaders reaffirmed support for Ukraine, but also signaled disagreements over funding and national priorities.

This could also explain why, as India's defense ties with Europe keep expanding, much of that cooperation still takes place between New Delhi and individual European nations.

Germany, Spain, Italy and Poland have all emerged as important partners.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Ashraf said Brussels could signal broader support for its member states' partnerships with India and provide access to EU-funded programs, boosting cooperation in areas such as maritime security, space, critical minerals, chips and defense technologies.

"India is also becoming an important source of defense supplies for a re-arming Europe," the diplomat added.

There may also be opportunities to share experience in emerging areas of warfare.

"India could also access drone technology in light of valuable experience gained by Europeans in Ukraine," Kumar said.

Germany, the EU's largest economy, is another important part of this relationship. Alongside France, it has become a defense partner for India, including through major equipment purchases. India could push for its relationship with Berlin to include industrial partnerships and technology development.

But experts warn that closer defense ties should not be confused with a shared strategic outlook, as European countries have their own priorities.

The differences between Brussels and New Delhi are particularly clear when it comes to Russia and China.

India still relies heavily on Russian equipment and technology, even as it seeks to diversify its defense supplies. European governments, meanwhile, have been engaged in a tense standoff with Russia since its 2022 invasion of Ukraine.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

That creates a problem for those seeking to deepen defense industry ties.

"Buying a few big-ticket defense items, such as Rafale fighter jets from France or submarines from Germany, is one thing," said Gulshan Sachdeva, a professor of European Studies at Jawaharlal Nehru University in New Delhi.

"However, integrating India's defense industry into the expanding European defense value chain is more challenging as long as Russia remains Europe's principal security concern."

China presents a different but equally important challenge. For India, China is a rival and source of major security issues, including an ongoing border dispute. While the EU also sees China as a "systemic rival," its members differ in how they balance security concerns with their economic relationships with China.

These differences complicate attempts to develop a shared assessment of Indo-Pacific threats.

On a geopolitical level, India wants partnerships that strengthen its capabilities without requiring it to align fully with another power's strategic priorities. European governments are also weighing their regional ambitions against security demands closer to home.

The new India-EU partnership creates a framework for closer engagement, but it does not amount to a joint security commitment.

Ashraf said the two sides could strengthen their mutual capabilities but were unlikely to exercise a visible influence on the global security order.

For now, maritime domain awareness, information-sharing, defense technology and selective industrial cooperation offer more realistic prospects than a common security role of the EU and India across the Indo-Pacific.

#132. Human trafficking victims stranded in Guinea-Bissau

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dozens of West Africans lured to Guinea-Bissau with promises of jobs and migration to Europe say they were exploited by a suspected trafficking network. Now many cannot afford to return home.

This article contains descriptions of rape, exploitation and sexual assault. Reader discretion is advised.

Sadio Konimba had hoped for a better future. The 26-year-old had lost her husband and was left alone with her child in Bamako, the capital of Mali.

When an acquaintance told her about the possibility of reaching Europe via the West African nation of Guinea-Bissau, she decided to make the journey. She left her 6-year-old daughter behind with relatives for the time being.

Konimba says she was invited to the country's capital, Bissau, with the promise of work and better chances of eventually continuing on to Europe.

She was told the journey from Bamako to Bissau would cost between 100,000–125,000 CFA francs (roughly €150-€190, or $170-$210), which she paid. She had only intended to stay temporarily in Guinea-Bissau, but when she wanted to return to Mali, her money was not refunded.

"I had to pay a large sum of money," Sadio told DW, adding that instead of the prospects she had been promised, she was confronted with a very different reality.

According to Konimba's account, men from Senegal offered to repay the money she had paid if she "worked" for them. When she realized what that meant, she refused and demanded her money back. To this day, she has received nothing.

Jane, a 21-year-old woman from Sierra Leone, recounted how she was subjected to an attempted rape and other abuses at the hands of a criminal network. She told French news agency Agence France-Presse they even tried to force her into prostitution.

Sadio and Jane are among dozens of victims of a suspected human trafficking ring that was dismantled by police following an operation in Guinea-Bissau on October 21. The intervention followed complaints from victims who had reported abductions and fraud.

In a written statement to DW, the criminal investigation police said that more than five suspects had been arrested.

According to police, the network used a front company to lure people with promises of jobs and assistance with migration to Europe.

Around 70 people are believed to have fallen victim to the scheme. Most came from abroad, including Guinea, Mali, Cote d'Ivoire and Senegal. The suspected perpetrators allegedly charged victims between 400,000 and 700,000 CFA francs for each journey to Guinea-Bissau.

According to preliminary findings, the victims were held against their will in Bissau, subjected to pressure and forced to take part in fraudulent pyramid schemes. They were expected to recruit additional participants into the schemes through social media.

To make their operations appear legitimate, those responsible allegedly used false contact details for supposed offices in Canada, the United States and Portugal. Information from Interpol, the international police agency, helped investigators uncover the network, according to police.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Issa, a 23-year-old from Guinea, says he paid a smuggler 10 million Guinean francs (around €1,000). When he refused to recruit more people, he was allegedly threatened with death.

For Bubacar Ture, head of the Guinean League of Human Rights, the case is an alarming sign.

"As a human rights organization, we are extremely concerned about this phenomenon, which has now reached deeply troubling proportions," he told DW. In May, authorities rescued 60 foreign nationals during a similar operation.

Ture sees a link between the human trafficking problem and Guinea-Bissau's weak institutions. Criminal networks exploit these conditions to continue their activities, he said, noting that women are particularly vulnerable to human rights abuses.

The small Portuguese-speaking West African country has been plagued by political instability since independence in 1975 and is also known as a transit hub for cocaine bound for Europe.

A week after their rescue, the victims face a new problem: many lack the funds needed to travel back to Mali, Sierra Leone or elsewhere in West Africa.

Sadio Konimba is temporarily staying with an acquaintance in Bissau. Her friend told DW that Konimba had been deeply traumatized by what she had experienced. She had never imagined she could fall into the hands of a human trafficking network that might force her into prostitution.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Konimba herself does not want to discuss in detail what kind of work she was asked to do. Police confirm that women in such cases are often sexually exploited.

Konimba says she has managed to borrow around 75,000 CFA francs from acquaintances in recent days. But that falls short of the cost of her journey back to Bamako.

According to available information, Guinea-Bissau's migration and border authority, the Direcao-Geral de Migracao e Fronteiras (DGMF), also lacks the funds needed to finance the victims' return journeys.

Contacts have been established with the diplomatic missions of the countries concerned. But progress appears slow, and no concrete arrangements for their return have yet been announced.

Bubacar Ture criticizes the lack of protection available to victims.

"There are no public shelters for cases like these," he told DW, noting that private facilities have limited capacity, and psychological support and funding for temporary accommodation are also lacking.

The human rights advocate commends the criminal investigation police for taking action against the networks despite limited resources. At the same time, he calls for greater support.

"We make an urgent appeal to the government and international partners to provide adequate support to the judiciary, the police and the other institutions," he told DW.

For Konimba, one thing matters above all else now: returning to Bamako.

"Now I definitely want to go back to my mother and my daughter," she told DW. "Every day she asks me, 'Mom, are you coming home?' I tell her, 'I will come, I will come. Just wait a little longer.'"

But Konimba still does not have enough money for the journey. She is free, but for her, the struggle to rebuild a life with dignity has only just begun.

#100. India increases growth forecast for fiscal year

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

India's Ministry of Finance has increased its growth forecasts for the current fiscal year. But it also warned about inflation, particularly for fuel and energy, amid continued instability in the Middle East.

India's Ministry of Finance expects India's economy to grow at a pace of 7.3% in the current fiscal year based on its latest assessment of current data, it said in its October economic review.

The estimate is a slight increase on the previous forecast of 7.1%.

India's current fiscal year, known as FY27, runs from April 1, 2026 to March 31, 2027.

The report contrasted these figures with global growth predictions of nearer 3%, saying "India's growth momentum, by comparison, has held firm."

The ministry said growth in various key indicators like energy consumption, port traffic, goods and services tax (GST) collections, and car sales had accelerated in the previous quarter.

However, the ministry also warned of continued inflationary pressure and pressure on the rupee currency, as well as downside risks from the El Nino weather phonemenon that could threaten soil moisture ahead of the winter sowing season.

It said that external factors like oil prices and "global uncertainties" — seemingly a reference at least in part to the instablity in the Middle East — continued to pressure the rupee's value and pose inflationary risks.

Consumer price inflation (CPI), the wholesale price index (WPI), food cost prices and fuel and power prices were all rising month for month, albeit at very different rates.

The CPI was up 5.54% year-on-year in September, but the WPI had risen more sharply at 11.39%. Food index inflation was up by 8% in rural areas, and a slightly more modest 7.18% in urban centers.

But, as in many other countries, fuel and power costs increases were in an entirely different league, at 25.85% inflation in September 2026 comapred to the same month a year earlier, prior to the outbreak of fighting in the Middle East.

Although it has been a comparatively slow decline, the rupee has been consistently losing value against the US dollar over the past five years, threatening the country's relative purchasing power internationally. This slide has picked up pace in the last 15 months or so.

But the ministry pointed to foreign exchange reserves worth some $721 billion and said that India was "well-positioned" to weather such risks and sustain its expansionary trajectory..

#91. Israel election: Authorities dispute Netanyahu’s fraud claims

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Benjamin Netanyahu's Likud claims large-scale foreign involvement in the elections led by Qatar and Turkey, with Israel’s authorities so far refuting the claims.

Israel woke up on Thursday to allegations by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's Likud party that foreign actors may have sought to influence voter mobilization efforts in Arab communities during this week's legislative election.

No'am Sohlberg, the Supreme Court judge who heads Israel's Central Election Committee, sent a letter to Israel's internal security agency Shin Bet to look into the Likud's claims.

According to Likud's statement, Arab parties Ra'am and the Joint List — which received a record share of the vote, mostly due to unprecedented turnout in communities of Arab and Palestinians with Israeli citizenship — allegedly used "severe criminal offenses" to spur voters to go to the polls, including bribery, threats and other forms of election fraud.

"Severe suspicions were brought to my attention about improper intervention in the elections by foreign funding by outside actors that act against the Israeli interest," Sohlberg wrote in the letter, adding that he was "unable to testify" as to whether the allegations are true.

"The elections in the State of Israel were stolen by Arab parties that support terrorism and the elimination of the Jewish state," Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's party claimed in a statement. His own coalition lost seats, leaving both the prime minister and his opponents short of a majority — unless they were to work with one or both of the Arab parties.

Likud and other right-wing figures pointed to the unusually high turnout in Arab communities, reports of organized transportation to polling stations and concerns over foreign-funded voter mobilization campaigns as grounds for requesting an investigation. They argue that a formal review is necessary to maintain public confidence in the electoral process, regardless of whether wrongdoing is ultimately found.

Israel's elections are administered by the Central Election Committee, a body comprising representatives of parliamentary parties and chaired by a Supreme Court justice. Large-scale election fraud has historically been rare, although disputes over campaign conduct and voting procedures regularly arise.

Israeli police said there is no evidence to support Likud's claims, neither "investigative” nor "informative.”

"Election day went with a minority of irregular incidents in the area of election integrity, also compared to other elections," it said in a statement.

A high-ranking Central Election Committee official, too, denied the allegations.

"We don't have any basis for claims of 'stolen elections,'" Dean Livne, the Committee's acting director general, told Israeli public broadcaster Kan 11, adding that the committee received "claims rather than evidence" from Likud and other parties.

Gadi Eisenkot, Netanyahu's main competitor and the head of the Yashar party, blamed the Israeli leader for leading an "improper influence operation" to cast doubt on the elections and create what he called "a fictive delegitimization" for the next parliament.

However, Eisenkot, also called for Likud's allegation of electoral fraud to be investigated.

The religious Muslim Ra'am party denied any involvement and said it would cooperate with any investigation.

Qatar released a statement denying any involvement in the Israeli elections, calling them "false propaganda aimed at dragging Qatar's name into Israel's political conflicts."

The controversy comes against the backdrop of growing polarization in Israeli politics and increasing public distrust between rival political camps.

Likud's motivation could be to decrease the Arab influence on Israeli politics, according to Amira Halperin, a senior fellow at the Palm Beach Center for Democracy and Policy Research. "The Arab votes were very, very important in this election," she said.

Such allegations could also hurt the public's trust in the election process Halperin argued: "We need to be cautious about predicting a failure of the democratic system."

Israeli media reports have suggested that some of the concerns cited by Likud were based on intelligence assessments examined by the Shin Bet, though the agency has not publicly confirmed the details of any such findings.

Public broadcaster Kan reported the suspicions' extent as "relatively small," citing sources familiar with the Shin Bet investigation. Israeli daily Ha'aretz, too, quoted sources familiar with the allegations as saying the reports are "being blown out of proportion.”

David Zini, the head of the agency, said on election day that the elections in Israel are "safe, sound and supervised," adding that the Elections Committee and Israeli police are "successfully" preventing anyone from hurting it.

Zini's appointment earlier this year was criticized by opposition figures, who questioned both his qualifications for the role and Netanyahu's motives in selecting him. Supporters of the appointment rejected those criticisms, arguing that Zini's military experience and security background made him suitable for the position.

Separately, the Shin Bet, has been involved in investigating several of Netanyahu's closest aides on allegations of undisclosed ties to Qatar. Those investigations are unrelated to the current election allegations.

Israeli outlet N12 has reported that in the weekend before the elections that Likud officials were discussing potential election-integrity complaints ahead of voting, although no evidence of actual irregularities had been presented at that stage, with a focus on Arab and Palestinian communities .

"They sat and brainstormed to find creative ideas of how to complain about this issue," said Daphna Liel, the reporter who broke the story.

Arab and Palestinian participation in this week's election was unprecedented, with turnout jumping from around 50% in 2022 to about 80% in 2026, according to reports in Israel.

Arab voter participation has fluctuated significantly in recent decades. Turnout has often been influenced by debates within Arab society over whether participation in national elections can effectively advance the interests of Arab citizens, who comprise roughly 20% of Israel's population. The exceptionally high turnout in this election was widely seen as a sign of renewed political mobilization.

This resulted in huge success forthe Joint List and Ra'am, with 11 and eight parliament seats, respectively, in comparison to six and four in the 2022 election. Both parties have already voiced their willingness to cooperate with Israel's Zionist opposition to replace Netanyahu.

Most parties in Israel's Zionist opposition promised not to join a coalition with Arab parties ahead of the elections, with the exception of left-wing Zionist party "The Democrats” (HaDemocratim).

On election day, there were also allegations over "systematic delays" in voting in Arab and Palestinian polling stations, according to Adalah, an Israeli-Palestinian NGO for the legal rights of the Arab minority in Israel.

#46. Justice for Maya women in Guatemala, girls back in school in India, restored land in Mali: The UN has named its 2026 SDG Action Award winners

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Champions of indigenous women's rights in Guatemala, girls' education in India, and restored farmland in Mali all featured among the winners of UN's SDG Action Awards.

A legal aid group for Indigenous women in Guatemala, a movement of village mothers in rural India, and a Malian entrepreneur who restores farmland have won this year's UN SDG Action Awards.

The winners were announced at the ninth Heroes of Tomorrow awards ceremony in Rome, Italy, run by the UN SDG Action Campaign. They were chosen from more than 7,000 entries from 190 countries.

Nine finalists were shortlisted for three categories: resilience, creativity and changemaker.

The Resilience Award went to the Women's Justice Initiative. Many indigenous women in Guatemala face high rates of violence, poverty and discrimination, made worse by language barriers and a lack of access to information. The initiative helps women and girls get access to justice and lead change in their communities. According to the organization, more than 16,000 women and girls have participated.

"I accept the UN SDG Action Award in the honour of the Maya Kaqchikel women and girls in Guatemala who are building a more just future for their communities. Ending violence against women requires all of us. Only together, we can build a future where every woman and girl lives with dignity and realizes her full potential," said Viviana Patal, the initiative's legal director.

The Creativity Award was given to Mothers of Courage, a grassroots campaign that aims to keep girls in education in rural India. Its organizers say more than 1.2 million people have joined and pledged to keep their daughters in education and to prevent early marriage.

"This award today belongs to all the mothers in those villages, all the women leaders, who are working hard to change the destinies of their daughters and their daughters-in-law," said Khushboo Awasthi, Mothers of Courage's chief curator.

"Together, let's create a world where no girl ever has to negotiate for her right to dream and to aspire and to thrive."

The Changemaker Award when to Malian entrepreneur Rokiatou Traore. Her organization, Herou Alliance, helps women and small farmers bring worn-out land back to life by planting trees alongside their crops. The trees also help farmers generate extra revenue, allowing them to sell products from moringa and other valuable trees.

"Receiving this award means a lot to me. It is a reminder of why I started this journey," said Traore. "I invite you all to join us, to plant more trees, to empower more women, and above all, to create a future full of opportunities for communities for today and for future generations."

The UN Trust Fund to End Violence against Women and Girls received an honorable mention. Speaking about the award, President of the Organization of Women's Freedom in Iraq Jannat Al Ghezi paid a tearful tribute to its founder Yanar Mohammed, who was assassinated outside her home in Baghdad in March.

UN Assistant Secretary-General Michelle Muschett said the finalists "remind us that the future is something we create together and that each of us has the power to help shape it and open doors for the next generations."

"When they tell you that one person cannot make a difference, prove them wrong," UN SDG Action Campaign Director Marina Ponti told the audience. "Change has always begun with people who refused to accept that the world could not be different. So be courageous. Be persistent. Be that change."

A panel of 20 judges from UN leadership, government, civil society, the arts, business and youth activism chose the winners. They included UN Development Programme Administrator Alexander De Croo, Oxfam International Executive Director Amitabh Behar, and Otto Group Vice President Tobias Wollermann.

The awards are run by the UN SDG Action Campaign, a special initiative of the UN Secretary-General. The campaign supports the UN Sustainable Development Goals, 17 targets adopted by member states that cover areas including poverty, hunger, education, gender equality and climate protection.

The awards are supported by Italy's Foreign Ministry and Germany's Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development.

Editor's Note: DW Director General Barbara Massing was a member of the 20-person jury panel.

#23. Germany's Alexander Zverev clinches tennis No. 1 ranking

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Alexander Zverev is the second German man, after Boris Becker, to clinch the No. 1 spot in tennis.

German tennis star Alexander Zverev is set to become the new world No. 1 in tennis, capping a successful year where he won two Grand Slams.

Zverev is the second German man, after Boris Becker, to clinch the top spot in the sport. Becker reached No.1 some 35 years ago.

Zverev replaces Italy's Jannik Sinner, who previously held the world's No. 1 spot.

Zverev got there by reaching the quarter-finals of the Erste Bank Open in Vienna this week.

He is also the second-oldest first-time No. 1 ranked player after Australian John Newcombe.

"(It is) very special. To have the feeling to be on the top of the world is amazing," Zverev said.

"It is different than winning a tournament or a title, but it's such a special feeling to know that the No. 1 is going to be next to your name when the new rankings come out."

Among German female tennis players, only Steffi Graf and Angelique Kerber have achieved this in the WTA rankings.

Zverev's career had been defined by getting close to Grand Slam titles but never quite reaching them, facing stiff competition from Sinner and Spanish talent Carlos Alcaraz.

The Hamburg-born also won a gold medal in the 2021 Olympics.

But his tennis career finally had its breakthrough this year, when he won the Grand Slam at both the French and US Opens.

Known for his consistency, he was just the ninth man to reach at least the semifinals at all four majors in the same season.
