#10. Israeli election: Netanyahu, main rival fall short of majority after all votes counted

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With all the votes counted, the pro-Netanyahu bloc finished ahead of Gadi Eisenkot's anti-Netanyahu bloc. But as neither grouping won a majority, tough coalition talks are ahead. Follow DW.

Follow below for all the latest on the Israeli election.

Ahead of the election, two clear coalitions had formed. On the one side, the ruling coalition of right-wing, far-right and religious parties centered around Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his Likud party.

On the other side was the anti-Netanyahu bloc, made up of a coalition of right-wing, centrist and liberal parties whose unifying principle was to oust Netanyahu from power.

However, neither of these blocs was able to secure a majority of seats, meaning a simple path to forming a government is off the table.

The task of figuring out a new working coalition is now beginning, with the question of whether the two Arab-Israeli lists, which together secured an unprecedented 19 seats, may form part of a future government or at least provide some form of support.

Yair Golan, chairman of the left-liberal Democrats, said he had met with opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot, former Prime Minister Yair Lapid and Mansur Abbas, head of the Arab Ra'am party.

He has called for discussions with the Arab-led parties, saying after his meeting with Eisenkot that "the rescue of Israel is at stake," in a post on X.

It remains to be seen if the right-wing factions of Eisenkot's anti-Netanyahu bloc would ultimately entertain the possibility of working with the Arab-led parties.

Another option that has been aired is the possibility of a unity government including both Netanyahu and Eisenkot, but such a move may be similarly distasteful for moderate voters.

The final count of ballots from Tuesday's vote has been released by Israel's Central Election Committee.

Israel's Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's Likud claims large-scale foreign involvement in the elections led by Qatar and Turkey, with Israel’s authorities so far refuting the claims.

Israeli police said there is no evidence to support Likud's claims, neither "investigative” nor "informative.”

A high-ranking Central Election Committee official, too, denied the allegations.

Read more in depth coverage of that story, right here.

With the final tally of votes in Israel's high-stakes parliamentary elections expected on Thursday evening, we take a look at today's events thus far.

With 89.8% of ballots counted, the bloc led by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is set to win 53 seats in the 120-seat Knesset, compared with 48 seats for the anti-Netanyahu alliance led by former military commander Gadi Eisenkot, according to the Central Election Committee (CEC) as reported by the Times of Israel.

That leaves both blocs far short of the 61 seats needed for a majority.

The two majority Arab parties seem set to win 19 seats between them, a record tally that beats their previous high of 15 seats.

Netanyahu has ruled out working with the Arab parties to gain a majority, but there have been signs that some parties in Eisenkot's bloc might be open to the idea, though Eisenkot himself has also previously rejected it.

The absentee ballots — "double-envelope" votes — whose count began on Thursday are not expected to materially affect the final results, despite making up almost 10% of the overall vote.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Central Elections Commission (CEC) has said that counting of so-called double envelope ballots began early Thursday morning, according to the Times of Israel.

Such ballots are cast mostly by soldiers, prisoners and diplomats stationed abroad, as well as others unable to vote at their assigned polling stations.

According to Israeli media, the CEC is reckoning with between 430,000 and 480,000 such ballots, lower than its previous prediction of some 600,000 but still representing just under 10% of votes cast.

This could theoretically still impact seat distribution to a considerable extent.

The Times of Israel also cited the CEC as saying that not all regular votes have been completely counted.

A full vote count is tentatively expected by Thursday afternoon, although the official finalized results will not be issued until November 4.

The United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights, Volker Türk, told reporters in New York that he hopes a new Israeli government will "change course massively" and respect international law.

"The message is: don't continue the current course of warfare where you think you are above international law, because I think that's what we have frankly seen in the current environment," Türk said, as relayed by the AFP news agency.

"Israel has its obligations under international law, international humanitarian law, international human rights law, and they need to respect them," he said. "And I hope that the next government will do precisely that and change course massively."

Türk also said in a statement on Monday that Israel's operation in recent days in the occupied West Bank city of Hebron raised serious concerns about "unnecessary and disproportionate" use of force.

He said that more than 2,000 people had been displaced, with homes and commercial property destroyed and free movement impeded by the lockdown.

"Collective punishment is a grave violation of international law," Türk said. "Threats to empty and erase entire neighborhoods also raise serious concerns of ethnic cleansing."

Omid Nouripour, one of the deputy speakers in Germany's Bundestag parliament, has told DW that the high turnout in Israel was a testament to the strength of the country's democracy.

"For now, of course, we do not know what the coalition-building will look like, but the people have put their trust in democracy," Nouripour said. "Above all, Arab Israelis have trusted democracy and gone to the polls in droves."

Speaking to DW's Rosalia Romaniec, as he accompanies German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier on his state visit to China, Nouripour also said that Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's and other right-wing parties' religiously-charged campaign strategy appeared to have backfired.

"This big campaign from Netanyahu's camp, first and foremost against the Arab Israelis, has on the one hand mobilized people to such an extent that there is no longer a majority for this camp," he said.

Nouripour, a former Green party co-leader, has been a Bundestag member for 20 years. Born in Tehran, his family moved from Iran to Germany when he was a teenager.

Israel's election has produced no clear winner, leaving both Benjamin Netanyahu and opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot short of a path to power. Israel could now be facing weeks of political uncertainty.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A new Israeli government is unlikely to bring big changes on controversial topics like Palestinian statehood and Israeli troops in Lebanon and Syria, experts say.

Some small positives may be possible though.

Read DW's full report on what might change for Arab countries after Israel's election.

The Central Elections Commission (CEC) estimates that there are still between 430,000 and 480,000 so-called "double envelope" ballots to count, according to Israeli media.

These are cast primarily by soldiers, prisoners and diplomats stationed abroad, as well as others unable to vote at their designated polling stations.

This estimate is lower than the CEC's previous prediction of around 600,000 but it remains subject to change.

Such ballots are still being received and processed, and officials also have to double-check that the individuals sending them in have not also voted at a physical polling station on election day.

Because of these complications, they tend to be among the last votes to be counted.

A full vote count is tentatively expected by tomorrow afternoon, although the official finalized results will only follow on November 4.

If the CEC's estimated range is roughly correct, this group of ballots would equate to not very far shy of 10% of the total number of votes cast. As such, they have at least the theoretical potential to impact the seat distribution quite considerably.

President Isaac Herzog, who will have a key role in choosing someone to try to put together the next government, has urged all parties to "come with an open mind" when the time for negotiations comes.

"I will receive  the official election results next week," Herzog said. "I wil then begin the process of the consultations here, hearing from each and every fraction, asking questions, and holding discussions."

Typically the president chooses the leader of the party with the most votes to try to form a government although he is not required to follow this convention. That nominee then has 42 days to try to set up a working coalition.

"One thing must be clear, and I said this before the elections too," Herzog said. "We must do everything we can, certainly after yesterday, to avoid another round of elections. So I expect everyone who comes here to come with an open mind."

Between April 2019 and November 2022, Israel had five general elections in rather rapid succession amid similar difficulties either forming or maintaining coalition governments.

Partial results published by the Central Elections Committee and reported by the Times of Israel newspaper showed the bloc led by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu on 54 seats, with 84.5% of votes counted.

The opposition bloc under Gadi Eisenkot of the centrist Yashar party was on 49 seats.

Neither bloc is set to receive the 61 seats need for a majority in the 120-seat Knesset, making some form of coalition likely.

The Arab-led parties — the Joint List and the United Arab List, commonly known under its Hebrew name of Ra'am — have dropped to 17 seats.

The numbers are still likely to change, particularly after a record number of absentee votes are counted.

The full tally of those will possibly not be confirmed until Friday, according to the newspaper.

The leader of the far-left Joint List, one of Israel's Arab-majority lists, has called for a "genuine Jewish-Arab partnership" to help remove Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

"Anyone who wants to offer a substantive and genuine alternative to Netanyahu's blood-soaked government will have to talk to us to secure the path to the change that the public in Israel longs for," Yousef Jabareen told Israeli media.

"We will know how to use our political strength to advance the values we believe in," Jabareen said.

"We are ready to pave the way for the replacement of the government of Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir and [Finance Minister Bezalel] Smotrich — and send them home."

After most votes have been counted from Tuesday's election, the conservative Islamist Ra'am party and the Joint List are projected to win a combined 19 seats in the 120-member Knesset.

As neither the pro-Netanyahu bloc nor the opposition bloc led by former military chief Gadi Eisenkot seem likely to win a majority, the Arab parties could play a key role in the formation of the next government.

Right-wing Jewish parties in Israel have traditionally refused to cooperate with Arab parties, and Netanyahu said on Wednesday doing so would be "treasonous."

However, at least one opposition party is open to the idea, with the leader of the left-leading Democrats, Yair Golan, saying Wednesday he supported bringing Arab-led parties into a coalition.

#397. World’s largest crypto exchange investigating reports of potential hack

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Binance said the extent of damages was not known and had not confirmed whether funds had been pilfered

Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange, said on Thursday it is investigating reports of a potential security issue as its founder said the incident “does look like a hack on Binance”.

It wasn’t immediately clear the extent of the damage hackers might have been able to accomplish and Binance had not explicitly confirmed that funds had been pilfered, but stablecoin issuers quickly froze funds Thursday evening.

“Rest assured that user funds are completely safe,” a Binance spokesperson said in a statement.

“Our security teams are reviewing all relevant information with urgency, and we will provide updates through official Binance channels as soon as we have verified information,” the statement continued.

Stablecoin providers Tether and Circle each said they froze funds Thursday in response to the incident.

Jeremy Allaire, co-founder and CEO of Circle, wrote on X that the company had frozen more than an equivalent of $800m in currency associated with the incident “in coordination with law enforcement”. Paolo Ardoino, the CEO of Tether, said on X that the company froze the equivalent of more than $1.5bn in currency related to “a potential Binance exchange hack”.

The price of bitcoin dropped 3.2% Thursday afternoon to under $79,000.

#616. Free hearing bus helping 2,000 kids faces closure as funding ends

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Eli Wright (middle), Ericka Wright (left) and Jody Wright have benefited from the service.  (ABC Western Plains: Jen McCutcheon)

More than 2,000 children receive free hearing tests in western NSW each year through the Hear Our Heart Ear Bus Project.

A test helped diagnose Nyngan teenager Eli Wright with a cyst in his ear that was millimetres from his brain.

The mobile children's ear clinic is facing closure if $400,000 in funding is not secured by the middle of next year.

Jody Wright knows just how lucky he is to have a healthy teenager at home.

His son Eli had chronic middle ear problems as a child, and Mr Wright said a chance screening by a mobile hearing service in Dubbo, in western NSW, saved the boy's life.

Eli Wright has had three surgeries to remove a growth from his middle ear. (ABC Western Plains: Jen McCutcheon)

Eli was diagnosed with a cholesteatoma, a cyst that grows behind the eardrum in the middle ear, which can be fatal if left untreated.

"With his third operation … they cleaned the growth out, but it was millimetres from his brain," Mr Wright said.

"We thought we were going to lose him at times, but he's pulled through.

But the free service is at risk of closing.

The charity behind it has lost its main sponsor and needs to find up to $400,000 a year to keep running.

The Hear Our Heart Ear Bus Project offers free hearing tests in the western region. (ABC Western Plains: Jen McCutcheon)

The Hear Our Heart Ear Bus Project began in late 2011 as part of the Dubbo District Deaf Club.

The makeshift mobile children's ear clinic travels about 9,000 kilometres each year to more than 40 stops in Dubbo and western NSW, providing free hearing tests to pre-school and school-aged children.

Project director Donna Rees said in the past five years, "over 10,000 tests have been conducted, and half of those have been Aboriginal children within the region".

The tests have led to 310 children being referred to ear, nose and throat specialists, and 51 being fitted with a hearing device.

Previously, the allied health service had significant philanthropic backing through bequests.

The Hear Our Heart Ear Bus Project makes 40 stops a year around western NSW. (Supplied)

The Hall Foundation has been the principal sponsor since 2018, but made its last contribution in July.

The project is looking for new supporters to cover running costs of up to $400,000 a year or it risks closing by the middle of next year.

"The funding is desperately needed to continue funding the staff," Ms Rees said.

"We've literally got 10 fingers in 10 pies or more."

The Western NSW Local Health District declined to comment on the service.

Without the service, many families would have to travel to Dubbo for private screening.

"We'd never have been able to afford help, there's no way," Mr Wright said.

The Hear Our Heart Ear Bus Project only has funding secured until mid-next year. (ABC Western Plains: Jen McCutcheon)

The aim of the bus is to reduce rates of middle ear infection, or otitis media, and hearing loss among Aboriginal and non-Aboriginal children.

The condition is a major cause of long-term ear and hearing problems in Aboriginal children, with about 8,800 children affected in 2022-23, according to the Australian Institute of Health and Welfare.

University of Newcastle professor and Worimi man Kelvin Kong is one of Australia's leading ear, nose and throat specialists.

He has spent his career improving access to culturally safe health care for Indigenous communities and said services like the ear bus were vital to help close the gap.

"It is so crucial that our children are hearing because if they're not hearing, they aren't learning.

"Hear Our Hearts do such an invaluable service to the community and they've been doing it for such a long time … they are incredible."

Kelvin Kong has a particular interest in treating ear diseases in Indigenous children.  (Supplied)

In the case of the Wright family, it was not just Eli who received help.

"Sebby, my 7-year-old, he's got grommets through the bus and not only them two but my wife as well; she's got grommets and hearing aids from them," Mr Wright said.

"We probably would never have gotten it without the bus, we are very grateful."

Mr Wright said since the screenings, both Eli and Sebby were making inroads at school.

"They were having trouble in school, but now they are actually learning and they're coming home happy that they've gone to school so we're getting on with life now," he said.

Ms Rees said without the bus, there would be a lot of children sitting in school with hidden hearing loss undetected and undiagnosed and not meeting their potential with educational outcomes.

#51. Counterfeit beauty products found to contain deadly chemicals after major raid

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Perfumes, skincare and cosmetics seized by Trading Standards have been found to contain potentially deadly chemicals

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Counterfeit beauty products have been found to contain a plethora of deadly chemicals following testing conducted by Trading Standards of goods seized.

Perfumes, skincare and cosmetics have become among Trading Standards’ most commonly seized counterfeits, with almost half of all goods found to contain harmful substances.

One skincare product was found to contain 13,200 parts per million of mercury, significant levels of lead in lipstick, eyeshadow and face products.

Nicotine products were found in perfume and fake fragrances as well, which were comprised of around 80% methanol.

According to the latest Intellectual Property Office (IPO) Counterfeit Goods Tracker, an estimated 20% of 18-24-year-olds who bought beauty products in 2025 bought them knowingly, a huge increase from 6% in 2023.

The Police Intellectual Property Crime Unit and Greater Manchester Police seized 1,666 counterfeit hair products in July from a warehouse in Manchester, including hair extensions, shampoo and conditioners.

It followed a raid in Bolton, in which Trading Standards officers seized £2 million worth of counterfeit goods, including perfume, cosmetics, clothing and vapes at a production facility in a commercial property.

The IPO’s Face the Fakes campaign is working with content creators and the beauty industry to raise awareness of the potential harms of counterfeit beauty products.

Independent toxicologist Rani Ghosh said: “When you hear that mercury has been found in face cream, lead in make-up or nicotine in perfume, it raises serious questions about what is going into these products and how they are being made.

“Some reactions to unsafe cosmetics may be visible, such as irritation, swelling or changes to the skin. But what concerns me as a toxicologist is what you might not see. Substances such as mercury and lead can affect the body internally, and those effects may not be immediately obvious.

“Counterfeit beauty products can look incredibly convincing. You cannot tell from the packaging what chemicals are inside, how the product has been made, or what risk it might pose. That is why knowing where your beauty products come from matters.”

Helen Barnham, head of enforcement engagement at the IPO, said: “These findings show why counterfeit beauty products can pose such serious risks to consumers.

“Products can look remarkably convincing, but behind the packaging they can contain dangerous substances that have no place in the cosmetics people use every day, and can be made in appalling, unhygienic conditions.

“Counterfeiters exploit the names and reputations of brands that consumers know and trust, while bypassing the rigorous safety standards that legitimate businesses work hard to meet.

“With our latest research showing a sharp rise in counterfeit beauty buying among young adults, we want to make sure people understand the potential risks and know how to protect themselves. Our Face the Fakes campaign is giving consumers practical advice to shop more safely, including checking who is actually selling a product and choosing trusted sources.”

Dr Emma Meredith, director-general of the Cosmetic, Toiletry and Perfumery Association (CTPA), said: “There is a rigorous safety framework behind every legitimate cosmetic product sold in the UK.

“Ingredients are carefully controlled, products have to be made in clean and hygienic conditions, and they must undergo a safety assessment. Companies have clear legal responsibilities for the products they place on the market.

“A huge amount of work happens behind the scenes to make sure people can use their cosmetics with confidence. Counterfeit products can bypass those safeguards, which is why knowing who you are buying from and choosing trusted sources really matters.”

Foreign Secretary Vikram Misri. File | Photo Credit: PTI

In a visit to fine-tune the details of Nepal Prime Minister Balendra Shah’s planned trip to India, Foreign Secretary Vikram Misri is expected to reach Kathmandu on November 4, along with newly designated Indian ambassador Sudhakar Dalela, a Nepali diplomatic source has told The Hindu.

Mr. Shah is expected to visit India by the end of the year, even as his seven-month-old government continues to deal with the aftermath of the devastating floods in the Bhote Koshi River while also planning a slew of administrative and political reforms.

If it materialises, this will be the first bilateral visit abroad by Mr. Shah, who rose to prominence during the September 2025 Gen-Z uprising in Nepal that overthrew the government of former Prime Minister K.P. Sharma Oli.

The Hindu had earlier reported that Mr. Shah, who belongs to the Rastriya Swatantra Party (RSP) led by former media personality Rabi Lamichhane, had vowed to focus on domestic priorities while deprioritising foreign interlocutors. In fact, Mr. Misri was earlier scheduled to travel to Kathmandu to meet Prime Minister Shah in May but the visit was postponed, ostensibly because of the new leader’s resolve to focus on domestic challenges.

Subsequently, RSP chairman Mr. Lamichhane was hosted in New Delhi and met Prime Minister Narendra Modi on June 3, after which Nepal’s Foreign Minister Sisir Khanal visited Delhi on June 5, in the first Minister-level representation to India from the new government, and met his Indian counterpart S. Jaishankar. This was followed by the visits of Nepal’s Finance Minister Swarnim Wagle in September and Home Minister Sudan Gurung, who participated in the first BIMSTEC (Bay of Bengal Initiative for Multi-Sectoral Technical and Economic Cooperation) Home Ministers’ meeting held on October 13. Mr. Gurung also met Mr. Jaishankar before he returned to Kathmandu. Mr. Wagle paid a repeat visit to New Delhi to participate in a private media conclave on October 23, when he also had a courtesy meeting with the Prime Minister.

Despite the flurry of visits, the Embassy of Nepal here has been left without an Ambassador for some months since the previous Ambassador, Shankar Prasad Sharma, returned home. Diplomatic sources said that the appointment of a new Nepali Ambassador to India will feature in the talks with Mr. Misri.

Nepal now has a complex new process to appoint envoys, as the new government announced that top ambassadorial posts would be selected through a public application process. In all, around 4,400 candidates had applied for the vacant ambassadorial positions. Nepali diplomatic sources told The Hindu that Kathmandu is awaiting the agrément (diplomatic permission) from India for Nischal Nath Pandey’s arrival in the Nepali Embassy in Delhi. Of the top ambassadorial posts in 13 Nepali missions, three remain uncertain, while Mr. Pandey’s name, along with nine others, has received the necessary clearance in Kathmandu.

Mr. Misri’s upcoming visit is also expected to review Nepal’s requirements for dealing with the aftermath of the disaster in the Bhote Koshi River, which affected hundreds of Indian families as well, as many individuals remain missing.

The Foreign Secretary is likely to meet Mr. Lamichhane, who earlier this week paid a visit to Kainchi Dham in Uttarakhand and the Kamakhya temple in Guwahati. The RSP chairman, who travelled to Uttarakhand by road from western Nepal, had described his visit to these pilgrimage centres as “personal and not political”.

“There are no political meetings planned. Since this is a personal visit, I urge people not to read politics into it,” he said, before starting his pilgrimage in his second visit to India in six months.

Mr. Misri’s conversations in Kathmandu may also include stakeholders from other political parties, though a final outlook on that is yet to be firmed up. It is understood that discussions may include a review of the Balendra Shah-led government, which came to power in March after the RSP’s sweeping electoral victory, defeating the Maoist parties and the Nepali Congress. Since coming to power, Mr. Shah has attempted several new initiatives, the latest of which includes an ordinance that will revise retirement age for government officials, bringing it down to 55 years from 58 years.

#62. Binance reports 'potential security issue' as stablecoin issuers freeze millions in funds

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange, said Thursday it’s investigating reports of a potential security issue as its founder said the incident “does look like a hack on Binance.”

It wasn’t immediately clear the extent of the damage hackers might have been able to accomplish and Binance had not explicitly confirmed that funds had been pilfered, but stablecoin issuers quickly froze funds Thursday evening.

“Rest assured that user funds are completely safe,” a Binance spokesperson said in a statement.

“Our security teams are reviewing all relevant information with urgency, and we will provide updates through official Binance channels as soon as we have verified information,” the statement continued.

Stablecoin providers Tether and Circle each said they froze funds Thursday in response to the incident.

Jeremy Allaire, co-founder and CEO of Circle, wrote on X that the company had frozen the equivalent of more than $800 million in currency associated with the incident “in coordination with law enforcement.” Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said on X that the company froze the equivalent of more than $1.5 billion in currency related to “a potential Binance exchange hack.”

The price of bitcoin dropped 3.2% Thursday evening to under $79,000.

Binance was founded in 2017 by Changpeng Zhao, who in 2023 pleaded guilty to a felony related to his failure to prevent money laundering on the platform and stepped down as CEO. President Donald Trump pardoned him in 2025.

Zhao posted on X on Thursday that “User funds are safe!” but added that the incident “does look like a hack on Binance.” He added that while he doesn’t manage Binance anymore, “as a shareholder, I expect all user funds will be fully covered.”

Zhao was released from prison last year after being sentenced for violating the Bank Secrecy Act. He was the first person ever sentenced to prison time for such violations of that law, which requires U.S. financial institutions to know who their customers are, to monitor transactions and to file reports of suspicious activity. Prosecutors said no one had ever violated the regulations to the extent Zhao did. Zhao and Binance have been key supporters of some of the Trump family’s crypto enterprises.

AP Technology Writer Barbara Ortutay contributed.

‘This is voter fraud at the highest level,’ president tells rally crowd in South Carolina; Alaska governor orders inquiry into Peltola campaign amid Republican outcry

Alaska governor orders investigation into Peltola’s Senate campaign

Speaking to a campaign audience in South Carolina, Donald Trump reiterated his belief that Mary Peltola should be criminally prosecuted and disqualified from the Alaska Senate contest.

“This is voter fraud at the highest level,” Trump said of the Peltola campaign’s alleged scheme to recruited a man who shared the name of her Republican opponent, Dan Sullivan, to run, all in a bid to peel away votes.

Trump added that Peltola “should be removed from the ballot and prosecuted, and so should the fake Dan Sullivan”.

“I think it’s a criminal act, what they did,” Trump said.

Donald Trump is so proud of the $400m luxury jet he received as a gift from the nation of Qatar, to use as Air Force One, that he frequently orders its pilots to show it off, by flying the Boeing 747-8 low over events he attends, including political rallies, Fourth of July celebrations and, on Thursday, the White House Halloween party.

The roar of a huge jet flying very low over the nation’s capital, and heading for the White House, as the president handed out candy to children on the South Lawn, was greeted with whoops of delight from some revelers who were perhaps aware of the planned flyover, but it alarmed many Washington DC residents who were not.

The fact that the jet is painted not in the traditional light blue, but in a dark blue and gold livery that resembles several commercial airlines, including the failed Trump Shuttle, might have added to fears from some residents who posted images and comments on social media indicating that they feared an air disaster or a terrorist attack was underway.

Tom Hoyle, who achieved instant viral fame on Thursday by comparing Donald Trump supporters to a cult in a live interview with Fox News before the president’s rally in South Carolina, told the Guardian that he was quickly removed from the venue by three Secret Service agents.

Hoyle, 57, explained in a phone interview that he was offended to have received an invitation to what he called the president’s “Nazi rally” in a text sent to his screen-printing business’s phone earlier this week.

He accepted the invitation, he said, with the intention of “screaming vulgarities” at Trump during his speech.

As he was waiting inside the relatively small venue in Rock Hill, South Carolina, however, he was approached by the Fox News correspondent Lucas Tomlinson.

Hoyle said he agreed, and then had just a few seconds to “come up with something not vulgar” to say on air and express his point that most Americans do not support Trump. (“It’s all a charade,” he told the Guardian later, calling Maga Republicans a minority of voters, but a very loud one.)

When Tomlinson then asked Hoyle “why are you here this morning?” he responded by comparing the assembled Trump supporters to members of a cult who committed mass suicide in 1978 by following their leader’s order to drink cyanide-laced fruit punch in Jonestown, Guyana (giving rise to the phrase that true believers “drink the Kool-Aid”).

“Well, the late 70s, I was a little too young to get involved and go to South America for the Jonestown massacre, but this is as close as I’ll ever get, so that’s why I’m here,” Hoyle said.

“Wait, the Jonestown massacre?” Tomlinson asked.

“That’s right,” Hoyle replied. “There’s nothing like seeing a cult right up front, man.”

“All right, so maybe this is not a Trump supporter,” Tomlinson told the studio before stepping away to find someone else to interview.

Before the camera moved, Hoyle took the opportunity to point to his cap and plug his business, MadPark Designs in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Hoyle said after he was ejected from the venue by the Secret Service, he spoke with a group of college student protesting against the president outside.

Speaking to the Guardian later, Hoyle pointed out that his business is in the same office park as a Customs and Border Protection office in Charlotte, adding that he has regularly told officers involved in Trump’s immigration crackdown what he thinks of them to their faces.

After his return from South Carolina, Donald Trump lashed out at a Reuters reporter who asked him why he just canceled a planned appearance at a rally in Kansas on Saturday in support of a embattled Republican senator, Dr Roger Marshall.

After the reporter inquired about Trump’s health, and Trump said he was well, the reporter asked: “So you not going to Kansas has nothing to do with you being sick of travel?”

With a look of disgust, Trump responded: “Oh, you’re sick to ask that question.

“You are a sick guy. Reuters is terrible,” the president added.

Then, stepping away to address other reporters, Trump said: “Can you imagine? Look at this dope. Look at this guy, he’s way out of shape.”

“He comes to me, ‘Sir, you’re not going to go to Kansas.’ I’m not going to Kansas because he’s doing very well. I’m going to do a tele-rally,” Trump continued, imitating the reporter and suggesting that Marshall no longer needs his help to win his Senate race.

“He said, ‘what’s the reason’, after doing what, 28? He says ‘what’s the reason for this, sir, that you’re not going to Kansas?’” the president continued, recapping the exchange for journalists who had just witnessed it for themselves.

“You know what the reason is? I don’t feel I have to go to Kansas,” Trump went on. “I’m going to Florida tomorrow, and I’ll do- if something else comes up – but he said, ‘You’re not going to Kansas, sir, are you feeling well?’ You’ve been following me for two weeks. I feel perfect,” the president said, with indignation.

“You are disgusting,” Trump said, turning back to the Reuters reporter. “Put yourself in shape – you look like hell.”

He then ended the discussion and walked away from the press corps.

The New York Times reports that the Trump administration has issued federal grand jury subpoenas to local election officials in California, opening a criminal investigation as part of a White House campaign to pre-emptively cast the results of the midterm elections in the nation’s largest state as fraudulent.

According to copies of the subpoenas obtained by the Times, the investigation is an effort to promote the unsubstantiated claim that thousands of noncitizens are on California’s voter rolls, and that elections in states run by Democrats are rigged against Republicans.

Hopes of averting a major new conflagration between Iran and the US soon after Tuesday’s midterm elections could rest on Iran’s response to a personal letter from the US vice-president, JD Vance, offering to restart diplomacy.

The letter, written after predominantly Qatari mediation, was sent to the secretary of Iran’s supreme national security council, Mohsen Rezaei, on Sunday. It requires Tehran to agree to make commitments on its nuclear programme at the start of a process that would lead to the opening of the strait of Hormuz within seven days in return for US sanctions relief.

A reply is imminent, but Iran is reluctant to give upfront and irreversible commitments on its nuclear programme, such as exporting its stockpile of highly enriched uranium to a third country, if the compensatory US steps, such as lifting sanctions on Iranian oil exports, are reversible.

Republicans escalated calls to investigate Mary Peltola’s alleged ballot-stacking scheme. Donald Trump, JD Vance and other Republicans echoed the president’s calls to criminally investigate Peltola for recruiting a man with the same name as her opponent to join the Alaska Senate race.

Alaska’s governor went one step further, ordering an investigation into Peltola. “Deliberately tricking voters into casting their ballots for someone else strikes at the integrity of our elections,” Mike Dunleavy, the governor, said in a post on X. “Anyone or any organization found to have broken the law must be held accountable.”

But it turned up no clear leader in Maine, once thought a likely Democratic pickup, or Kansas, a deep red state where the race has turned unexpectedly competitive.

According to the New York Times, the president ordered Bolsonaro, the defeated right-wing candidate for Brazil’s presidential election, to be allowed into the US on temporary status. A White House official also arranged the flight that brought Bolsonaro to Miami.

The president opted not to appear in-person at a rally in Kansas on Saturday, instead opting to focus on participating in remote tele-rallies. Other slated campaign appearances in Florida and Iowa are set to continue as planned.

President Trump has canceled one of his last scheduled campaign stops before the midterm elections next week, axing his plans to appear at a rally in Kansas.

Instead of appearing in Kansas, Trump will instead partake in a remote tele-rally for Marshall.

Trump’s other slated appearances through the weekend – including one in Florida and another in Iowa – are still expected to go ahead as planned.

Her campaign has energized New Mexico’s Indigenous communities. But while the historic nature of her potential victory isn’t lost on her, Haaland says the hectic final stretch of her campaign has pushed it to the back of her mind.

Trump, speaking to a crowd in South Carolina, mused about continuing his habit of unilaterally renaming bodies of water with the strait of Hormuz, the Hudson River and the Atlantic Ocean.

Flávio Bolsonaro, the rightwing candidate who clandestinely fled Brazil for Miami after losing the Brazilian presidential election, planned his escape with the blessing of the Trump administration, the New York Times reported on Thursday.

The paper, citing US officials familiar with the plan, said that Trump had ordered that Bolsonaro be welcomed into Florida on a temporary parole status, which will probably be extended to permanent residency. A White House official also arranged the private jet that shuttled Bolsonaro and his family to Miami, the Times reported.

Bolsonaro fled Brazil after his losing the election in a come-from-behind victory for the left-wing incumbent, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, who had also unseated Bolsonaro’s father, Jair Bolsonaro, in 2022. Jair Bolsonaro, once one of Trump’s strongest allies in Latin America, is now serving a 27-year sentence for allegedly trying to unseat Lula in a coup.

Flávio Bolsonaro picked up his father’s torch, running on a right-wing platform to unseat Lula. After his defeat, he was ordered to surrender his passports in connection with a criminal money laundering investigation.

Instead, Bolsonaro absconded across the border with Argentina alongside his family, flight from there to Ecuador and ultimately to Florida.

Trump did not outright endorse Flávio Bolsonaro in the race. But he has an increasingly embittered relationship with Lula, and has said that the results in the Brazilian election appeared rigged.

From his new home in Florida, Flávio Bolsonaro has declared himself the true winner of the election and vowed to contest the results.

As we reported earlier, Republican officials have called for an investigation into an alleged ballot-stacking scheme in Alaska’s competitive Senate race by Democrat Mary Peltola’s campaign.

For months, Donald Trump and others accused Peltola and Democrats of recruiting candidates with the same name as her Republican opponent, Dan Sullivan, to purposefully confuse voters when they cast their ballots in next week’s midterms.

Though Peltola’s campaign denied encouraging or soliciting a man named Dan J Sullivan to run, a recent New York Times report indicated Peltola’s campaign did recruit him.

While ballot stacking is not illegal itself, the US president and justice department have filed a lawsuit pre-emptively to block the certification of the Senate election results if the incumbent senator loses.

In this explainer, my colleague Ariana Baio takes you through what to know about the situation and ballot-stacking.

A US Senate race in one of the most Republican states in the country is now a dead heat, according to a new poll showing Democrat Annie Andrews tied with Darline Graham, the Republican incumbent senator.

The Palmetto State has not elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1998, and Donald Trump won it by nearly 18 points in 2024, but a New York Times/Siena poll this week has the two candidates neck and neck, at 48% each.

Graham was appointed to the seat after her brother, Lindsey Graham, died in July, and she entered the race at Trump’s urging, without having ever held elected office.

Her inexperience has become one of the defining aspects of the campaign, which culminated in an August debate when she admitted she was “not that informed on national security” when asked about US policy on Taiwan. In the poll, 51% of voters said she lacked the right experience to be an effective senator.

Andrews, a pediatrician from Charleston, has meanwhile built her campaign around her medical career. Her solution to better access to doctors and climbing medical bills has been to advocate for a public option and cheaper prescription drugs.

An Obama-era federal investigation of Minnesota representative Ilhan Omar found some evidence suggesting that she entered into a sham marriage in 2009 to help a man suspected of being her brother obtain a green card, according to the New York Times (paywall).

At issue is Omar’s 2009 marriage, when she was already a US citizen, to Ahmed Nur Said Elmi, a British citizen whom she divorced in 2017.

Current and former officials told the paper that the FBI and the Department of Homeland Security investigated the marriage in the summer of 2016, but the case stalled chiefly because prosecutors concluded that the suspected crime fell outside a five-year statute of limitations.

The Times notes that it remains unclear what evidence investigators had that suggested the marriage was fraudulent and that the person Omar married was her brother.

Marrying someone for the sole purpose of helping them obtain a green card is illegal under federal law, while marrying a sibling is illegal under state law.

Omar has denied the allegations. Spokesperson Jackie Rogers told the NYT in a statement:

double quotation markRep. Omar has repeatedly and unequivocally denied this baseless, disgusting conspiracy theory. This is a desperate political smear designed to distract from Trump’s failed presidency and two years of Republican failure in Congress.

Another aspect that remains unclear whether federal prosecutors could still pursue charges in spite of the statute of limitations for marriage-based immigration fraud.

The Trump administration has revisited the case as part of a wider investigation into Omar’s family’s immigration history to the United States.

Investigators are examining whether Omar’s late father lied or omitted information in immigration forms before becoming an American citizen, and whether Omar, who was a child when she immigrated to the United States, was too old to have qualified for citizenship through her father in 2000, the Times reported.

Trump’s homeland security secretary Markwayne Mullin said of the matter last month, in remarks directed at Omar:

double quotation markIf we can prove that you shouldn’t have been here in the first place, we’ll deport you.

Officials in the Trump administration lured automakers to expand manufacturing in the US by dangling a chance at tariff relief, according to new reporting from the Wall Street Journal.

The outlet, citing sources privy to details of the plan, reported that the Trump administration had promised Volkswagen executives that companies that invest in the US would soon benefit from a tariff relief program, before the administration unveiled a new $4bn Audi plant in Georgia. (Audi is owned by Volkswagen.)

The tariff relief program the Trump administration promised would come through the US Department of Commerce, the Journal reported. The plan, which is not yet finalized, would establish carve-outs for companies that commit to building factories in the US.

On his way to a campaign event in Texas, JD Vance backed an investigation into Mary Peltola’s alleged scheme to siphon away votes from her Senate opponent by a recruiting a candidate with the same name.

Addressing reporters on the tarmac, Vance said that Peltola had “tried to commit election fraud” in the Alaska Senate race by recruiting a man named Dan Sullivan to run against her primary opponent, Republican incumbent Dan Sullivan.

When asked whether he agreed with Trump’s insistence that Peltola be criminally prosecuted for the tactic (which, while underhanded, has been employed elsewhere, and is not clearly illegal) Vance backed the idea, though in not in quite so strong terms.

“I think that we should certainly look into whether she committed election fraud,” Vance said.

He continued: “She is trying to steal a Senate seat from the voters of Alaska, and it’s disgraceful. So whether it’s criminal or not, she certainly has committed, I think, a great moral failing.”

While Trump is campaigning in South Carolina, Vance is heading to Texas to try and win support for Ken Paxton. Paxton, a scandal-marred former Texas attorney general and Trump devotee, has trailed in the polls in recent weeks, putting the Republicans at risk of losing a Senate seat in Texas for the first time in decades.

#338. US bans Chinese labs from testing electronics ahead of Trump-Xi meeting

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The FCC measure targets testing facilities with alleged PLA ties, giving US labs until December 2028 to expand capacity

The United States on Thursday banned all Chinese laboratories from testing electronic devices destined for the US market, just weeks before US President Donald Trump is due to meet Chinese leader Xi Jinping on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen.

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) voted to bar testing laboratories and certification bodies in China and other economies that lack a “mutual recognition agreement” or reciprocal trade agreement with the US.

“This is consistent with President Trump’s broader effort to restore balance and fairness with respect to America’s dealings with foreign nations,” said FCC Chairman Brendan Carr.

#440. Trump cancels weekend rallies, plans to phone in to other midterm campaign events

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

US President Donald Trump has hosted 25 rallies in 19 states since Oct 1.

JOINT BASE ANDREWS, Maryland – President Donald Trump on Oct 29 cancelled planned weekend campaign stops in Kansas and Iowa, and his allies said he would instead phone in to rallies nationwide after four weeks of travel to boost Republicans’ election chances.

Trump told reporters he did not “feel like I have to go to Kansas”, a Midwestern state where Senator Roger Marshall, a fellow Republican, is in an unexpectedly competitive race with Democratic challenger Adam Hamilton.

“He is doing very well,” Trump said of Marshall. “We are going to have a strong Tuesday night.”

Later on Oct 29, the Republican National Committee said in a statement that it would also cancel a planned rally in Iowa, home to several competitive congressional races.

The cancelled in-person rallies were set for Salina, Kansas, on Oct 31, and Dubuque, Iowa, on Nov 1.

Since Oct 1, Trump has hosted 25 rallies in 19 states, often speaking for his party in rural areas of Republican-friendly states.

The Nov 3 midterm elections will determine whether Republicans retain narrow control of the US Senate and House of Representatives.

MAGA, a campaign funding group aligned with Trump, said on Oct 29 it would host a series of “tele-rallies” with the president in at least 19 states, including Kansas and Iowa, before Election Day.

The president is still expected to travel to rallies in several states before the Nov 3 election, including in Florida on Oct 30.

Trump has noted that his recent series of more than hour-long campaign speeches has been challenging. Sounding hoarse in Mississippi on Oct 26, Trump said: “God didn’t make your vocal cords to do a rally every day.”

On Oct 29, after campaigning for Senator Darline Graham in South Carolina, Trump ridiculed a Reuters reporter who asked how he felt after weeks of rallies and whether he was sick of travelling.

“You’ve been following me for two weeks,” Trump said before boarding his motorcade back to the White House. “I feel perfect.” REUTERS

#347. Manosphere and online misogyny should be treated as public health issues, say UK experts

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Report calls for an effort to tackle harms in similar way to obesity and smoking, and stresses need to ‘offer boys something better’

The manosphere and online misogyny should be treated by the government as a public health issue, in the same way as obesity or smoking, experts have said.

Writing in the Lancet, the authors of a new report argue that a coordinated response is needed, involving governments, schools, health services and community organisations, similar to the public-health response to tobacco use and ultra-processed food.

“Men’s problems are framed [in the manosphere] as the result of feminism rather than structural conditions, and masculine identity is commodified through products and paid content that normalise and reinforce health-harming and performance-oriented masculinities,” the authors write.

The report defines the manosphere as “a diffuse commercial ecosystem and loose collection of online communities united by shared beliefs about masculinity, misogyny and male victimisation”.

It goes on to outline how the manosphere has evolved into a highly lucrative online industry that makes money by promoting “the most extreme expressions of misogyny and aggrieved masculinity”.

Prof Kent Buse, a co-author of the paper and co-founder of the social justice thinktank Global 50/50, said: “This is a business model, not a fringe. Outrage and grievance are good for business, so the system rewards the most extreme content. Expect the playbook that served tobacco so well: blame the individual, and call regulation an attack on freedom. Public health has a tested toolkit for commercial harms and we should use it.”

He added: “Boys and men are not the problem. The problem is the conditions that leave them isolated and without a secure future, and the companies that profit from their anger and insecurity.”

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

The authors also point to growing disengagement from education among boys around the world, as well as loneliness, money worries and social exclusion, which can leave young men searching for a sense of belonging. These problems, they say, can make them more vulnerable to online communities that offer simple explanations for their difficulties and often encourage them to blame women and feminism.

Prof Sarah Hawkes, the report’s other author, said: “The manosphere offers boys and young men belonging, identity and an explanation for why life feels hard.

“Those needs are real. A response that only removes the manosphere, without offering something credible in its place, will fail. We need to offer boys something better, not just tell them what to avoid.”

Mark Brooks, the policy and communications director at the Centre for Policy Research on Men and Boys, welcomed the report’s position that it was a public health issue and that men, boys, women and girls were all victims of this content.

“Far too often, the manosphere discussion has positioned all men and boys as a problem and not taken into account the broader economic, educational and cultural challenges that drive their engagement,” he said.

“Not all men and boys engage with this content equally. We are currently working on research to identify who is most at risk.”

#89. Manosphere must be treated as a public health problem like smoking, academics warn

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Boys and young men have their insecurities cultivated in the manosphere and subsequently monetised, the study warns

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Academics have called for the so-called manosphere to be treated as a public health problem, with the harms it causes online confronted in the same way as those created by the tobacco industry.

The manosphere, according to the authors of a new report, is “a diffuse commercial ecosystem and loose collection of online communities united by shared beliefs about masculinity, misogyny and male victimisation”.

The movement centres on a so-called crisis of masculinity and a shared conviction that feminism has systematically disadvantaged men, they said.

It has contributed to many health harms, the authors argued. These include violence against women and girls and mental health issues, as well as “delegitimising” the idea of boys and men seeking help.

In this online environment, the authors said, outrage and grievance are “good for business”.

Boys and young men are especially having their insecurities cultivated in the manosphere and subsequently monetised, they said.

The report, published in The Lancet, stated: “Similar to the tobacco and ultra-processed food industries, content creators and hosting platforms deploy strategies that individualise responsibility.

“Men’s problems are framed as the result of feminism rather than structural conditions, and masculine identity is commodified through products and paid content that normalise and reinforce health-harming and performance-oriented masculinities.

“Efforts to regulate or moderate content are frequently framed as threats to men’s autonomy, restricting political space for intervention.”

Professor Sarah Hawkes, report co-author and co-founder of Global 50/50 social justice think tank, said: “The manosphere offers boys and young men belonging, identity and an explanation for why life feels hard.

“Those needs are real. A response that only removes the manosphere, without offering something credible in its place, will fail. We need to offer boys something better, not just tell them what to avoid.”

Fellow co-author Professor Kent Buse, affiliated with the Department of Global Population Health at Monash University Malaysia, argued that boys and men are not the problem, but rather the business model set up to target them.

He said: “This is a business model, not a fringe. Outrage and grievance are good for business, so the system rewards the most extreme content.

“Expect the playbook that served tobacco so well: blame the individual, and call regulation an attack on freedom. Public health has a tested toolkit for commercial harms and we should use it.

“Boys and men are not the problem. The problem is the conditions that leave them isolated and without a secure future, and the companies that profit from their anger and insecurity.”

The authors said factors such as educational marginalisation, social exclusion and isolation in boys and young men must be addressed in various ways.

These include boosting employment, skills to improve access to jobs, re-engaging boys in education and ensuring “equitable access to mental health and other frontline health services” designed to reach this group.

Tackling the manosphere, they argued, should involve a co-ordinated response by governments, schools, health services and community organisations, as for any other public health risk.

They suggested communications strategies should avoid “framing masculinity as inherently toxic or boys and men as inherently problematic”.

Regulation, they said, should focus on the online platforms rather than the users, similar to bans on tobacco advertisements directed at children, rather than criminalising smokers.

The authors argued the manosphere “is best understood and addressed as a public health problem” and that regulation should include “strengthening existing content moderation to restrict harmful environments and harnessing the power of algorithms to promote positive messaging and norms alongside regulation of the structural features that sustain harm”.

The report concluded: “The harms to boys and men, to women and girls, and to wider society, provide a clear basis for action.

“Reducing these harms requires addressing the societal and economic conditions, commercial structures, and social norms that sustain the appeal of the manosphere.

“This is a political, legal, economic, and social challenge demanding co-ordinated, sustained action on multiple fronts — a public health framing can unlock such an approach.”

Mark Brooks, from the Centre for Policy Research on Men and Boys charity, said: “We’re extremely pleased to see this report classifying this as a public health issue and making the really important point that men and boys are targets for online harms as well as women and girls.

“All too often, discussion around the manosphere represents men and boys as being the problem without taking into account the broader economic, education and cultural challenges that drive their engagement.”

Separate research, also published in The Lancet, considered how patriarchal expectations around masculinity are harming men’s health.

It argued that online narratives around masculinity and male grievance are filling a void left by an absence of support.

The author, Professor Jim Catto, Professor of Urology at the University of Sheffield, said: “These findings highlight why we need to look beyond individual behaviour and consider the wider environments that shape men’s attitudes and actions.

“Tackling the online influence of the manosphere should be part of a broader public health conversation about how we support men and address the social factors affecting their health.”

#357. Polymarket temporarily suspends trading on international platform due to security incident

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Security incident linked to performance-monitoring tool spurred platform to halt trading for about an hour

Polymarket temporarily halted trading on its international platform on Thursday because of a security incident that compromised a third-party provider, the company said.

Trading was paused for about an hour and resumed on Thursday afternoon. The company has promised to fully reimburse any affected traders.

The prediction market platform said it halted trading “out of an abundance of caution” after identifying and fixing the issue affecting its international platform.

“We’ll make this right and are fully reimbursing all affected traders,” the company posted on X once trading had resumed.

Polymarket said it referred the incident to a law enforcement agency in the United Kingdom.

Reports have linked the source of the incident to DebugBear, a UK-based company offering website performance-monitoring tools.

DebugBear’s own website was unavailable on Thursday afternoon and the company posted on X that it was “impacted by an ongoing security incident”. It also advised clients who were using one of its products to remove the embed code from their websites. The company did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

It was not immediately clear to what extent traders using the popular prediction market platform were affected, or the breadth of the impact. The company said anyone who was affected “will be made whole”.

#242. Netanyahu accused of smearing Arab voters to hold on to power

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Prime minister claims Arab Israeli parties used foreign backing to steal elections, in which his coalition lost its majority

Benjamin Netanyahu has been accused of using smear tactics to cling to power, after he claimed that Arab Israeli parties had used foreign backing to “steal” Tuesday’s election, in which the prime minister’s coalition lost its parliamentary majority.

Netanyahu and his allies called on the internal security service, Shin Bet, to investigate alleged vote buying and other irregularities in Palestinian communities in Israel, supposedly funded by foreign money.

Gadi Eisenkot, the leader of the main opposition bloc, accused the prime minister and his Likud party of trying to derail the election results.

“It’s already clear that Likud and Netanyahu have embarked on a coordinated, organised campaign seeking to delegitimise the results of the election,” Eisenkot said in a video message.

The prime minister’s allegations were quickly disowned by a range of government officials, including police officers and members of the election committee. Netanyahu was also rebuked by the president for “tarnishing an entire community”.

On Wednesday night, the prime minister posted a statement by Likud online, which described the claimed violations as “an attack on democracy whose purpose is to lead to a foreign takeover of the Knesset, the Islamisation of Israel, and the overthrow of rightwing rule”.

Tuesday’s elections led to Netanyahu’s far-right coalition losing its majority after four years in power. The main Jewish opposition bloc also fell short, however, leaving two Arab Israeli parties, the Joint List and Ra’am, holding the balance of power.

Final results released early on Friday local time showed the pro-Netanyahu bloc winning 53 seats in the 120-member Knesset and Eisenkot’s bloc taking 48 seats. Joint List and Ra’am won the remaining 19 seats, a historic high for the Arab Israeli community, which makes up about a fifth of Israel’s population.

The prime minister’s attack appeared aimed at stopping the Jewish and Arab opposition from voting together in the new Knesset to oust the incumbent speaker, Amir Ohana, a Likud politician and former Shin Bet official, and advancing their own legislation.

The Likud statement posted by Netanyahu on X said: “It is clear that no Zionist party should cooperate with Ra’am and the Joint List, replace the Knesset speaker through votes bought for cash, and form a government that is dangerous to Israel.”

Opposition leaders lambasted the claims of Netanyahu, 77, who has run Israel for 19 of the past 30 years.

Yousef Jabareen, the Joint List leader, accused the prime minister of incitement. “Netanyahu was defeated at the ballot box,” Jabareen said, adding that he “refuses to come to terms with the fact that the Arab public voted and decided”.

Eisenkot said any investigation should be conducted “without fear or favour,” and with “the utmost caution”, but insisted it could not be used as a reason to delay the convening of the newly elected Knesset, due to be sworn in within 14 days of the election.

The acting director general of the central elections committee, Dean Livneh, said there appeared to be no basis for the claims. Police officials quoted across the Israeli press said the allegations were groundless, adding that voting had been relatively trouble-free.

The president, Isaac Herzog, called for political leaders to “avoid tarnishing an entire community that sought to exercise its right to vote and take an active part in democracy”.

It was not specified in Likud statements, but press reports said Qatar was the foreign power alleged to have funded vote-buying and voter mobilisation. Majed al-Ansari, an adviser to Qatar’s prime minister, said its government “categorically” denied any involvement in the elections.

The record turnout among Arab Israelis (Palestinians with Israeli citizenship) appeared to be driven by outrage at routine discrimination and fear that it could become far worse if extremist and openly racist members of the government, such as the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, and the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, secured another term in office.

On Thursday, Ben-Gvir called for the arrest of Arab party leaders, and Smotrich demanded they be subjected to Shin Bet interrogation.

Voting in Nazareth, the biggest majority-Palestinian town in Israel, was brisk on Tuesday, with few signs of irregularities, other than apparent ploys by the government and coalition parties to suppress the Arab vote.

Some packets of ballots sent to Nazareth polling stations were in Hebrew alone, rather than in Hebrew and Arabic. Election workers said that monitors sent by far-right parties had attempted procedural ploys to slow down voting but had been largely unsuccessful.

The leaders of the Joint List and the Zionist “Change” bloc have ruled out a formal coalition, but officials from both have discussed the possibility of tactical voting in the Knesset to unseat Ohana, the speaker, and to pass some consensus legislation.

One proposed bill would establish a commission of inquiry into the security failures that helped lead to the Hamas attack on Israel on 7 October 2023, something Netanyahu has blocked over the past two years.

Ahmad Tibi, a party leader on the Joint List, said Netanyahu was trying to subvert the election results by trying to sabotage a common front between the Eisenkot bloc and the Arab Israeli parties.

“This entire campaign is directed against Eisenkot, to prevent him, intimidate and scare him off forming a government that relies on external support from the Joint List or Ra’am,” Tibi told the Reuters news agency.

Naftali Bennett, a former prime minister who is a partner in the “Change” bloc, pointed out the irony of Netanyahu denouncing Qatar for election meddling while the Israeli leader’s own political advisers are being accused of taking Qatari money for promoting the Gulf monarchy’s image.

“This morning, suddenly, when it serves his interests, Netanyahu remembered Qatar and is using it to sow chaos and undermine public confidence in the democratic process,” Bennett wrote on X.

#668. Youth theatre raises more than $68k to carry forward 50-year legacy

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shopfront Arts Co-Op hosted a youth conference, featuring a silent disco, to celebrate its 50th year. (Supplied: Shopfront/Lucy Parakhina)

Joining Shopfront Arts Co-Op changed Paul Capsis's life.

Fresh out of high school, the writer and performer — known for the 1998 movie Head On — was dragged by a friend to watch a show at the youth theatre in southern Sydney in 1983.

Seeing performers his own age on stage was "remarkable", he recalls.

"It was a magical experience," he says. "I was like, 'What have I been missing out on all these years?'"

After the show, Shopfront's co-founder, director Errol Bray, invited him to get involved in its upcoming production of A Midsummer Night's Dream.

The theatre became a refuge after Capsis's difficult school years.

"I was severely bullied," he says. "You start to think that is how the world is going to be outside."

At Shopfront, Capsis began writing and performing in his own shows, created in collaboration with other young people in the company.

In 1986, Paul Capsis played the mock turtle in Shopfront's adaptation of Alice in Wonderland. (Supplied: Shopfront)

"It was a major thing to be with people that liked me," he says. "We laughed and we performed and we hung out.

The youth theatre that shaped Capsis's life turned 50 this month.

Over the past five decades, it has kick-started the careers of artists including Olivier Award-nominated performer Trevor Ashley, former Sydney Theatre Company artistic director Andrew Upton, and playwright Hilary Bell.

It also continues to empower young people as performers, writers and decision-makers, and has also been acknowledged by the wider industry at the Sydney Theatre Awards.

One of its award-winning shows, The Lies We Were Told, created with Australian Theatre for Young People, was remounted at the Sydney Opera House this month in celebration of the company's 50th anniversary.

The Lies We Were Told won best production for young people at the Sydney Theatre Awards in 2024. (Supplied: Shopfront/Lucy Parakhina)

It followed a youth conference, featuring Heartbreak High star Thomas Weatherall and musician Dobby, and a 50-hour act-a-thon fundraiser, which aimed to raise $50,000 for the theatre. It raised more than $68,000.

The act-a-thon was a fitting tribute to the company's punk past, not only bringing together alumni with the current ensemble, but replicating its successful fundraiser of 1978.

That year, to avoid eviction, the young people of Shopfront resolved to buy the building and raised a $43,000 deposit through an act-a-thon, raffles and government support.

It is a history Shopfront's Young Leader, Liv Tsigaropoulos, who works as a conduit between the theatre's staff and young people, knows well.

When she was in Year 9, she made a mini documentary about the theatre for the National History Challenge, which won her the national Democracy Matters award.

By then, she had already been part of Shopfront for a couple of years, having started when she was 13.

Now 19, she's thrilled to be a custodian of the company during its anniversary year.

"It's the best time to be Young Leader," she says.

"It's such a full-circle thing. I remember reading about the act-a-thon when I was doing my research."

The performance of The Lies We Were Told is part of that full-circle feeling; Tsigaropoulos auditioned for that show when she first joined Shopfront in 2021.

"Since that, I never left, and they could never get rid of me," she says.

Over her six years with the theatre, she has participated in its drama workshops, made short films, learned about technical production, and joined one of its five advisory panels.

“I just do whatever I can; I just love being part of Shopfront,” Liv Tsigaropoulos says.  (Supplied: Shopfront/Joshua Morris)

For Tsigaropoulos, the special thing about being part of the theatre is the lengths it will go to bring a young person's ideas to life.

She recalls how that gave her the "confidence to stand by [her] ideas".

"In a space like Shopfront, you can say a crazy, big, unrealistic or impossible goal, and the Shopfront staff make it happen," she says.

"You're gonna see your words expressed on stage, what you think expressed on stage, and that's so special … People watch it and laugh and cry."

For example, she suggested she start a show stuck outside.

"It was so silly," she says. "I was like, 'I've missed the show.'

"It's small things I think are funny or cool and would want to see. Getting that put in a show is really fulfilling."

Now, it is up to her as Young Leader to help make those things happen for the next generation of young people.

"The only thing more beautiful than [your idea being brought to life] is working at Shopfront, and a young person tells you an idea, and you make it happen," Tsigaropoulos says.

The money from the fundraiser will help Shopfront keep bringing young people's ideas to life at a time when funding for arts companies can be precarious.

It is something Shopfront knows all too well, having lost multi-year funding from the federal government alongside many youth arts companies in 2016, and again in 2021.

Shopfront is currently receiving funding from Creative Australia and Create NSW.

More than 19,000 young people have taken part in Shopfront's programs since 2018. (Supplied: Shopfront/Lucy Parakhina)

The money raised will also help the theatre stay open to everyone, through scholarships and free tickets to performances.

"We never turn young people away if they can't afford it," Tsigaropoulos says.

"We'd rather lose money and let a young person come for free and get to experience Shopfront."

Capsis recalls his own fundraising efforts for Shopfront, standing on a box outside Hurstville Station, singing Janis Joplin.

"We're raising money, we're making shows, and that became my blueprint," he says.

"The big world of professional theatre and musical theatre didn't see me as somebody they wanted, so I made my own work."

It was his training ground, which saw him tour shows to the UK and Canada, and sing on stage for the first time.

"Some people go to NIDA [National Institute of Dramatic Art] and they do three years intensive," Capsis says. "I did Shopfront theatre one night a week and every weekend."

It also helped him make connections, including with Hillary Bell and Julian Meyrick, who collaborated on his award-winning one-man show about his Maltese grandmother, Angela's Kitchen.

For Capsis, what sticks out is how the theatre empowered him and the young people around him.

"I think it's extremely important young people have a place to voice how they feel, and to learn," he says.

Paul Capsis spent five years at Shopfront and went on to become a mainstay of Australian stage and screen. (Getty Images: Don Arnold)

That centring of young people may be what's behind the longevity of Shopfront Arts Co-op, Australia's longest-running and only youth-owned and led theatre company.

"[That longevity is] a testament to all the people who have ever come through Shopfront and the determination, the belief they have in amplifying young people's voices, and that has stayed at its centre since the very beginning," Tsigaropoulos says.

She adds empowering young people remains important, even if they don't want to pursue careers in the arts.

"We're not an acting school pumping out mini DiCaprios and Margot Robbies," Tsigaropoulos says.
