#686. Permit seeks higher antibiotic dosage for diseased Tasmania salmon

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tasmania's salmon farming industry has been beset with controversy in recent years. (Supplied: Bob Brown Foundation)

A federal regulator is assessing an application to allow farmed salmon in Tasmania to be treated with a higher dose rate of antibiotic florfenicol.

The salmon industry's permit was revoked after drug residue was detected in wild fish several kilometres away.

The Greens are calling for the permit application to be rejected.

An animal health company has applied to "increase the dose rate" of the controversial antibiotic florfenicol in salmon farms in Tasmania, the federal regulator has confirmed.

Florfenicol was first used in the state's south-eastern waters in November last year after an emergency permit was approved by the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines Authority (APVMA).

It was in response to a bacterial disease that led to the deaths of 15,000 tonnes of salmon in early 2025, and resulted in fish parts washing up on beaches.

Florfenicol was previously used in south-east Tasmania's salmon farms. (Supplied: EPA)

However in March this year, the emergency permit for florfenicol was revoked by the APVMA on the basis of "unacceptable risk of residue exposure to non-target species".

The revocation occurred after traces of the antibiotic were detected in wild fish species up to 10 kilometres from the nearest salmon pen in which the antibiotic had been used.

Florfenicol was later found in abalone up to 14km away.

At a Senate estimates hearing earlier this week, APVMA chief executive Scott Hansen outlined details of a renewed push by pharmaceutical company Abbey Animal Health, known as Abbey Labs, to use florfenicol in Tasmanian waters this summer.

"There's a second application, which is a minor use permit," Mr Hansen said in response to questioning from Greens senator Vanessa Bleyer.

"It's looking to move the location to not just the south-eastern biosecurity zone, but the eastern biosecurity zone as well.

"And to increase the dose rates and the treatment regime that's proposed.

"That obviously has a significant amount of work that is being done on that to look at environmental impacts, to look at any microbial resistance impact, as well as residues and depletion."

Scott Hansen says there is a second application for a minor use permit. (ABC News)

Mr Hansen said Salmon Tasmania had separately sought approval for another emergency use permit with the same dose rates and treatment frequency as its previously revoked permit.

The previous permit allowed for 10 to 20 milligrams per kilogram of fish per day for 10 consecutive days, with no re-treatment permitted.

No details were disclosed during the hearing about the degree to which Abbey Labs wants to increase its dose rate or treatment regime.

The APVMA said it could not comment on the new proposed dose rates because they were still under assessment.

The use of florfenicol last summer forced the closure of some rock lobster and abalone fisheries amid concerns any detections could trigger international export bans.

The Tasmanian Rock Lobster Council [TRLC], Abalone Council and Commercial Divers Association have previously warned of further fishery closures if florfenicol use resumes.

"Increasing dosage rates and treatment duration will increase both the likelihood and extent of environmental exposure," the groups said in the TRLC's newsletter in July.

Florfenicol was used in response to a bacterial disease affecting salmon. (Supplied: Bob Brown Foundation)

In June, it emerged that a total of 5.4 tonnes of the antibiotic had been used last summer, during which about 9,000 tonnes of salmon died.

Tasmania's Health Department has said there were no reports of adverse human health effects from eating seafood with florfenicol residues.

However, as a precaution it has said people can avoid seafood caught within 3km of a treated salmon pen.

In a statement, Senator Bleyer called on the APVMA to reject the florfenicol applications from Abbey Labs and Salmon Tasmania.

"We saw last time around that the antibiotic ended up in other animals such as rock lobster and abalone in our wild fisheries, jeopardising exports and threatening human health by increasing antibiotic resistance.

"Despite being labelled by the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines Authority (APVMA) as a 'minor use' permit, the risks are anything but that."

Earlier this month, the Department of Natural Resources and Environment (NRE) released a plan designed to reduce the risk of antibiotics entering wild fisheries.

The state's environment watchdog has released its insights from a mass salmon mortality event in which dead fish washed up on Tasmanian beaches.

The plan involves setting up multiple zones, in which rock lobster and abalone fisheries would be closed for at least six weeks if an antibiotic is administered.

Mr Hansen told the Senate hearing that the NRE's plan would form part of the APVMA's assessment of Salmon Tasmania's emergency use permit application.

Salmon Tasmania chief executive John Whittington said florfenicol was one part of a "much broader fish health strategy".

"It provides veterinarians with an important treatment option when additional support is required to protect fish welfare," he said.

"Its use is tightly regulated, clinically justified, and managed within a framework designed to protect animal welfare, food safety, environmental management, commercial fisheries and market access."

John Whittington says the antibiotic is one element of a broader strategy for fish health. (ABC News: Monty Jacka)

If a new florfenicol permit is issued, Mr Whittington said the number of fish needing treatment, according to veterinary advice, would dictate how much florfenicol is used.

"Our aim is to reduce the need for medication as much as possible through a range of strategies, including selective breeding, vaccination and other management practices."

The ABC contacted Abbey Labs for comment, but did not receive a response.

#150. Reeves urges EU to ‘act now’ and seize £175bn of Russian assets to protect Ukraine

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The former chancellor said Brussels must act now in her first major intervention since leaving government

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Rachel Reeves has urged the European Union to seize €210bn (£175bn) of frozen Russian assets to help Ukraine defend itself against Kremlin aggression, warning that failing to act now would risk the continent’s security.

In her first significant intervention since she left government earlier this year, the former chancellor told Brussels that “the time to act is now” as Kyiv faces “a desperate winter”. She warned Kyiv is confronting a funding blackhole as Donald Trump seeks to pressure the nation “to cut a deal with Moscow”.

The MP for Leeds West and Pudsey warned that Ukraine’s “capacity to sustain its defence will be gravely impaired” without the funding, which in turn would “undermine Europe’s security”.

It comes after Zelensky hailed an additional £10bn support package from the UK to Ukraine after discussions with Andy Burnham. The funding was announced in Wednesday’s Budget by Ms Reeves’s successor John Healey.

Ms Reeves, who was sacked as chancellor by Mr Burnham when he replaced Sir Keir Starmer as prime minister, wrote the rallying call in The Times alongside three fellow senior politicians - the former German defence minister Annegret Kramp-Karrenbauer, the former French minister for Europe Nathalie Loiseau and the former Canadian deputy prime minister and finance minister Chrystia Freeland.

The article said: “We are coming together to say we need to stand by Ukraine in its time of need.

“A critical next step should be for the EU to take custody of the 210 billion euros of sovereign Russian assets it immobilised in 2022 so it can use them to help Kyiv in its defence.”

The quartet pointed out the funding would be enough to bankroll Ukraine’s defence into 2029. Ms Reeves’ article said: “This eliminates the risk that elections in key EU member states next year may halt financial support for Kyiv.

“The assets would also fund Ukraine’s defence until after the next US presidential election, at which point Washington may be more supportive than it is today.”

Relations between the US and Ukraine have been strained after Mr Trump struck a diesel deal with Russia in the face of rising fuel prices and waived fresh sanctions against the Kremlin.

The US president also recently declined to blame Russia’s leader Vladimir Putin for starting the conflict and sought to portray Ukraine’s president Mr Zelensky as an obstacle to peace.

The public row was followed by disruption to Kyiv’s access to the vital Starlink satellite communications system, leaving the country exposed to attack.

In the article, Ms Reeves and her co-authors said: “Moscow has an obligation under international law to pay compensation for its illegal invasion.

“Using these funds to help Ukraine defend itself would not only be just, it would also be more acceptable to our fellow citizens than using more European taxpayers’ money or further borrowing.”

A previous attempt to tap into the funds last year to help Ukraine failed over concerns the liability would fall on the Belgian bank which held most of the assets and so “expose it to unacceptable risks”.

To counter this, Ms Reeves and others proposed to “transfer custody” of the frozen accounts to the EU itself, along with the associated liabilities.

The bloc would also provide cover to mitigate the risks in the event Moscow “considers acts of retaliation”.

The articles comes as Putin said that Russia ⁠is prepared ‌for a potential war with Europe in his latest threat, though ‌he added Moscow does not ‌want one.

The Russian president said his country supports any steps aimed at restoring relations with European countries, as he commented on his phone call with the leaders of Norway and Finland, which took place on Tuesday.

But in a chilling threat on Thursday, he said that if tensions were to escalate too far, there would be no winners.

Ms Reeves pointed out there was precedent for this following the fall of former Iraqi leader Saddam Hussein, which saw the EU transfer Iraqi assets to an account at the Federal Reserve Bank of New York.

Stressing the need for action, the article said: “Ukraine faces a desperate winter as Russia pummels its almost defenceless people from the air.

“It also faces a 70 billion-euro financial hole next year. Without new funding, Kyiv’s capacity to sustain its defence will be gravely impaired.

“This will undermine Europe’s security because Ukraine is our first line of defence against Russian aggression.

“Although other allies, including Canada and the United Kingdom, must do their bit, the European Union will have to take the lead in filling the funding gap.”

They argued if the bloc moved “others will follow”, although it was acknowledged it was “much less probable that the United States will act”.

They added: “The time to act is now. This is partly because of Ukraine’s financial needs but also because Trump is putting pressure on Kyiv to cut a deal with Moscow.

“Ukraine is ready to engage in peace talks as it has always been. But Russia must feel increased pressure to engage in genuine negotiations. The transfer of its accounts would provide this needed pressure.”

#744. Attack of the clones: Alaska has a “black PR” moment

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

US President Donald Trump has accused Democrats of fraud over an alleged plot to field a namesake candidate in Alaska’s Senate race.

An electoral trick familiar to Russians who remember the political free-for-all of the 1990s, the tactic involves recruiting a candidate whose name matches that of an established politician in the hope of confusing voters and siphoning off votes.

In Russia, such contenders became known as “clone candidates” and were a hallmark of the country’s notoriously ruthless election campaigns, forming part of the political “black PR” playbook.

In Alaska, Republican incumbent Senator Dan S. Sullivan now faces a namesake challenger, Daniel J. Sullivan Jr., a retired teacher who is also running in the November 3 election.

According to a New York Times investigation, operatives linked to Democratic candidate Mary Peltola’s campaign and the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee allegedly helped recruit the namesake candidate as part of a broader effort to undermine the incumbent. The newspaper cited interviews, text messages and documents detailing the operation, which reportedly began in 2025. Both Peltola’s campaign and the Democratic committee have denied involvement.

Following the report, Alaska Governor Mike Dunleavy, a Republican, ordered an investigation into whether any laws had been broken and whether criminal charges or civil action was warranted. Trump has branded the alleged scheme “voter fraud at the highest level” and called for Peltola and the namesake candidate to be removed from the ballot and prosecuted.

@Odinsraven66 @StephenM To be precise, there is no indication what party "Sullivan, Daniel J. Jr.” might be affiliated with on the Alaska bingo card. https://t.co/TWjsD2dWtT

Russians know the playbook. During the 1998 elections to the St. Petersburg Legislative Assembly, namesake candidates appeared in multiple constituencies. At least two challengers sharing the surname Mironov ran against Sergey Mironov, then acting speaker of the regional parliament. He nevertheless secured a decisive victory with around 70% of the vote.

In a 2000 State Duma by-election in Sverdlovsk Region, Yekaterinburg police chief Nikolay Ovchinnikov faced Vladimir Ovchinnikov, who appeared above him on the alphabetically arranged ballot. The namesake reportedly secured more than 5% of the vote, although the police chief ultimately won.

An isolated example of the practice surfaced in St. Petersburg as recently as 2021, when opposition politician Boris Vishnevsky faced two rivals who had legally changed their names to Boris Vishnevsky, adopting similar hairstyles and beards for their election portraits.

This is not the first time the practice has appeared in the US. In the 2020 Florida state Senate race, a little-known candidate named Alex Rodriguez received more than 6,000 votes, while Democratic incumbent Jose Javier Rodríguez lost to Republican Ileana Garcia by just 32 votes.

The namesake candidate subsequently pleaded guilty to campaign-finance offenses connected with the scheme, which involved a former Republican state senator.

→ Baca artikel asli

#706. Australia sitting on unused goldmine of old gigantic tyres

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Recyclers say mining companies are burying $200m worth of natural rubber every year. (Supplied: Tyrecycle)

Every year, Australia's major miners choose to bury more than 100,000 tonnes of end-of-life tyres.

The Australian Tyre Recyclers Association says the natural rubber alone could be worth up to $200 million.

Advocates are calling on state and federal governments to more tightly regulate mining tyre disposal.

Among the largest tyres ever manufactured, they help extract and haul billions of dollars of Australian resources.

But recyclers say the huge amount of rubber discarded by mining companies is its own kind of buried treasure.

For decades, the mining industry has been legally allowed to bury, stockpile, or send to landfill more than 100,000 tonnes of end-of-life tyres every year.

It is in sharp contrast to the rest of the tyre market, which collects about 96 per cent of passenger and commercial tyres for recycling, according to the Australian Tyre Recyclers Association (ATRA).

A haul truck operating in Kalgoorlie-Boulder's Super Pit gold mine. (ABC Goldfields: Jarrod Lucas)

ATRA executive officer Robert Kelman said just two per cent of off-the-road (OTR) mining tyres were recovered.

"These are very big mining tyres. They might be four, five, six tonnes," Mr Kelman said.

Long argued on environmental grounds, a new report is mounting the economic case for OTC tyre recycling.

ATRA found up to $200 million of raw, natural rubber was being buried annually on mine sites.

That dollar figure is expected to climb as global rubber production lags behind demand.

"There are risks to virgin rubber production, which is essential for these mining tyres," Mr Kelman said.

"The mining industry should be a part of shoring up the production of new tyres, getting that material off site, [and] getting it into the recovery markets."

Global production of natural rubber is expected to fall short of demand. (Reuters: Nguyen Huy Kham)

The Association of Natural Rubber Producing Countries forecasts a natural rubber shortfall of 317,000 tonnes in 2026, citing erratic weather across tropical forest plantations in south-east Asia.

Miles Kenney-Lazar, a rubber expert at the University of Melbourne, said the commodity often cycled through highs and lows, but now other factors were affecting production.

"More recently, there's a lot of environmental changes that are impacting rubber plantations, like flooding and climate change," he said.

Meanwhile, the need for the material has only grown alongside electric vehicles, which tend to be heavier and wear through rubber more quickly.

"If better systems can be put in place to ensure those tyres can efficiently be recycled and put on the market, then I don't see why … mining companies or others wouldn't want to take advantage," Dr Kenney-Lazar said.

The West Australian and Commonwealth governments have collectively invested $20.7 million into six tyre recycling infrastructure projects under the Recycling Modernisation Fund, while a further $62 million has been kicked in by industry.

One of those facilities was built by Tyrecycle in WA's Pilbara region — home to 49 per cent of the nation's mining tyres.

But 18 months after opening for business in Port Hedland, the plant is processing a fraction of its 30,000 tonne capacity.

Tyrecycle's Port Hedland facility is operating at 10 per cent of its capacity. (Supplied: Tyrecycle)

"It has been slow going, to be honest," company spokesperson Ashley Battilana said.

"We can do 30,000 tonnes easily and, with some more infrastructure put in there, we could easily go a bit more, but currently we're only doing 2,000 to 3,000 tonnes per annum."

Mr Battilana called on lawmakers to tighten regulations and force companies to face the up-front costs of recycling.

"The government's undermining the tyre recycling industry by allowing the burial of tyres," he said.

"The infrastructure is there to process these tyres. The offtake is there as well, both domestically and internationally.

Western Australia's Chamber of Minerals and Energy said the sector aimed to minimise waste "wherever feasible."

Policy and advocacy director Anita Logiudice pointed to existing partnerships with tyre recyclers to produce crumb for road bitumen.

"However, some barriers remain," she said.

"These include logistical challenges associated with transporting OTR tyres from remote mines to the nearest recycling plant, as well as the relatively small market for crumb rubber in WA."

The Chamber of Minerals and Energy says logisitics remain the biggest barrier to tyre recycling. (ABC News: Keana Naughton)

Earlier this year, a parliamentary inquiry made 12 recommendations to the federal government, including the staged, legislated reduction of onsite burial for OTR tyres over the next five years.

A spokesperson for Industry and Innovation Minister Tim Ayres said the government was still considering its response.

#527. Brazil’s Flavio Bolsonaro isolated, ridiculed after self-exile

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Defeated presidential candidate Flavio Bolsonaro sneaked out of Brazil to the US, where he declared himself to be the “true president” of his country.

RIO DE JANEIRO - In a viral wave of mockery, Brazilian popstar Anitta declared herself free of cellulite, one influencer declared himself the “true president” of Twitter, and another social media user proclaimed himself the winner of the next lottery.

The target of the taunts was Flavio Bolsonaro, who – after fleeing to the United States following his election defeat – proclaimed himself the leader of Brazil on Oct 28 in a confusing series of revised statements.

The moves by the 45-year-old senator have been met in Brazil with a mixture of derision, disbelief, and disapproval, even from within his own camp.

The eldest son of far-right former president Jair Bolsonaro was blindsided by his election defeat to leftist incumbent Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva on Oct 25, after leading a first-round vote earlier this month.

In a cloak-and-dagger operation he sneaked out of Brazil via Argentina and Ecuador, after being ordered to surrender his passports due to several ongoing investigations against him.

Hours after his arrival in Florida, he posted a statement on social media declaring himself the “true president” of Brazil, before revising the wording a few minutes later to describe himself as the “leader” of the country.

A Bolsonaro ally who published an earlier version of the statement bearing Brazil’s old coat of arms, abandoned in the sixties, said it had been a ChatGPT error.

The letter was met with outrage in Brazilian media, which described its tone as “coup-mongering,” while politicians from across the spectrum called for the election results to be respected.

A column in the O Globo daily likened Flavio Bolsonaro to a “lunatic asylum Napoleon,” while the Folha de Sao Paulo newspaper slammed the statement as “pathetic.”

On the right, the powerful governor of Sao Paulo, Tarcisio de Freitas, quickly distanced himself.

De Freitas was a former minister under Jair Bolsonaro who had been tipped as the main candidate for the right before the former president chose his son

“We are democrats. The election result is sovereign. What we have seen is the will of the majority of the population, which must be respected,” De Freitas said in an interview with the Estadao daily.

As speculation switched almost immediately to who would lead the right with Flavio out of the country, the governor – a favourite in business circles – said a 2030 run was “a possibility”.

Rogerio Marinho, who co-ordinated Flavio Bolsonaro’s campaign, said there was “no evidence” of electoral fraud, as claimed by the defeated candidate.

Electoral court chief Kassio Nunes Marques, who was appointed to the Supreme Court by Jair Bolsonaro, issued a statement to highlight the “secure electoral process.”

Flavio Bolsonaro also said he would lead the opposition from abroad.

His self-proclamation as Brazil’s leader in exile “is not a misstep, it is a calculated move” intended to maintain his influence over the conservative camp in Brazil, said Geraldo Monteiro, a political scientist at the State University of Rio de Janeiro.

The stakes are high: despite their election defeat, the right scored a strong majority in Congress.

Bolsonaro’s Liberal Party (PL) will hold the largest number of seats from February.

“I do not believe he has the means to lead the opposition from afar, as the newly elected officials have already secured their seats and no longer need him,” Monteiro told AFP.

He said Flavio Bolsonaro’s letter has “drawn a red line” between those who choose “extreme loyalty” and those who “prefer not to embark on another putschist adventure.”

Monteiro said Flavio was following in the footsteps of his father, who was sentenced to 27 years for plotting a coup after his 2022 election loss to Lula.

On Oct 29, PL leader Valdemar Costa Neto visited Jair Bolsonaro, who is serving his sentence under house arrest.

“No one approves” of the letter, Neto said as he left the meeting, adding that the party needed to “move forward.” REUTERS

#368. Patrick Stewart, Star Trek actor and acclaimed thespian, dies aged 86

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Star Trek: the Next Generation and X-Men actor died at his home after ‘a brief and poignant journey with Alzheimer’s disease and Lewy body dementia’

Patrick Stewart dies aged 86 – latest updates

Patrick Stewart, the acclaimed British stage and screen actor renowned for both Shakespeare and sci-fi, has died. He was 86.

The Star Trek: the Next Generation and X-Men star died on Wednesday at his home in Los Angeles after “a brief and poignant journey with Alzheimer’s disease and Lewy body dementia”, said Kelly Bush Novak, his longtime spokesperson, to the Hollywood Reporter.

“He has been held in the warm embrace of family and friends, surrounded by live music, prayer, tears, laughter and the kind of profound emotion that only he could inspire after a lifetime of devotion to us all,” she wrote. “We will forever cherish the peaceful ease and reflection on his face, an enduring reminder of his invincible grace.”

With his authoritative baritone voice and commanding visage, Stewart was already a staple of the British stage by the late 1980s when, at 47, he was cast as a graying Capt Jean-Luc Picard in Star Trek: the Next Generation, the first live-action spin-off of the groundbreaking 1960s TV series. Over seven seasons onboard the Starship Enterprise, Stewart’s even-keeled performance and steely directives – “engage!” and “make it so” – turned him into a global star.

Stewart reprised his role in four feature film spin-offs – Star Trek Generations (1994), Star Trek: First Contact (1996), Star Trek: Insurrection (1998) and Star Trek: Nemesis (2002) – as well as the TV series Star Trek: Picard, which ran from 2020 until 2023.

Fellow Star Trek actor William Shatner paid tribute on social media. “From the stage to the bridge of the Enterprise, he brought intelligence, grace, and a deep sense of humanity to everything he did,” he wrote. “Captain Picard stood for the best of us, and so did the man who played him. I was filled with affection and admiration for him and his work.”

George Takei, who played Hikaru Sulu in the original series, also wrote about Stewart on Bluesky. “He inspired a whole next generation of star-gazers who looked longingly ahead to a brighter future for all humanity,” he posted. “Our hearts are heavy, indeed. Rest in the heavens, Captain.”

His other most well-known role was playing Prof Charles Xavier in the X-Men series. The actor had found work hard to obtain after feeling typecast as a result of his Star Trek role but had been intrigued by working with director Bryan Singer for 2000’s X-Men.

He went on to play the role in seven films and will be seen reprising the role in this December’s Avengers: Doomsday.

His X-Men co-star Hugh Jackman shared the following on Instagram today: “We lost one of the greats of stage and screen today. And, in my book, a tremendous friend and man. I learned so much from him. I will miss you, Patrick Stewart, all of my days.”

Marvel chief Kevin Feige has paid tribute in a statement calling him “brilliant, charming, warm, generous, with a quiet strength and gravitas”. He added: “Away from the cameras, he was even more remarkable, with a wonderfully sharp sense of humor. It was a true privilege to know him and to work with him. His legacy lives on not only in decades of extraordinary work, but in the many, many people he mentored. He will be deeply missed.”

His other film roles included the gay comedy Jeffrey, romantic thriller Conspiracy Theory, violent horror Green Room and voice roles in Ted, Gnomeo & Juliet and, most recently, The Sheep Detectives.

Born on 13 July 1940 in the small industrial village of Mirfield, England, Stewart was the youngest of three sons born to Alfred Stewart, a British military veteran turned semiskilled laborer, and Gladys Stewart, a weaver at a textile factory. His childhood, in a small house with no running hot water, was marred by alcoholism and domestic violence, a situation he later described, in an article for the Guardian from 2009, as “barely tolerable”.

He found respite in acting, first in school – he was 12 when an English teacher assigned him the part of Shylock in Shakespeare’s The Merchant of Venice – and then local drama groups. “I managed to find my own refuge in acting,” he wrote. “The stage was a far safer place for me than anything I had to live through at home – it offered escape. I could be someone else, in another place, in another time.”

After graduating from secondary school at 15, he worked as a local reporter and furniture salesman before a local arts council provided the funding to attend the prestigious Bristol Old Vic Theater School, where he enrolled in 1957, at 17. Having shed his Yorkshire accent, he took his first professional theater role two years later, and in 1966 joined the Royal Shakespeare Company. During his 16 years with the company, Stewart developed a reputation as one of the finest thespians of his era and a representative of Shakespeare to new audiences; it was while conducting master classes on Shakespeare in LA, at the invitation of a friend, that he first caught the eye of a TV casting director for Star Trek.

Stewart made his Broadway debut in 1971 in Peter Brook’s A Midsummer Night’s Dream, and appeared again 20 years later in a solo production of A Christmas Carol. The holiday season show, which he revived in 1992, 1994 and 2001, received a Drama Desk award.

Over the course of his career, Stewart embodied many of Shakespeare’s signature roles, including Macbeth, Othello, Prospero, Shylock and Mark Antony. He was the recipient of two Laurence Olivier awards – in 1979, for Antony and Cleopatra, and in 2009 for Hamlet – as well as a Grammy in 1996 for a spoken word children’s album Prokofiev: Peter and the Wolf. He was also nominated for a Tony and four Emmys.

His final major stage role was in 2016, when he appeared as Hirst in Harold Pinter’s No Man’s Land at London’s Wyndham Theatre. In 2023, he released his memoir called Making It So.

Stewart was a vocal fan of Huddersfield Town FC since he was a child and was seen cheering them on most recently in 2024. In a Facebook post from the official team, he was called “a titan of his craft and a legend and hero to millions worldwide”.

Stewart was married three times: to theater choreographer Sheila Falconer, from 1966 until 1990 and with whom he had two children, fellow actors Daniel and Sophie; to Star Trek producer Wendy Neuss from 2000 until 2003; and to jazz musician Sunny Ozell from 2013 until his death. His friend and occasional collaborator Ian McKellen, with whom Stewart starred in two Broadway productions in 2013, officiated his final wedding after a rehearsal.

“I think I’ve been in denial about my life for a very long time,” he said to the Guardian in a 2023 interview. “I never really took on board how successful I had become. Even today I sometimes think, ‘You’re a lucky son of a bitch, Patrick,’ when something unexpected or good happens to me.”

#765. First Guardian investors dealt another 'utterly devastating' blow

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

First Guardian investor Melinda Kee is fighting for her superannuation. (ABC News: Keith Blackburn)

First Guardian investors are devastated to learn they may not recover millions of dollars.

Out of nearly half a billion lost in the collapse, liquidators have recovered about $8 million.

The corporate regulator ASIC is taking legal action.

Thousands of First Guardian investors have received the devastating news that they may not be able to recover the millions of dollars they invested in the scheme that later collapsed.

Out of nearly half a billion lost in the collapse, liquidators have been able to recover about $8 million, but some $7 million has already gone in liquidation fees.

The corporate regulator ASIC is taking legal action against several players involved in marketing and running the schemes.

The liquidators of Falcon Capital, Ross Blakeley and Paul Harlon of FTI Consulting, said they still couldn't tell 6,000 First Guardian investors how much, if anything, they'll get back from asset sales.

That's because much of the funds appear to have been channelled offshore.

They expect to give a firmer picture after a series of court hearings involving some of the major players in November and December this year.

The directors valued the fund's investments at about $512.8 million.

So far the liquidators said they had recovered $8.02 million, or just 1.56 per cent.

First Guardian investor Melinda Kee, who also runs the group Save our Super, felt broken after hearing the news.

In November 2023, Ms Kee was given financial advice to switch her super from a retail fund into First Guardian and she lost $400,000 of her retirement savings.

The collapses of the First Guardian and Shield schemes have exposed deep flaws in the regulation of Australia's $4.3 trillion superannuation sector.

She is one of 11,800 investors who collectively lost more than $1 billion in retirement savings after moving their super into First Guardian and the Shield-managed investment schemes, both of which later collapsed.

"It is utterly devastating that after 18 months, just $8 million has been recovered from more than $512 million in investments, while almost $7 million has already been absorbed by liquidation costs," Ms Kee told ABC News.

"People living with unimaginable stress and uncertainty, watching their futures slip away while they continue to wait for answers, justice and accountability."

In December, under a deal with the corporate regulator, Netwealth reimbursed about $100 million to about 1,000 First Guardian investors who invested through its platform.

Investors who lost out in a superannuation scam are considering a potential class action.

The settlement raised suggestions two other financial services groups, Diversa and Equity Trustees, through which clients invested in First Guardian, would also do a similar deal with the regulator.

But the Australian Securities and Investments Commission (ASIC) is pursuing both Diversa and Equity Trustees in court, and they have said they will defend the lawsuits.

Some First Guardian investors are considering a class action to try recover their money.

Others can apply through what's known as the Compensation Scheme of Last Resort, but that's capped at $150,000 and some investors lost far more than that.

In their report, liquidators FTI Consulting, sort the investments into three main groups.

Six thousand First Guardian investors have been told by the fund's liquidator there may be insufficient funds to pay back the superannuation funds they invested.

These include "written off" or "settled" investments worth about $117.6 million.

Of these, only $3.49 million, about 3 per cent, has come back, and the liquidators don't expect much more, since several of the companies involved are themselves in liquidation.

The second category includes "further recoveries expected" based on investments totalling $50.75 million.

So far $4.53 million (or 8.9 per cent) has come back, but the liquidators said they don't expect to recover the full value.

The third category the liquidators list falls under "uncertain" recoveries worth about $344.5 million.

In this case, nothing has been recovered so far and may never be, as they are mostly investments that the fund's operators channelled into overseas property and "speculative technology start-ups".

FTI Consulting also reports the value of the fees it is taking in the process—payments were about $6.96 million. The biggest items were their legal fees ($3.33 million) and liquidators' remuneration ($3.23 million).

On Thursday, ASIC told Senate Estimates that the corporate regulator is considering all options to disrupt the sale of InterPrac Financial Planning to Bellbird Australia.

Of the more than 11,000 investors who pumped more than $1 billion of their retirement savings into the First Guardian and Shield schemes, more than 6,000 were clients of advisers under InterPrac, which is owned by publicly listed company Sequoia.

These 6,000 clients put $680 million into the two funds on the advice of their financial planners who were licensed under InterPrac, and worry that the sale could shrink the pool of money available to compensate them.

ASIC chair Sarah Court told Senate Estimates that the regulator was "considering all options" on whether it could stop InterPrac's attempted sale of its business to Bellbird Australia.

#262. Palestinians injured, arrested in Israeli raids across West Bank

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This comes as more than 3,300 Palestinians are forced from homes in Hebron in Israeli military offensive.

Israeli forces and settlers have escalated pogroms and raids across the occupied West Bank, attacking and injuring Palestinians, restricting their movement, carrying out arrests and demolishing homes.

This comes as a military offensive and siege on the Hebron governorate entered its seventh day – an operation that has displaced or besieged hundreds of Palestinian families and kept tens of thousands of students out of classrooms.

In the Qalandiya refugee camp just north of occupied East Jerusalem, Israeli forces carried out a raid that triggered clashes on Thursday. Two children were wounded, the Palestine Red Crescent Society said.

It said its teams treated a 15-year-old boy who was shot with live ammunition in both legs, and a child, aged 12, who was injured by live-bullet fragments. Both were taken to hospital.

In a separate incident, Israeli soldiers and settlers beat disabled Palestinian Mohammad Ahmad Ayesh Thawabta in the village of al-Maniya, southeast of Bethlehem, the official Palestinian news agency Wafa reported.

In the northern West Bank, Israeli forces detained 13 Palestinians, including three brothers, in raids before dawn on Thursday, the Palestinian Prisoners Media Office and local sources said. Most of the raids were in the Nablus governorate, where soldiers searched homes and harassed families.

In Hebron, Israeli forces continued raids and closures that began last week after two Israeli soldiers were killed in a gun attack. Both Palestinian suspects were also killed.

Hebron Governor Khaled Dudin said on Thursday that 537 families, comprising 3,361 people, have been forced from their homes, Wafa reported.

They include 232 infants, 326 older people, 147 pregnant or breastfeeding women and 213 people with chronic illnesses. Municipal figures put the total number affected, including those under siege, at about 5,000 people from 780 families.

Dudin said the offensive has disrupted education for about 262,000 students across 589 schools in the governorate this week, forcing schools to move to remote learning. Officials estimated daily economic losses at 10 million to 12 million shekels ($3.2m to $3.9m).

The Palestinian Prisoner’s Society said Israeli forces detained 110 Palestinians in the governorate from Friday to Wednesday. Soldiers beat two men in Dura and Wad al-Saman and arrested two brothers while they were ploughing their land east of Yatta, it said. Israeli forces also raided homes in Taffuh and Halhul, and settlers attacked vehicles at the city’s closed northern entrance, it added.

In nearby Masafer Yatta, armed settlers attacked US journalist Jasper Nathaniel and two activists in the village of Umm al-Khair on Wednesday. Russian activist Adele Shoko said on X that the settlers beat and threatened them and stole their equipment and Nathaniel was pepper-sprayed and arrested.

The United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) said Israeli forces are still operating in Hebron, blocking access and imposing tight restrictions on movement across the governorate.

They are “disrupting Palestinians’ access to livelihoods, education and healthcare. Most displaced families are staying with relatives,” OCHA said, calling for the prevention of further forced displacements and demolitions.

Israeli forces also demolished memorials to Palestinians in several parts of the Hebron governorate on Thursday. In Dura, south of Hebron city, soldiers fired a heavy barrage of tear gas before bulldozing a mural bearing the images of several of the town’s dead, including Bajis Abu Atwan, who was killed in the 1970s.

The targeting of memorials “appears to be a new pattern recently adopted by Israeli forces across all West Bank governorates – though at a higher rate here in Hebron”, Al Jazeera’s Muntasir Nassar said, reporting from Dura.

Israeli military and settler attacks across the West Bank have intensified since Israel’s war on Gaza began in October 2023.

#813. Russia, Africa call for reform of global financial institutions

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

MOSCOW, October 29. /TASS/. Russia and African countries consider reform of the International Monetary Fund (IMF), the World Trade Organization and the World Bank necessary, according to the Declaration of the Third Russia-Africa Summit.

The parties agreed to advocate comprehensive reform of the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank in order to ensure that the institutions operate based on the principles of depoliticization, equality and respect for the interests of all parties. They also called for greater African representation in the IMF and World Bank leadership, as well as a greater role for African countries in decision-making.

Russia and African nations also agreed to promote reform of the World Trade Organization to ensure an open, transparent, inclusive, non-discriminatory and rules-based trading system.

The parties emphasized the need to preserve the central role of the World Trade Organization and uphold fundamental principles, including consensus-based decision-making, most-favoured-nation treatment, and special and differential treatment for developing and least-developed countries.

#282. Israel election result shows no overall majority as vote count complete

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Opposition explores options to form coalition as no party secures the 61 seats required for parliamentary majority.

Vote counting is complete in Israel’s elections with neither Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s bloc nor the opposition winning a majority in parliament, Israeli media have said.

As the provisional results were being published on Thursday, opposition politicians met to explore options for a coalition.

With more than 99 percent of Tuesday’s ballots tallied, Netanyahu’s bloc held 53 seats in the 120-member Knesset while the opposition bloc led by Gadi Eisenkot secured 48 seats, Israeli outlet Haaretz reported. The Arab list parties, meanwhile, held a combined 19 seats, it added.

Both Netanyahu’s bloc and the opposition parties fell short of securing the 61 seats required for a parliamentary majority.

The final results of the Knesset elections will be certified and announced by Wednesday. Until that date, the results are subject to change.

Tuesday’s elections – the first since the October 7, 2023, attacks on southern Israel and the start of Israel’s genocidal war on Gaza – were widely seen as a referendum on Netanyahu’s premiership.

On Thursday, Eisenkot, who heads the Yashar party, met former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett, who leads the Together party, to discuss ways to form a new government “as quickly as possible”, according to a statement reported by Israel’s Ynet News outlet.

Eisenkot also met Democrats party chairman Yair Golan, the only member of the opposition alliance to support forming a coalition with parties led by Palestinian citizens of Israel, while all other political groups have rejected any such union.

Golan met separately with Mansour Abbas, who leads the United Arab list, or Ra’am, which is projected to win seven of the 19 Arab party seats.

“This is a time for action. Our responsibility is not to miss the opportunity for change and the salvation of Israel,” Golan said in a post on X after his meeting with Abbas.

Al Jazeera correspondent Nour Odeh noted that “Eisenkot has an uphill battle ahead of him.”

The former military leader-turned-politician has pledged “not to ally himself or get into a partnership with the Palestinian Israeli parties”, Odeh said, reporting from the occupied West Bank because Al Jazeera is banned from Israel.

But given the fact that no political bloc has a majority, the Eisenkot-led opposition bloc cannot form a governing coalition without the support of Arab-led parties, she added.

Meanwhile, Netanyahu’s Likud party has continued to amplify unsubstantiated allegations that Arab-led parties had “stolen” the election, potentially with foreign assistance – claims that have been rejected.

The head of the Central Elections Committee, Supreme Court Justice Noam Sohlberg, said “serious suspicions” of foreign interference had been brought to him in a letter to the Shin Bet security agency. Sohlberg said he could not verify the claims, but given their serious nature, he asked the agency to investigate.

Eisenkot accused Netanyahu of working to delegitimise the election results with his allegations.

In a video posted on his X account, Eisenkot said: “The days of Netanyahu using state institutions against Israeli interests for his personal needs are over,” adding that no “spin” can change voters rejecting his government at the polls.

“Benjamin Netanyahu is not satisfied with the results, no matter how much he has been saying that he won,” political analyst Xavier Abu Eid told Al Jazeera.

He added that while it seems unlikely the opposition could take advantage by overcoming differences with the Arab-led groups, there could be an understanding to replace the current speaker of the Knesset, who is close to Netanyahu.

“If there is a new speaker in the Knesset, … Netanyahu loses an important ally,” Abu Eid said, adding that this might give an opening for the opposition to “actually start passing legislation” against him.

Odeh said the next steps would involve negotiations between political groups after the Central Elections Committee announces and certifies the election results by Wednesday.

#820. Antisemitism envoy proposes mask ban and visa review measures

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Australia's envoy to combat antisemitism, Jillian Segal, with her latest report. (ABC News: Dan Guia)

Australia's envoy to combat antisemitism is proposing visa review measures and bans on face coverings in a report delivered 10 months after the Bondi Beach terror attack.

Jillian Segal is also calling for creative industries to take steps to re-engage with Jewish artists.

The measures are among a raft of proposals building on an earlier plan to combat antisemitism.

Bans on face coverings, exclusion zones for protesters and extra visa review measures are among a raft of proposals being put forward by Australia's envoy to combat antisemitism.

Jillian Segal's report comes 10 months after 15 people were killed in a shooting targeting a Jewish festival at Bondi Beach.

It builds on an earlier plan released by Ms Segal and suggests dozens of new measures to tackle antisemitism in response to emerging threats.

The report singles out progressive antisemitism, incorporating elements of far-left and Islamic extremist ideology, as the "most pervasive" form of antisemitism facing Australia.

The report calls for the introduction of restrictions on protests, such as bans on face coverings.

Israeli president Isaac Herzog's visit to Sydney sparked heated protests. (ABC News: Jack Fisher)

Victoria has already given police powers to remove masks at protests and the NSW government brought in laws to restrict protests that the state's top court deemed unconstitutional.

Ms Segal has also proposed safe access exclusion zones for protests, citing similar measures that prevent protests outside abortion clinics.

"Antisemitism doesn't start with violence, it starts with words and then those words progress through different stages if they're not appropriately dealt with," she said.

The report also identifies areas where progress on tackling antisemitism "remains insufficient" and names the arts and cultural sector as one of particular concern.

Ms Segal said Jewish people continued to feel excluded in creative industries and she recommended institutions take steps to address this.

"Those Jewish artists have not been re-embraced and we have to find a way to make that happen," she said.

The Royal Commission on Antisemitism and Social Cohesion heard evidence from Jewish artists who said they had been blocked from professional opportunities and lost friends after the leaking of private messages.

The report has recommended the government trial a "modest" grant program to help re-engage Jewish creatives and stories into the arts sector.

It also recommends enforceable conditions for arts funding from organisations such as Creative Australia that would allow grants to be terminated if a recipient engaged in antisemitism.

Ms Segal argued the university sector had been previously stalling efforts to tackle antisemitism, but new racism standards, set to come into force on January 1, had since allayed her concerns.

Jillian Segal wants Home Affairs to check visa holders for antisemitic views. (AAP: Sarah Wilson)

The report also calls for further efforts to strengthen the visa processing system.

The Department of Home Affairs has already begun training staff involved in visa processing to help them recognise antisemitism.

But the report recommends setting up a dedicated group within the department to review previously granted visas where there are "credible concerns" the visa holder may hold antisemitic views.

It also calls for extra risk-based filters to determine whether a visa applicant may hold views "antithetical to Australian values", along with public reporting on the number of refusals and cancellations.

The report includes further detail on a proposal for extra oversight of public broadcasters the ABC and SBS, to prevent "the spread of antisemitic ideas and narratives".

Ms Segal previously floated the idea of an independent oversight committee to review the public broadcasters' handling of complaints about its coverage.

During evidence at the royal commission in July, she accused the broadcasters of "marking their own homework" in regard to internal complaints processes. The ABC and SBS have rejected that claim.

Now Ms Segal is calling for the existing responsibilities of the ABC and SBS ombudsmen to be transferred to an external ombudsman.

"It is in no way to attack their independence, because their independence is incredibly important for all of us, but to assure everyone that it's working effectively," she said.

The royal commission will deliver its final report on December 18.

#324. Palestinian President says hopes for peace talks with new Israeli government

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Updated - October 29, 2026 08:09 pm IST - Nicosia

Cyprus' President Nikos Christodoulides, right, welcomes the Palestinian President Mahmoud Abbas at the Presidential Palace in capital Nicosia, Cyprus, on Thursday, October 29, 2026. | Photo Credit: AP

Palestinian president Mahmoud Abbas said on Thursday (October 29, 2026) that he hoped the election of a new Israeli government would allow for the resumption of peace talks.

"For many years now, we have been extending a hand to the Israeli side in order to achieve peace with them, so that Israel and Palestine may live in security, stability and peace," Mr. Abbas said during a meeting with Cypriot President Nikos Christodoulides in Nicosia.

"We hope that, immediately after a new Israeli government is formed, we will be able to engage in dialogue together," he added.

With almost all ballots counted from Israel's pivotal election, neither the bloc of Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu nor the opposition bloc led by former military chief Gadi Eisenkot looked able to secure a majority in parliament.

The uncertain outcome might mean weeks of trying to cobble together coalitions.

The Israeli-Palestinian peace process has been stalled for years, despite the recognition of the State of Palestine by a growing number of countries.

Under Mr. Netanyahu's watch, settlement construction and violence against Palestinians has soared in the occupied West Bank, where Mr. Abbas' Palestinian Authority exercises limited self-rule.

Ministers in Mr. Netanyahu's hard-right coalition have openly called for Israel's annexation of the West Bank and advocated policies encouraging Palestinians to leave the territory as well as the war-ravaged Gaza Strip.

Mr. Netanyahu led Israel in a devastating war which has left much of Gaza in rubble following Hamas's unprecedented October 7, 2023 attack.

Israel has since 1967 occupied the West Bank, where more than 500,000 Israelis live in settlements illegal under international law among some three million Palestinians.

#336. Israel piles pressure on Arab parties as Netanyahu seeks government

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Account subscription benefits alongside Premium Stories, Editorials, Opinions and more. Unlock these with Subscription

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Updated - October 29, 2026 08:06 pm IST - Jerusalem

People walk next to a Likud party campaign ad featuring Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and U.S. President Donald Trump, with a Hebrew message reading "We have one opportunity to win," in Jerusalem on Wednesday, October 28, 2026, a day after the elections in Israel. | Photo Credit: AP

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's bloc on Thursday (October 29, 2026) piled pressure on Israeli Arab leaders over unproven accusations of foreign funding, in a push to neuter parties that could hold the key to forming a government after an inconclusive election.

Israeli President Isaac Herzog called for an investigation into allegations of fraud but cautioned against stigmatising the entire Arab Israeli community, who voted at unprecedented rates.

Israel was nearing the completion of ballot-counting from Tuesday's (October 27, 2026) election, in which neither Mr. Netanyahu nor the Opposition bloc led by former military chief Gadi Eisenkot looked able to secure a majority in the parliament known as the Knesset.

The Netanyahu camp's accusations, which a key Arab party has rejected, could bring uncertainty to the electoral process at a time when the threat of a return to war with Iran looms over the region.

Israel's election saw soaring participation by Israeli Arabs, descendants of Palestinians who stayed after the country's creation in 1948, as outrage boiled at calls by officials such as National Security Minister Itamar Ben Gvir to treat their community much as Israel has dealt with Palestinians in Gaza and the occupied West Bank.

Following Mr. Netanyahu's accusations of impropriety, Ben Gvir went so far as to encourage Israel to "immediately" order the arrests of Arab party leaders.

Raam party leader Mansour Abbas said he welcomed an investigation into claims of wrongdoing, adding that his moderate Islamist party had "no connection" with the accusations.

The Arab parties together have enough votes to take the opposition bloc over the finish line, but Mr. Eisenkot has baulked after relentless attacks by Mr. Netanyahu, who warned his right-wing Jewish base that voting for the Opposition would put Arabs in power.

Supreme Court Justice Noam Sohlberg, a conservative who heads the Central Election Commission, requested an investigation by the Shin Bet security service into allegations that Raam received money from Qatar, the energy-rich Gulf powerbroker that has channels to Hamas.

"The Raamgate scandal is a political earthquake. Israel's elections have been stolen!" Mr. Netanyahu's Likud party said.

Government officials did not offer evidence to back the allegations, and left-wing candidate Yair Golan accused Mr. Netanyahu of attempting to stir controversy after failing to get a majority.

"Now he is trying to challenge the election results through an ugly delegitimisation campaign against the will of the people, and against the Arab public in particular."

Mr. Netanyahu himself has long been under the cloud of allegations related to Qatar after the arrests last year of two of his aides for allegedly receiving payments from the Gulf state.

Qatar categorically denied the latest accusations of interference.

"They accuse Qatar of buying off Benjamin Netanyahu, the Israeli political left and now, in this latest campaign, the Arab vote," Foreign Ministry spokesman Majed Al Ansari said. "These accusations are ridiculous and dangerous in equal measure."

Mr. Netanyahu, Israel's longest-serving Prime Minister, for years tacitly supported Qatar funding for Hamas-ruled Gaza, hoping to preserve stability.

That paradigm collapsed on October 7, 2023, when Hamas carried out the deadliest single attack ever on Israel.

With nearly 93% of the vote counted, Mr. Netanyahu's bloc was at 53 seats and the Opposition forces led by Mr. Eisenkot on 48 — both well under the 61 needed to form a government.

The two main Arab formations were on track for an unprecedented 19 seats between them.

Some observers quickly said that the allegations would help Mr. Netanyahu by making the Arab lawmakers even more toxic to Mr. Eisenkot.

Yousef Jabareen, the head of the leftist Arab-majority Joint List, told AFP on Wednesday (October 28, 2026) that he was willing to cooperate with the Opposition to bring down Mr. Netanyahu but without joining it in a government.

In response to the new allegations, Mr. Eisenkot promised "utmost vigilance" against election interference.

"On the other hand, we cannot allow political actors to exploit the situation and influence the timing of the inauguration of the 26th Knesset," he said.

One of Mr. Eisenkot's right-leaning allies, Avigdor Lieberman, called the allegations "dramatic and unprecedented" and urged a swift investigation.

An Israeli police statement said that they would investigate every complaint about election integrity.

Mr. Netanyahu had tried during the voting on Tuesday (October 27, 2026) to raise allegations of fraud, saying that Arab parties were caught with envelopes of cash, and his Likud party peddled the accusations throughout Thursday (October 29, 2026).

Police said they arrested three suspects but the election commission called it an isolated local case.

Another factor that could play into the formation of a government is an attack on Iran.

Mr. Netanyahu has repeatedly threatened to strike Iran, with his ally U.S. President Donald Trump, signalling he was ready to attack after next Tuesday's (November 3, 2026) U.S. midterm elections.

Mr. Eisenkot has warned the military that Mr. Netanyahu has no mandate and that officers must put Israel's security over the premier's "political needs".

An attack could raise pressure on Mr. Eisenkot to join an emergency unity government, an option he has publicly ruled out, or keep the clock running until Israel can call new elections.

President Herzog will decide after the official results whom to task with the first chance to form a government.

Published - October 29, 2026 08:03 pm IST

#377. Healey backs away from cutting welfare cost amid warnings of public finance shortfall

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Experts round on chancellor over ‘scattergun’ budget, saying he ducked big tax and spending decisions

John Healey has backed away from cutting the cost of welfare this parliament, amid dire warnings his first budget could lead to brutal spending cuts because of a £28bn shortfall in public finances.

Experts rounded on Healey’s steady-as-she-goes budget on Thursday, with the Institute for Fiscal Studies saying he had left himself a mountain to climb by ducking big tax and spending decisions.

In a damning analysis, the IFS said Healey would need to have set aside an additional £28bn a year, to offset the impact of inflation on future spending plans and hit the target of spending 3% of GDP in defence by 2030.

Instead Helen Miller, the IFS director, said Healey had opened the way to “a spending review that looks pretty brutal for some departments” – including real-term budget cuts.

“Public service spending plans look extremely tight. They don’t look to me like plans that this government would want to deliver,” she said, adding that meant Healey could face “another season of damaging speculation about tax rises”.

The welfare bill will not be part of the spending review, which focuses on departmental budgets. But in a bid to underline his determination to balance the books, the chancellor pointed to plans for reform, telling BBC Radio 4’s Today programme: “We have to bring the benefits bill down.”

He had earlier appeared to say cuts would be made in this parliament, when asked if there would be a reduction in the bill before the next election. “There will be. There will be a reduction in the benefits bill,” said Healey. “We’ll do that in a Labour way and we will do that because it is simply not good for Britain or progress to have a bigger and bigger benefits bill.”

However, Treasury officials clarified that he had not meant to give a timeline and Healey later told Channel 4: “It’s not correct to say that we are going to bring this welfare bill down in this parliament because we’ve not set a timetable for that.”

Instead, he pointed to forthcoming reviews from Alan Milburn on employment support for young people, and from Stephen Timms on disability benefits.

Milburn is expected to recommend additional spending upfront, aimed at bringing down costs over the longer term by helping more young people into employment.

The Office for Budget Responsibility has said total welfare spending will grow from £334bn last year to £431bn by 2030 – with about half of that being spent on pensioners and a quarter on sickness and disability benefits. The overall forecast spending has risen by £8bn since March.

With a tough global economic backdrop, Healey has said the single biggest driver of better prospects for the UK would be an end to the Iran and Ukraine wars.

But in his interviews, Healey appeared to take a more cautious view than the prime minister on the potential for growth from rejoining Europe or the single market. Asked by LBC if growth would be stronger if the UK were in the EU or the single market, Healey replied: “Well, I don’t actually accept that. Brexit has certainly hit our level of growth. It’s certainly hit our level of trade. But we had a referendum. We’re out of the European Union. The challenge for me now is how do we make the best of an economy which this year has proved remarkably resilient.”

Asked later by Channel 4 if he, like Andy Burnham, hoped for the UK to re-enter the bloc in his lifetime, Healey said: “I’m older than Andy Burnham. I don’t expect to see that.”

The chancellor put measures to ease the cost of living at the heart of his first budget, including a £100 winter cost of living payment for most households. However, critics lined up on Thursday to accuse him of taking a “scattergun” approach – and of not hitting Britain’s banks hard enough to fund the package.

Get the day’s headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

The Resolution Foundation thinktank said Healey had failed to target energy support to the households that need it most ahead of a difficult winter, with bills expected to rise sharply in January and the Bank of England to raise interest rates next week.

Ruth Curtice, Resolution’s chief executive, said: “Faced with a tough backdrop amid global economic turmoil, the chancellor delivered a scattergun budget that did little to address the sharp challenges facing Britain.

“Cost of living support is being spread far too thinly and will leave even those families receiving help facing higher energy bills than they did during the last energy price shock.”

Paul Nowak, the TUC’s general secretary, has strongly advocated for a bank windfall tax to fund energy support. He said he welcomed Healey’s approach, but would be pushing for him to go further.

The chancellor has doubled the surcharge banks pay on their profits from 3% to 6% for 15 months. Novak said: “It was right to tax banks to help people with their energy bills, but I think banks could have been asked to pay a fairer share. He’s going to review it in 15 months. We’re going to be looking as part of that review – if banks are still making windfall profits – for him to extend and increase the bank tax.”

As well as casting doubt on whether Healey’s future spending plans are realistic, the IFS said the £15bn headroom, or buffer, he has left himself against Labour’s fiscal rules could easily be eroded by higher than expected energy costs.

The chancellor allowed the buffer to absorb some of the impact of the Iran war, which has driven up the government’s cost of borrowing. At the time of the spring statement, Rachel Reeves had built up headroom of £24bn.

Miller said that was a “reasonable” decision to make, but “a lower buffer leaves the chancellor more exposed to future bad news”.

#298. Government working flat out to reduce welfare bill, PM says after cost warnings

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The Government is working “flat out” to cut the ballooning costs of welfare spending, Prime Minister Andy Burnham has insisted after the Chancellor faced scrutiny about the rising costs of benefits in wake of the Budget.

John Healey was repeatedly pressed about the cost of welfare by broadcasters, after the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) warned sickness and disability benefits could cost £100 billion a year before the end of the decade.

But Treasury officials pushed back against media interpretations of Mr Healey’s responses which suggested he wanted the bill to start falling before the next election.

The Chancellor later said there was no “timetable” set to bring the cost of welfare down.

The Prime Minister was asked about his timeframe for reducing the benefit bill during a visit to Derby on Thursday.

He told reporters: “We want to get the welfare bill down as quickly as possible, and we’re working flat out to that end.

“Now that we are through the Budget, the big next priority in front of us is the publication of the Milburn report, long awaited, but it’s coming soon.

“We just want to get stuck into supporting people to help people off benefits and into work.

“We want that to happen as quickly as possible.”

In an interview with the BBC’s Today programme, Mr Healey was asked whether the benefits bill would be cut before the next general election.

He told the BBC: “There will be a reduction in the benefits bill, we’ll do that in a Labour way and we’ll do that because it is simply not good for Britain or progressive to have a bigger and bigger benefits bill.”

However, Treasury officials later stressed he was not setting a timeframe for the welfare reduction.

The Chancellor also said on LBC Radio that welfare reform would not amount to “crude benefit cuts” which would be a “pressure too far” when people were “suffering” with the cost of living.

He later told Channel 4 he and Mr Burnham were committed to bringing down the bill but that “it’s not correct to say that we’re going to bring this welfare bill down in this Parliament because we’ve not set a timetable for that”.

He later added: “I’ve set an indication, a timetable for the changes we look to make. What I’ve not done is set a timetable for any savings that come from those.”

Downing Street insisted the Chancellor had remained “completely in line with what the PM has said over the last few months that the welfare bill needs to come down” and added the Government was not “setting an arbitrary target or timetable on that”.

More details about the Government’s plans to bring down benefits spending will follow the publication of two major reviews next month, the Prime Minister’s official spokesman said.

“We’ll bring the bill down by tackling the underlying drivers of spending, getting more people into work, and matching support with clear opportunities and responsibilities to engage with the Government’s support offer,” the spokesman added.

The Institute for Fiscal Studies (IFS) warned that the Government’s plans to help households with the cost of living will not prevent living standards from falling.

Helen Miller, director of the influential think tank, said in her post-Budget analysis: “This decade looks set to be the worst decade for growth in household incomes on record. Just wait until you see the chart; it’s depressing. No amount of temporary cost of living packages will fix that.”

The IFS criticised the Chancellor for choosing to fund a £4.7 billion black hole in the defence investment plan (Dip) – left over by Sir Keir Starmer – in part from the Treasury’s reserves.

This further narrows Mr Healey’s options to keep his spending plans on course in the event of another global economic shock, the think tank said, while suggesting the Chancellor could face rampant speculation about tax rises ahead of the next budget.

The reserve may also be needed to top up departmental budgets facing inflationary pressures, according to the IFS.

Later on Thursday, Mr Healey admitted he had not been able to take action he “might have wanted to” on student loan repayments.

The Conservatives have promised to cut the amount of interest paid on some student loans amid widespread concerns over costs.

Asked why the repayment threshold had not been changed on ITV’s The Martin Lewis Money Show, he said: “I simply haven’t been able to do action I might have wanted to take at this Budget … quite honestly, I’m dealing with the pressures I’ve got.

“There’s a range of things I’d like to be able to look at and change, but in this Budget I had to concentrate on what was most important, most immediate, and that was help to households facing big energy bills through the winter, taking some of the cost-of-business pressures off.”

#557. US files lawsuit claiming Google wouldn’t give employee new job following military leave

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Google is accused of failing to give an employee his old job back or put him in a similar job following one year of military leave as a US Army reservist.

NEW YORK – The US filed a lawsuit accusing Google of failing to give an employee his old job back or find him a similar job after he took one year of military leave as a US Army reservist.

The Oct 29 case in Manhattan federal court is a rare instance of the US government suing a private employer for violating the Uniformed Services Employment and Reemployment Rights Act of 1994 (USERRA), which protects the civilian employment of military personnel called to active duty.

According to a complaint filed in Manhattan federal court, Mark Davisson had spent 2½ years as a Google key account executive, handling cloud services accounts for asset manager BlackRock and hedge fund Bridgewater Associates, and managing the US business of the former Credit Suisse.

Upon Davisson’s return from leave in May 2023, Google allegedly refused to let Davisson handle any customer accounts, depriving him of career advancement though he kept his pay and title.

The complaint said Google assigned Davisson in January 2024 to a cloud solutions position that also involved no management of customer accounts, and fired him seven months later following another military leave and as part of a broader workforce reduction.

Davisson, a Bronze Star and Meritorious Service Medal recipient, has a home in Charlotte, North Carolina, but lived and worked in Manhattan while Google employed him, the complaint said. Google’s parent Alphabet is based in Mountain View, California.

“We disagree with the claims in this complaint,” Google spokesman Atle Erlingsson said in an e-mail.

“Google takes compliance with employment laws, including USERRA, seriously and investigates allegations of workplace wrongdoing.”

The lawsuit was brought by the civil rights unit of the US Attorney’s office in Manhattan, with backing from Assistant Attorney-General Harmeet Dhillon, the Trump administration’s top civil rights enforcer.

It seeks to recoup lost wages and benefits as well as additional damages for Davisson, and requires Google to ensure a non-discriminatory workplace including by improving training regarding USERRA.

“Federal law ensures that our brave soldiers, who sacrifice so much to keep us safe, are not punished by employers when they return to the civilian workforce after completing their military service,” US Attorney Jamie McDonald in Manhattan said in a statement. REUTERS

#311. Trump says won’t ‘take sides’ in Brazil election, but calls it a ‘disgrace’

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Comments come after Trump ally Flavio Bolsonaro fled to Miami in the US and claimed, without evidence, that vote was stolen.

United States President Donald Trump declined to say if he agreed with the outcome of Sunday’s presidential elections in Brazil, but said he thought the contest was a “disgrace”.

“I thought the election was a great embarrassment to the people of Brazil,” Trump said in response to a question from a reporter on Thursday morning. “I watched the election. I didn’t take sides. I let it be. I was asked not to take sides by the president, the current president,” he said. “But when I watched what went down in Brazil, it was a disgrace…take a look at the market, I mean their market went down through the floor.”

Trump’s remarks came a day after former Brazilian Senator Flavio Bolsonaro and his family fled Brazil and landed in Miami, Florida, in the US on Wednesday.

Bolsonaro and his family were aided in their journey out of Brazil by Trump and his administration, the New York Times reported Thursday. Officials told the newspaper that the US government would likely provide the Bolsonaro family permanent residency.

On Sunday, Bolsonaro had lost a run-off election for Brazil’s president to the left-wing incumbent Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva by more than four million votes. The Brazilian real dropped against the dollar by 12 percent in spot trading after the election.

Brazil‘s Supreme Court had ordered Bolsonaro to surrender his regular and diplomatic passports shortly after the election, in an attempt to make sure the right-wing senator did not flee the country before he could be investigated for embezzlement and money laundering.

Bolsonaro left Brazil under cover of darkness, crossing by land with his wife and two daughters into Argentina, where the government of Trump’s right-wing ally President Javier Milei had granted them visas. The family then took a small private plane to Ecuador and later on to Miami, where they landed on Wednesday morning.

Shortly after landing, Bolsonaro issued a statement saying he was “the true elected president of Brazil” and the allegations against him were “a politically motivated witch-hunt”. He asked the international community “not to normalise what is happening in Brazil” and said he would lead the country’s opposition from the US. He did not provide evidence supporting his claims.

Bolsonaro’s own Liberal Party said on Monday that it was accepting the outcome of the election.

Although Brazil and the US have had an extradition treaty for several decades, it is unclear if Washington would turn over Bolsonaro, or whether Brazilian authorities would actually indict him and ask for such a move. On Wednesday, Brazil’s Supreme Court dispelled rumours that a search warrant had been issued for his home.

Flavio’s father, Brazil’s former President Jair Bolsonaro, undertook a very similar journey to Florida after he lost his bid for re-election in 2022. While he was in the US, the elder Bolsonaro’s supporters attempted to orchestrate a coup. Some in Brazil had called for the country to seek his extradition from the US. He later returned to Brazil and is currently under house arrest there.

A person holds a flare as members of parliament from the Democratic Party, Albania's biggest Opposition party, protest against the government inside the parliament in Tirana, Albania, October 29, 2026. | Photo Credit: Reuters

Opposition lawmakers in Albania disrupted a parliament session by letting off flares on Thursday (October 29, 2026) ​following weeks of violent anti-government protests called by students ‌demanding the resignation of Prime Minister Edi Rama.

Members ​of the Opposition Democratic Party of ⁠Albania also blocked access to the podium when a law on the Central Bank was being voted on.

"These (ruling lawmakers) are ‌criminals and they deserve tear gas, rubber bullets, cracked skulls and broken bones, not the ‌young people," Opposition leader Sali Berisha said ‌during his ⁠speech before his microphone was cut off.

Student-led demonstrations, fuelled by anger over perceived government corruption, a lack of jobs and poor education standards, have engulfed central Tirana since mid-October and spread to ‌other cities.

Demonstrators demanding Mr. Rama's resignation have set ​fire to government buildings and banks, smashed shop windows and pulled up paving stones to ⁠throw at police.

Prime Minister ⁠Rama has again called on students for dialogue and introduced new economic measures such ‌as cheaper fuel and more money for poor families in an attempt to stop the violence in ​the streets.
