#778. Von der Leyen’s Canada power play: Words fail the EU boss

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By RT newsroom, a team of multi-lingual journalists with over a decade of experience in Russian and international reporting, delivering original research and insights often missing from mainstream coverage

By RT newsroom, a team of multi-lingual journalists with over a decade of experience in Russian and international reporting, delivering original research and insights often missing from mainstream coverage

EU Commission President Ursula von der Leyen is stirring up trouble at home during a controversial trip to Montreal, turning what should have been a straightforward trade mission into a petty argument over Canada’s fictional “membership” in the bloc.

The EU mandarin traveled to Montreal for the 21st annual EU-Canada summit on Thursday, with her tail between her legs. A month after unilaterally promising Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney “associate member” status of the bloc, she was reportedly forced to scrub all mentions of the term and downgrade her offer to one of an “ever closer association” between the EU and Canada.

When the summit closes on Friday, both sides will issue a revised joint statement that reads: “We will create an even closer association between Canada and the EU... and work to define by the 2027 EU-Canada Summit the concept of an ever closer association of Canada to the EU, short of full membership itself,” according to a draft seen by Policito.

Von der Leyen reportedly gave neither the EU’s vast bureaucracy, nor its members’ wary leaders, any warning before she told Carney in front of the European Parliament that she “would like to work with you on opening the door for Canada to being the first associate member of the European Union.”

Unilateral decisions and boundary-overstepping are von der Leyen’s calling cards, as her fellow Eurocrats have repeatedly complained to the press. “Nobody was consulted about associate membership apart from [von der Leyen’s] closest staff, it would seem,” an unnamed diplomat told The Guardian on Wednesday. The widespread anonymous briefing to various mainstream European media made it clear that France, Germany, Greece and Italy had forced von der Leyen to adopt the new, watered-down terminology.

In her first speech in Montreal on Thursday, von der Leyen refused to adopt the new, sanitized wording. After noting that “Canada cannot become a full-fledged member of the EU because of geography,” she insisted that “we are discussing the idea of Canada becoming the first associate member of the European Union. Our idea is simple: it is an ‘association without accession’.”

Whatever form Friday’s communique will take, von der Leyen has evidently chosen to disregard the concerns of the EU’s largest member states for the sake of her own personal project. However, the difference between both statements is purely rhetorical.

Neither “Associate member” nor “ever closer association” are defined anywhere in EU law.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz attempted to define “associate membership” in May when he suggested that it be granted to Ukraine. Under his proposal, Ukraine would participate in European Council meetings without voting rights, send representatives to the European Parliament, receive more EU funding, and become party to the EU’s mutual defense clause.

The proposal was rejected by Kiev, with Ukrainian leader Vladimir Zelensky arguing that this kind of ‘membership-lite’ would leave his country “present in the European Union, but voiceless.”

“Ever closer association” is an even more meaningless term, lifted straight from ‘Ever Closer Union’, a hagiography on EU integration by the bloc’s court historian, Desmond Dinan. Writing in 1994, Dinan argued that “European integration is a good thing,” and the phrases he used – “ever closer union” and “pooling sovereignty,” for example – have since become staples of EU speeches and press releases.

US President Donald Trump ridiculed von der Leyen’s word games last month, calling the concept of associate membership “laughable,” and threatening to “put very serious tariffs or stop trading with Europe on many things” if the EU-Canada proto-alliance ever harmed US interests.

Carney – who plainly insisted last month that Canada is “not looking to become a member of the European Union” – responded diplomatically to the intra-EU squabbling. “The good news is it doesn’t matter about ⁠specific names or nomenclature,” he said ahead of the summit. “What matters is the difference it will ​make to the lives ​of Canadians.”

However, Von der Leyen’s “membership” promise and the subsequent retraction has undermined Carney in front of his opponents. In a social media post on Thursday, Conservative leader Pierre Poilievre described the concept of “associate membership” as “theater,” and dismissed the summit as “yet another grand theatrical meeting to sign a bunch of meaningless declarations filled with big words (catalyze, anyone?) and promises to meet again to talk more."

How long will this theatre go on? While Canadians can’t afford food and homes, Mr. Carney has distracted for months with big talk about “Associate Membership” with Europe. Now, Europe has rejected it. For the next two days, we will be distracted again from the cost-of-living crisis with wall-to-wall media coverage on yet another grand theatrical meeting to sign a bunch of meaningless declarations filled with big words (catalyze, anyone?) and promises to meet again to talk more. No one’s mortgage or grocery bill will come down. We already have a free trade deal with Europe, Mr. Carney. Get the 9 countries who have not ratified it to do so. And get EU countries to let our beef into the continent and cancel their planned carbon tariff on Canada. Otherwise stop wasting your time and our money. Both are running out.

Von der Leyen has far more riding on this summit than Carney. While the EU needs energy, minerals, and Canada’s geographic clout in the Arctic, Canada only needs to diversify some of its trade away from the US – which accounts for around 72% of its exports and 46% of its imports – in order to hedge against Trump’s tariffs and trade restrictions. At present, the EU accounts for less than 6% of Canada’s exports and 12% of its imports.

🛬 Touchdown in Montréal 🇨🇦 Rarely have I looked forward to a Summit this much. An ocean separates Europe and Canada. Yet we have never felt closer. We have a new ambition: bringing Canada closer to us than ever before, as Associate Member of the EU. And we want to take our unique partnership even further. With an Alliance for the Future, we will stand together and respond to a world of growing rivalries. Montréal, we’re about to write a new chapter in our shared history.

Although the agenda is wrapped up in the language of “shared values” and “respect for the rules-based international order,” Brussels’ priorities in Montreal are clear: the energy-starved EU needs access to Canada’s resources.

According to Euronews, the latest draft of the joint declaration includes a promise to “assess opportunities for long-term energy connectivity,” including the supply of Canadian liquefied natural gas to the EU. Remember that in July 2025 von der Leyen personally pledged to Trump that bloc members would buy $750 billion worth of US energy by the end of 2028, in a trade deal that was widely seen as one-sided.

Canadian Finance Minister Francois-Philippe Champagne has also announced that Ottawa and Brussels intend to sign an agreement on the supply of critical minerals including lithium, nickel, and cobalt. The EU currently controls less than 4% of the world’s supply of these minerals, which are vital in the production of electric vehicles and missiles – both of which the EU intends to increase.

The Europeans also intend to sign defense production and technology-sharing deals, to squeeze cash out of Canada for the bloc’s increasingly stretched €90 billion loan package for Ukraine, and deepen involvement in the Arctic, according to the summit agenda. As the EU set out in its updated Arctic Strategy’ earlier this month, Brussels intends to increase its military presence to challenge Russia in the north, and plans to literally redraw the map of the region to increase its meager Arctic claims, and to pursue closer ties “with like-minded partners,” including Canada.

To challenge Russia in the Arctic, the EU likely has its eyes on Canada’s polar-trained military and its 20-strong icebreaker fleet, compared to the ten such vessels spread across the 27-member bloc. None of these icebreakers are nuclear powered, and even if they were to operate on joint Arctic missions, they would be outnumbered and outclassed by Russia’s fleet of 43, eight of which are nuclear powered

However, any hope von der Leyen may have had of securing preferential single market-style trade deals with Carney have apparently been dashed. According to AFP, the “associate member” version of the joint statement included an “explicit goal of deeper financial market integration.” The new, “ever closer association” communique has replaced this with a promise by both sides to “limit potential negative effects on each other.”

Whether von der Leyen will, by virtue of repetition and against the will of four EU member states, invent an “associate membership” is immaterial. The concept is a legal fiction, and by insisting on the word “membership,” the European Commission president set herself up for an entirely avoidable clash with the French, Germans, Greeks, and Italians.

It’s very interesting to see the Commission president goes so defiantly against the position of Germany — the biggest member state and her home country — on an issue that, as of now, remains strictly theoretical.

The argument over the statement’s wording has overshadowed the summit, but the geopolitical reality remains unchanged: the EU needs Canada’s energy supplies to keep its economy afloat, Canada’s money to prop up its Ukraine project, and Canada’s geography to legitimize its Arctic expansion. For these, it doesn’t matter how the deal is dressed up.

#623. EU pressure on Antigua's 'golden passport' trade threatens budget cushion

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Antigua and Barbuda’s Prime Minister Gaston Browne addresses the 80th United Nations General Assembly at U.N. headquarters in New York, U.S., September 26, 2025. REUTERS/Eduardo Munoz

CASTRIES, St Lucia, Oct 29 - Antigua and Barbuda's likely move to end its citizenship-by-investment program under European Union pressure threatens a vital revenue stream for the island nation and could test the future of Caribbean "golden passport" schemes, analysts said.

The issue exposes a vulnerability across the Caribbean, where several small economies have built government revenues and luxury developments around demand for passports valued partly for the access to Europe they provide.

Prime Minister Gaston Browne said this month Antigua would “very likely” commit to phasing out its citizenship-by-investment program after the EU gave Antigua and four other Eastern Caribbean nations until November 12 to agree to close their schemes by June 1, 2028.

The EU says the programs pose security risks by allowing foreigners with few ties to a country to obtain citizenship and visa-free entry to the Schengen area, a key selling point for passport buyers. Countries that keep the schemes risk losing that access.

The EU had initially requested the June 2028 phase-out in letters sent to Antigua and Barbuda, Dominica, Grenada, Saint Kitts and Nevis and Saint Lucia in June. Under a revised EU visa-suspension mechanism, operating an investor-citizenship program can itself provide grounds for suspending visa-free access, though the suspension is not automatic.

The programs, which require investments starting at $200,000, grant foreign applicants citizenship and a passport, rather than temporary residency, typically in exchange for contributions to state funds or investments in approved projects.

Buyers have included nationals of China, Russia and Middle Eastern countries who would normally need visas to enter the EU, and applicants generally have not been required to live in the Caribbean to obtain citizenship. Demand from Americans has grown since 2020, according to Kristin Surak, a London School of Economics professor who researches investment migration.

Citizenship-by-investment inflows in 2023 equaled about a fifth of the combined GDP of the Eastern Caribbean Currency Union, a group of eight Caribbean economies that share a currency and central bank, according to a 2025 International Monetary Fund paper.

The IMF estimated in a paper published in 2025 that direct investment in projects tied to the passport programs was so large that it represented between 100% to 170% of all foreign direct investment officially recorded across the five countries with such schemes.

Schengen access has traditionally been an important attraction for buyers of Caribbean citizenship-by-investment passports, and losing it could reduce their value while affecting ordinary citizens too, said Surak.

A suspension could force hotels and other developments financed through passport sales to seek fresh capital, delay construction or abandon projects altogether, analysts said.

The immediate stakes are high for Antigua, where citizenship sales are the government’s largest source of non-tax revenue, according to C. Justin Robinson, director of the University of the West Indies Five Islands Campus.

The government collected about $58 million from the program in 2025 and budgeted $44.4 million for 2026, or roughly 7% to 11% of recurrent revenue, he said.

Ending the program would erase more than half of Antigua’s projected primary surplus this year, Robinson estimated.

The consequences could be even greater elsewhere.

In St Kitts and Nevis and Dominica, where such inflows have at times exceeded 10% of GDP, suspension could pose what Robinson called an “existential fiscal question.”

Antigua's experience could also test how aggressively Brussels is prepared to use access to Europe as leverage over citizenship policies beyond its borders. The EU also withdrew Vanuatu’s visa-free access in 2024 over concerns surrounding its investor-citizenship scheme. REUTERS

#424. Georgia woman accused of walking into auto shop and leaving her baby with employees

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Laraven Davis has been charged with child abandonment and reckless conduct

I would like to be emailed about offers, events and updates from The Independent. Read our Privacy notice

A Georgia woman left her baby with auto shop staff only to return with a knife and demand the infant’s return, according to a report.

​Laraven Davis handed her six-month-old daughter to employees at a Jiffy Lube store in Mableton, a city just 20 miles northwest of Atlanta, around 8.30 a.m. Saturday.

“She came up to me and my coworker, Elijah, with a stroller,” assistant manager Jarayya Mayes told Fox 5 Atlanta.

“And she was like, hey, doesn't my baby look so cute? Is my baby so cute? We were like, yeah, babies adorable. And she was. She was absolutely adorable.”

Mayes said she had never spoken to Davis previously but had seen her walking near the business.

​“She just kept saying: ‘The paperwork is in the bag,’” Mayes told the outlet. “‘You guys can just have her if I don’t make it back.’”

​Concerned by the woman’s remarks, Mayes told Fox 5 Atlanta she called 911. Around 15 minutes later, the mom allegedly returned with a knife.

According to Mayes, the mom said: “Where’s the baby? I need the baby. I gotta go, I gotta go. Some stuff just went down. I gotta go.”

Emergency dispatchers told Mayes not to let Davis leave with her baby as cops were on the way.

Officers arrested Davis and she was later charged with child abandonment and reckless conduct.

​The baby was taken to a local hospital for treatment, Cobb County Police Department told The Independent.

​“It was determined that the baby was wearing soiled clothes and had multiple rashes despite being in overall good health,” a spokesperson added.

The infant has been placed in the custody of the Georgia Division of Family and Children Services since Davis has no other family members to care for the baby.

Davis remains in custody at Cobb County Adult Detention Center, according to jail records reviewed by The Independent.

​“As a mom, it broke me down; I did cry a lot,” Mayes told Fox 5. “I was just a mom looking out for another kid.

​“I just couldn’t let that baby go like that. That was heartbreaking.”

The Independent has reached out to the Georgia Division of Family and Children Services and Jiffy Lube for comment.

#389. Albanian opposition storms parliament, sets off flares and smoke bombs

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Opposition lawmakers disrupt parliament session, accusing the prime minister of downplaying ongoing antigovernment protests.

The leader of Albania’s opposition has branded members of the government as “criminals”, while he and fellow lawmakers disrupted a parliamentary session by setting off flares and smoke bombs.

Sali Berisha and other opposition members stormed parliament in the capital, Tirana, on Thursday in support of demonstrators, who are demanding Prime Minister Edi Rama’s resignation. Berisha and his colleagues unsuccessfully tried to prevent a vote on a new law governing the country’s central bank.

“These [ruling lawmakers] are criminals and ⁠they deserve tear gas, rubber bullets, cracked skulls and broken bones, not the young people,” Berisha, leader of the Democratic Party of Albania, said.

The protests began in May and were triggered by Rama’s preliminary approval of a luxury resort linked to Jared Kushner, the son-in-law of United States President Donald Trump.

The student-led protest movement has since evolved into something far bigger, with demonstrators citing economic stagnation, alleged corruption and poor education standards, coupled with high tuition fees.

The anti-government protests are Albania’s largest in decades and have turned violent, injuring hundreds of people, including police officers. Government buildings, banks and private buildings have also been attacked and set on fire.

Rama has been in office for 13 years and has refused to resign after winning a fourth term last year.

As Berisha addressed parliament on Thursday, his colleagues surrounded him while holding photos of protesters allegedly injured by rubber bullets.

Rama has introduced fines of more than $6,000 for anyone who attends a protest that turns violent, even if they did not participate in such acts. That is six times Albania’s average monthly salary of around $1,000.

Rama has announced a series of measures aimed at easing the protests. These include increasing the monthly scholarship stipend for students from low-income families, according to Albania’s public broadcaster, Radio Televizioni Shqiptar. Scholarships have also been extended to master’s degree students. Rama has also announced subsidised rent for students, the broadcaster reported.

#710. At The Cheesecake Factory, a campaign culture war is blissfully ignored

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You have reached your maximum number of saved items.

Remove items from your saved list to add more.

You've used all of your gift articles. 3 more will become available on the 1st of every month.

What in the World, a free weekly newsletter from our foreign correspondents, is sent every Thursday. Below is an excerpt. Sign up to get the whole newsletter delivered to your inbox.

New York City: It’s not quite the seismic “October surprise”, but the final week of the US midterm election has been partially consumed by a debate about a restaurant chain – The Cheesecake Factory.

It all began when Barack Obama, stumping for the Democrats at a rally in Cleveland, Ohio, mocked President Donald Trump’s extensive renovation and redecoration of the White House.

“There’s gold everywhere,” Obama observed. “What’s up with the gold? He’s got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory.”

Trump was triggered. “The White House … is a symbol of America,” he told reporters during a lengthy rant. “It has to be clean. The columns can’t be broken. The bricks can’t be falling off from under the plaster. And for you [Obama] to say it’s a Cheesecake Factory, that just shows why you were a lousy president.”

Senior fellow at the US Studies Centre and former political staffer

Moreover, though, Obama’s jibe led Republicans to proclaim that he and the Democrats were elitist and out of touch. Here he was, they argued, mocking a restaurant chain that is enormously popular with middle America – and at the pointy end of an election campaign.

The Cheesecake Factory is a market-leading operation, with some 370 restaurants across the US (not all under the same brand). It’s renowned for its vast menu: you can order pretty much anything – pastas, steaks, burgers, salads and breakfast items – and, of course, multiple varieties of cheesecake.

It’s also a family favourite – not a cheap night out for mum, dad and the kids, but affordable for a regular treat or special occasion. Most mains are under $US30 ($42.50) – a sandwich with fries and a side salad is $US21 or so.

The Trump administration has responded to Obama’s joke with full force. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent had lunch there on Tuesday, with Fox News’ cameras in tow. He ordered the French dip cheeseburger – his usual, he claimed.

“I had a great meal, always have a great meal, and I would tell the Martha’s Vineyard set to give it a try,” Bessent added, referring to the island synonymous with celebrity retreats.

I felt compelled to see what all the fuss was about, so I headed to the only Cheesecake Factory in New York City, in Queens about 25 minutes by subway from Manhattan.

The restaurant is on the ground floor of a shopping mall, and at lunchtime on a Wednesday, the cavernous room is scattered with friends catching up over burgers and shakes, two solo diners at the bar, and four men near me speaking German. Black, white, Latino – it’s a kaleidoscope of Queens.

The menu – sometimes called “the Bible” – runs to 22 pages, including drinks, plus a separate “skinnylicious” menu with lower-calorie choices, from which I select the grilled steak medallions ($US28.95, 440 calories) and a little house salad ($US9.50, 260 calories).

My server says the restaurant has been busy, but not because of the ruckus between Obama and Trump. Indeed, he hasn’t heard of the kerfuffle, and neither have any patrons. Rather, people are here to try the Cajun salmon, which recently had a viral moment on social media.

“What Obama said was funny and accurate,” says patron Dakota Rylander. She doesn’t buy Trump’s indignation. “That’s just what he does – he tries to distract from what he’s doing by pointing the finger at other people.”

Her friend Faith is more interested in how the restaurant apparently short-changed her on a slice of cheesecake that was meant to be half price for rewards members.

I receive my free bread, with a slab of butter and an unknown number of calories, and the salad, which is fresh (a rarity in the US) and plentiful. The beef medallions come with lots of asparagus, mushrooms, mash and a delicious Madeira wine sauce. Honestly, I can’t fault the meal.

The service is also friendly without being over-the-top in that American way that can sometimes get annoying.

As for the decor, I don’t find it tacky. The lighting, wood panelling and furniture blend orange, red and sandy yellow hues, rather than the overt gold of Trump’s Oval Office trinkets.

I don’t think Obama was really dissing The Cheesecake Factory, and I think Trump quite famously can’t take a joke at his own expense. It’s likely neither of them has been to a Cheesecake Factory in some time. I couldn’t recommend it to them highly enough.

#263. Israel election results: Qatar says accusations of election interference in Israel ‘ridiculous and dangerous’ – as it happened

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Netanyahu’s claim of interference in election also dismissed by Israeli police and opposition parties

Israel’s choice: Netanyahu remaining in power or a Palestinian-backed coalition

Qatar’s foreign ministry spokesman rejected accusations “made by certain Israeli politicians and their contacts in the media” that his country had interfered in the Israeli elections.

“We categorically deny allegations that Qatar has interfered in Israeli elections or funded any activities intended to influence their outcome,” Majed al-Ansari said on X.

“They accuse Qatar of buying off Benjamin Netanyahu, the Israeli political left and now, in this latest campaign, the Arab vote... These accusations are ridiculous and dangerous in equal measure.”

That’s all from me, Tom Ambrose, and from the Israel election results live blog for today.

Benjamin Netanyahu has been accused of using smear tactics to cling to power, after he claimed that Arab Israeli parties had used foreign backing to “steal” Tuesday’s election, in which the prime minister’s coalition lost its parliamentary majority. Netanyahu and his allies called on the internal security service, Shin Bet, to investigate alleged vote buying and other irregularities in Palestinian communities in Israel, supposedly funded by foreign money.

Gadi Eisenkot, the leader of the main opposition bloc, accused the prime minister and his Likud party of trying to derail the election results. “It’s already clear that Likud and Netanyahu have embarked on a coordinated, organised campaign seeking to delegitimise the results of the election,” Eisenkot said in a video message.

Netanyahu’s allegations were quickly disowned by a range of government officials, including police officers and members of the election committee. He was also rebuked by the president for “tarnishing an entire community”. On Wednesday night, the prime minister posted a statement by Likud online which described the claimed violations as “an attack on democracy whose purpose is to lead to a foreign takeover of the Knesset, the Islamisation of Israel, and the overthrow of rightwing rule”.

Opposition leaders lambasted the claims of Netanyahu, 77, who has run Israel for 19 of the past 30 years. Yousef Jabareen, the Joint List leader, accused the prime minister of incitement. “Netanyahu was defeated at the ballot box,” Jabareen said, adding that he “refuses to come to terms with the fact that the Arab public voted and decided”.

Ahmad Tibi, one of the leaders of the Joint List party, said Benjamin Netanyahu was on an orchestrated, toxic campaign meant to undermine the election results. “This entire campaign is directed against Eisenkot, to prevent him, intimidate and scare him off forming a government that relies on external support from the Joint List or Ra’am,” Tibi told Reuters.

Meanwhile, Qatar’s foreign ministry spokesman rejected accusations “made by certain Israeli politicians and their contacts in the media” that his country had interfered in the Israeli elections. “We categorically deny allegations that Qatar has interfered in Israeli elections or funded any activities intended to influence their outcome,” Majed al-Ansari said on X.

Iran attempted to interfere in the Israeli election this week and will try to disrupt the US midterm elections next week, the Telegraph reports. “We have been looking into ways to recruit agents in both the American and Israeli regimes’ election processes,” one official said, adding that “powerful cyber groups have also been established to hack their election systems”.

The Israeli newspaper Haaretz has reported that, with 97% of votes counted, the balance between the political blocs remains unchanged. Netanyahu’s coalition remains at 53 seats and Eisenkot’s bloc at 48. Parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel hold 19 seats. The final tally of absentee ballots is not expected to significantly shift the results, with no grouping managing to reach the 61 seats needed for a majority in the Knesset.

Mahmoud Abbas, the president of the Palestinian Authority, expressed hope that the Israeli election would pave the way for renewed peace talks. “For many years now, we have been extending a hand to the Israeli side in order to achieve peace with them, so that Israel and Palestine may live in security, stability and peace,” Abbas said during a meeting with Cypriot president Nikos Christodoulides in Nicosia.

The Israeli president, Isaac Herzog, has come to the defence of Palestinian voters, as he urged political leaders against “tarnishing an entire community that sought to exercise its right to vote”. He said Shin Bet and the country’s central elections committee would investigate allegations of foreign interference “throughly”.

The central elections committee’s acting director general, Dean Livne, said there was “no basis for claims of systematic violations of election integrity”. The Israeli police also dismissed the claims, according to comments reported in Hebrew-language media, with one source quoted as saying: “Election day passed with very few irregular incidents.”

Israel’s chief opposition candidate Gadi Eisenkot has warned that prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu was working to delegitimise election results with his allegations of malfeasance by parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel that performed well.

“It’s already clear that Likud and Netanyahu have embarked on a coordinated, organised campaign seeking to delegitimise the results of the election,” Eisenkot said in a video message, referring to the prime minister’s right-wing party.

Supreme Court justice Noam Sohlberg, a conservative who heads the Central Elections Committee, requested an investigation by the Shin Bet security service into allegations that Ra’am received money from Qatar.

“The Raamgate scandal is a political earthquake. Israel’s elections have been stolen!” Netanyahu’s Likud party said.

Ra’am chief Mansour Abbas said his party had “no connection whatsoever to the affair” and welcomed an investigation.

Iran attempted to interfere in the Israeli election this week and will try to disrupt the US midterm elections next week, the Telegraph reports.

“We have been looking into ways to recruit agents in both the American and Israeli regimes’ election processes,” one official said, adding that “powerful cyber groups have also been established to hack their election systems”.

Meanwhile, another official described it as an extension of its war with the US and Israel.

“War does not just mean missiles and guns when we are under their blockade,” he said. “It also means we are prepared to disrupt their elections, their banking, their travel and all their ordinary systems. We are not in favour of any candidate anywhere, but we would like to see a divided America and a divided Israel.”

Ahmad Tibi, one of the leaders of the Joint List party, said Benjamin Netanyahu was on an orchestrated, toxic campaign meant to undermine the election results.

“This entire campaign is directed against Eisenkot, to prevent him, intimidate and scare him off forming a government that relies on external support from the Joint List or Ra’am,” Tibi told Reuters.

During and after Tuesday’s election, Netanyahu and his allies warned against rising Arab turnout and his right-wing Likud party issued statements accusing the parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel of “stealing the vote.“

It demanded police and the Shin Bet domestic intelligence service investigate incidents of suspected election bribery and intimidation at ballot stations. It said it possessed evidence of such activity but has not presented it to the ‌public.

The Israeli newspaper Haaretz has reported that, with 97% of votes counted, the balance between the political blocs remains unchanged.

Netanyahu’s coalition remains at 53 seats and Eisenkot’s bloc at 48. Parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel hold 19 seats.

Mahmoud Abbas, the president of the Palestinian Authority, expressed hope that the Israeli election would pave the way for renewed peace talks.

“For many years now, we have been extending a hand to the Israeli side in order to achieve peace with them, so that Israel and Palestine may live in security, stability and peace,” Abbas said during a meeting with Cypriot president Nikos Christodoulides in Nicosia.

“We hope that, immediately after a new Israeli government is formed, we will be able to engage in dialogue together.”

As vote counting continues in Israel, here is a roundup of today’s key developments:

Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies have amplified allegations of election fraud against parties representing Palestinian citizens in Israel. He and members of his coalition have referred the parties to Shin Bet, the country’s domestic security service, for investigation. Netanyahu’s Likud party accused the Palestinian parties of “stealing” the election and claimed, without evidence, that they received money from Qatar.

Qatar’s foreign ministry spokesman, Majed al-Ansari, rejected the allegations, saying they were “ridiculous and dangerous”. Responding to the claims, he said: “We categorically deny allegations that Qatar has interfered in Israeli elections or funded any activities intended to influence their outcome.”

The Israeli president, Isaac Herzog, has come to the defence of Palestinian voters, as he urged political leaders against “tarnishing an entire community that sought to exercise its right to vote”. He said Shin Bet and the country’s central elections committee would investigate allegations of foreign interference “throughly”.

The central elections committee’s acting director general, Dean Livne, said there was “no basis for claims of systematic violations of election integrity”. The Israeli police also dismissed the claims, according to comments reported in Hebrew-language media, with one source quoted as saying: “Election day passed with very few irregular incidents.”

Netanyahu’s chief rival, former military chief and Yashar party leader Gadi Eisenkot, warned political opponents against exploiting allegations of foreign interference to delay the swearing-in of the next Knesset, which is set for 10 November.

The sentiment was shared by Yair Golan, the leader of the centre-left Democrats, who accused Netanyahu of trying to “undermine the election results through an ugly delegitimisation campaign” targeting Palestinian citizens in Israel.

So far, Netanyahu’s bloc is projected to win 53 seats, compared with 48 for the grouping led by rival Eisenkot. Palestinian parties are projected to win 19 seats, a record high.

The final tally of absentee ballots is not expected to significantly shift the results, with no grouping managing to reach the 61 seats needed for a majority in the Knesset.

Qatar’s foreign ministry spokesman rejected accusations “made by certain Israeli politicians and their contacts in the media” that his country had interfered in the Israeli elections.

“We categorically deny allegations that Qatar has interfered in Israeli elections or funded any activities intended to influence their outcome,” Majed al-Ansari said on X.

“They accuse Qatar of buying off Benjamin Netanyahu, the Israeli political left and now, in this latest campaign, the Arab vote... These accusations are ridiculous and dangerous in equal measure.”

Both Benjamin Netanyahu and his immediate challenger, Gadi Eisenkot, lack the seats in the Knesset to form a majority government. Eisenkot and his allies face a clear choice.

They could forge a pact with the Arab Israeli parties, currently heading for an historic 19 seats, which would allow them to vote Netanyahu out and form a governing coalition. Or they could enter a unity government with Netanyahu, in return for a ministry or two.

Likud officials are cited in today’s Israeli press as saying Eisenkot will ultimately have no option but to throw in his lot with Netanyahu. Other observers say that would be political suicide, especially as the opposition leader vowed in the campaign he would never collaborate with the prime minister.

However, Eisenkot and key members of his bloc have also ruled out cooperation with the Arab party lists. Breaking either pledge would be costly for them.

The prime minister’s attack on the legitimacy of the Palestinian vote is designed to tip the scales, to force Eisenkot into the Netanyahu camp by increasing the political toxicity of any arrangement with Arab parties, the Joint List and Ra’am.

For its part, the Joint List has ruled out participating in an Eisenkot government because of his bloc’s support for the war in Gaza and the occupation of southern Lebanon, but it has offered to support an Eisenkot minority government with tactical voting in the new Knesset, in the shared interest of ousting Netanyahu.

Even such a transactional arrangement is hard to achieve given the state of Israeli Jewish politics. The only Zionist party that advocates cooperation with Palestinians, the Democrats, won just 6.5% of the total vote by the latest count.

The right and centre-right accounted for 66.5%, while ultra-orthodox Jewish parties won 11%.

Against that background, Eisenkot has doubled down on his determination to form a Zionist government. Such rhetoric makes the prospect of cooperation with Arab parties even more remote, but he is hoping instead to lure away conservative Jews from Likud, just as Netanyahu’s is trawling for defectors from Eisenkot’s “Change” bloc.

This is the current phase of the post-election struggle, and it is one that, for now, excludes Arab Israelis, despite their dramatically increased political strength.

The head of one of Israel’s parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel rejected allegations of electoral fraud and welcomed an investigation, saying his moderate Islamist Ra’am party had “no connection” to the matter.

“Ra’am has no connection whatsoever to the affair in question or to any other affairs, and we declare that we will fully cooperate with all law enforcement authorities to uncover the truth,” Mansour Abbas said, according to a spokesperson for his party.

The race to form a government in Israel has begun with characteristically brass-knuckle tactics from Benjamin Netanyahu. He and other members of his coalition have referred the parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel, the only part of the opposition which over-performed in Tuesday’s elections, to the country’s domestic security service, the Shin Bet, for investigation.

The government’s claims centre around the allegation that Qatari funds were used illegally to buy votes and mobilise Palestinian Israeli citizens. There is so far no evidence to support the accusation. Haaretz quotes a senior police official saying there were no signs of election fraud and that Tuesday’s vote was cleaner than usual.

In Nazareth on election day, it was clear that turnout was unusually high because of anxiety and fear of what actions the extremists in Netanyahu’s government might turn their attention to Palestinian communities in Israel if they continue in power.

The only reported irregularities involved the the ruling coalition. Many of the ballots delivered to the polling station were printed only in Hebrew, while monitors from the far right used a variety of ploys to slow down the vote.

There is also a certain irony underpinning the allegations as Netanyahu is mired in a scandal over Qatari payments to his advisers in return for improving the Gulf monarchy’s image.

Israel’s president, Isaac Herzog, has come to the defence of Palestinian voters in the Israeli election, amid accusations by Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies of alleged ballot fraud by parties representing Palestinian voters of Israel.

Herzog said he has spoken to the heads of the country’s central elections committee and Shin Bet regarding allegations of foreign interference in Tuesday’s vote, saying the issue must be “thoroughly investigated”.

“I am closely following developments in the investigation into the suspicions reported this morning,” he said in remarks published by Israeli media.

He added: “But even at a moment like this, we must avoid tarnishing an entire community that sought to exercise its right to vote and take an active part in democracy.”

Herzog called on party representatives ahead of impending coalition talks to reconsider their positions out of a sense of responsibility towards the country.

double quotation markOnce the official results are in, I will meet with representatives of all the political groups. I say this to everyone here – to the public and its leaders – ahead of next week’s consultations: keep an open mind, keep an open heart and broaden your perspective.

There is only one question that should be at the forefront of your minds: what is in the best interests of the country?”

A Palestinian party leader in Israel has accused Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies of undermining the legitimacy of “the clearest election results Israel has seen in decades”.

Sami Abu Shehadeh, chair of the Balad party which forms part of the Arab-led Joint List, said unsubstantiated allegations of foreign interference in the election sought to delegitimise the Palestinian vote.

Abu Shehadeh, a former Knesset member who was effectively forced to drop out of the race himself by the Israeli supreme court, said on X:

double quotation markWhat began with the illegal disqualification of Arab parties escalated into incitement against Arab parties and the Arab public. On election day, hundreds of far-right activists armed with weapons were deployed at polling stations in Arab towns in an attempt to suppress voter turnout. Now, this racist blood libel is entering its next phase, fuelled by dangerous accusations.

Netanyahu and his allies are dangerous. They are laying the groundwork to reject the clearest election results Israel has seen in decades.”

He added that Netanyahu, as “a man charged with bribery, fraud and breach of trust”, was inciting against the Palestinian citizens of Israel, which make up 20% of the population.

double quotation markIn any functioning democracy, those who lose an election accept responsibility and step down. In Israel, the losing side manufactures a racist blood libel and calls on the security forces to use force to overturn the people’s verdict.

We must not let the far right steal the election or drag the country into another election on the basis of fabricated, delusional accusations.

Echoing Ben-Gvir, the far-right Israeli finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, has also called on Shin Bet to interrogate the leaders of parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel, amid claims of alleged foreign election interference that critics say risk casting suspicion on the Arab vote.

“The suspicion of election manipulation using foreign Islamist money is a litmus test for the State of Israel,” Smotrich said on X.

“The heads of the Arab parties must be interrogated by the Shin Bet today.”

Hot on Eisenkot’s heels, Yair Golan, the leader of the centre-left Democrats, has accused Benjamin Netanyahu of trying to “undermine the election results through an ugly delegitimisation campaign” targeting Palestinian citizens in Israel.

Netanyahu’s Likud party has claimed – without evidence – that parties representing these citizens have “stolen” the election and demanded the police and the Shin Bet investigate the allegations.

double quotation markNetanyahu suffered a crushing democratic defeat. The public fired him, and he refuses to accept the verdict of the voters.

Now he is trying to undermine the election results through an ugly delegitimisation campaign against the will of the public, and against the Arab public in particular.

Any genuine suspicion of damage to the integrity of the elections must be investigated to the end.”

Israel’s central elections committee has requested the Shin Bet to investigate alleged foreign interference, the Associated Press reported, citing claims in a letter dated Wednesday of funding by “foreign entities against the Israeli interest” but providing no details or assessment of their validity. The source of the allegations remains unclear, and the committee has requested findings within a week, ahead of the 4 November deadline for certifying election results under Israeli law.

#422. UAE sentences eight people to life over arms supplies to Sudan’s army

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Defendants handed ⁠life sentences amid tensions between Khartoum and Abu Dhabi over Sudan’s war and the RSF.

A court in the United Arab Emirates has sentenced eight people to ⁠life imprisonment over ⁠arms trafficking and money laundering to Sudan’s army, state media report.

The individuals supplied weapons and military equipment to the Sudanese army and were part of a money-laundering network connecting the two countries, the Emirati state-run news agency WAM said on Thursday.

It said the Abu Dhabi Federal Court of Appeal concluded on Thursday that the convicted individuals had participated in an integrated scheme for the illicit trafficking of weapons, ammunition and military equipment.

The accused were also found to have forged documents using companies and bank accounts in the UAE to channel funds relating to the arms deals and “conceal” their sources and destinations.

The court ordered the confiscation of ammunition and communications devices used in the crimes and the deportation of those convicted from the UAE after serving their sentences, WAM reported.

The “arms deals, money laundering endangered UAE interests”, the court order said.

Ties between the UAE and ⁠Sudan have deteriorated ⁠as the Sudanese military has accused Abu Dhabi of supplying arms ⁠to its rival in Sudan’s civil war, the Rapid Support Forces (RSF) paramilitary group, which it has been fighting ‌since April 2023. The UAE has denied ‌the ‌allegation.

The war has displaced more than 14 million people and created severe poverty and food shortages.

This week, more than 100 people – mostly women and children – were killed when military drones hit a busy city market frequented by RSF fighters in the city of Nyala in the western region of Darfur.

Doctors Without Borders, known by its French initials MSF, said a second attack hit a civilian neighbourhood. MSF said a hospital it supports in Nyala received 36 bodies in the hours after that attack, 21 of them wearing civilian clothing, and its teams saw more than 150 wounded in the emergency room.

The Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights has previously accused the RSF of carrying out war crimes and a possible crime against humanity in its takeover of el-Fasher, the capital of North Darfur state, after forcing Sudan’s army to withdraw from its last stronghold in the Darfur region.

A large police presence is expected for the parade to handle the large crowds and heavy traffic.

#586. Healey’s ‘risky’ Budget criticised for failing to tackle worst decade for UK growth on record

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The influential Institute for Fiscal Studies warns that ‘no amount of temporary cost of living packages’ will fix the lack of household income growth, in a damning indictment of Andy Burnham’s approach to government

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

John Healey’s “risky” Budget has exposed Britain to further economic shocks and failed to tackle the “worst decade for growth” for household incomes on record, an influential think tank has warned.

The chancellor was told that the reduced financial headroom he has left himself with as a result of his first Budget could leave the country “more exposed to future bad news”, as government departments also face real-terms value cuts to their spending.

In a damning assessment of the government’s spending plans, the Institute for Fiscal Studies (IFS) hit out at the “temporary cost of living measures” unveiled, which it said were no substitute for “a strong economy”, appearing to take aim at Andy Burnham’s approach since he became prime minister of introducing only minor reductions to energy bills and capping the price of bus tickets at £2.

It comes as another leading thinktank, the Centre for Social Justice (CSJ), found that a “£33bn stealth tax on workers” which it claims has been “wiped out” with increased payments on benefits.

The CSJ has looked at the impact of continuing to freeze income tax thresholds which drag more people into paying the basic rate of 20p, higher rate of 40p and top rate of 45p - equating to £33.1bn in extra revenue. But it noted that benefits are set to rise by £3.4bn.

Joe Shalam, policy director at CSJ, said: “Sickness and disability benefit spending is now forecast to exceed £100bn by the end of the decade and 6.7 million people are on out-of-work benefits, while the rest of the country is dragged into higher tax to pay for inaction. Public support for the system there to protect the most disadvantaged is being pushed to breaking point.”

In its analysis of Mr Healey’s Budget, the IFS said there were “risks” in his approach, which come amid a period of dismal growth for household incomes.

IFS director Helen Miller said while it was “reasonable” to allow headroom to fall to absorb higher borrowing costs, she cautioned: “Higher borrowing will need to be paid for. A lower buffer leaves the Chancellor more exposed to future bad news. There are risks to this approach.”

The government’s fiscal headroom has been slashed by over £8bn to £15bn ​in the 2029/30 fiscal year, down from the budge watchdog, the Office of Budget Responsibility’s (OBR), March estimate of £24 bn under Rachel Reeves.

Ms Miller said the chancellor must do more to tackle slow economic growth, added: “It’s depressing. No amount of temporary cost of living packages will fix that. We need a stronger economy.”

The headline cost of living measure unveiled by the chancellor on Wednesday was the £100 payment for around 20 million homes to help with the cost of energy bills.

While it will likely be welcomed by some of the poorest household, many have said it does not go far enough.

The Resolution Foundation, set up to help people on lower and middle incomes, said the Budget would do little to address “the sharp challenges facing Britain”.

The rebuff for Mr Healey's package of measures is particularly embarrassing because until two years ago the think tank was headed by pensions minister Torsten Bell.

The Confederation of British Industry (CBI) also said the chancellor had delivered a “nothing” Budget which did not “meet the scale of that ambition” set out by Mr Burnham.

Meanwhile, the OBR said the UK’s “economic and fiscal position remains very challenging” after almost two decades of shocks and stagnation, pushing up the national debt.

Professor David Miles of its Budget Responsibility Committee (BRC) warned it marked a “pretty grim picture”.

“If you go back to just before Covid and take that as your base, where we are right now which is what some seven years later, is virtually no growth in per person real household disposable income,” he said.

“That’s pretty unusual in the history of the UK.” He added: “It’s been a dismal period in British economic history.”

Ms Miller also criticised Mr Healey’s “small, unhelpful fiddling with the tax system”, leaving it in a “mess” and calling his new Founders’ Relief, a new capital gains tax relief for founders and early investors of high-growth UK companies, “bonkers”.

“It is far more complex than it should be,” she said.

“And that is because, Budget after Budget, chancellors make seemingly small tweaks that lack principle and don’t systematically move us to a better place. This approach gives us more complexity and more instability. Both have real economic costs.”

The IFS also warned Mr Healey’s decision to leave government departmental statement largely unchanged means “means that the real-terms value of budgets has been cut” in the face of higher inflation.

In Wednesday’s speech in the Commons, Mr Healey committed to spending 3 per cent of economic output on defence by 2030, and to “fully funding” the £4.7bn black hole in the defence investment plan (Dip) left by Sir Keir Starmer.

Departments across government will be asked to reduce their capital budgets by 1 per cent each between 2027-28 and 2029-30 to raise some £3bn. The remaining £1.7bn shortfall will be made up from an “unallocated contingency in the Treasury’s capital reserve”, the Budget said.

“It effectively implies that some public services will see real-terms cuts in next year’s Spending Review,” Ms Miller said.

“Avoiding the cuts implied by both higher inflation and tight envelopes could plausibly require a top-up of £17 billion in 2030-21. That’s true even before thinking about the £11 billion needed to fund defence plans.”

Bee Boileau, a research economist at the IFS, said the plans imply “really very tough times ahead for public services” as a result of inflation, slow spending growth and commitments to defence spending.

“The government could deliver these, but these will imply setting out lots of real terms cuts to departments over the coming years, and there’ll be huge pressure to put in some top-ups,” she said.

“Next year’s spending review will be the real crunch point for this government.”

Ms Miller also said Mr Healey’s decision to fund a £4.7bn black hole in defence spending in part by drawing down on reserves weakens the government’s ability to weather future global shocks.

In a scathing rebuke of the lack of clarity on defence spending in the Budget, she said she was still “no wiser” as to how Mr Healey planned to hit his target of spending 3 per cent of GDP on defence by 2030.

“I am grumpy about how the chancellor decided to fund the relatively small hole in the defence investment plan – that’s the plan that gets defence spending to 2.7 per cent of GDP next year and keeps it there until 2029-30,” she said.

“Much of it came from further cuts to the capital budgets of other government departments. Fine. Priorities change but the rest came from raiding reserves in future years.

“This is worth drawing out: John Healey has now spent 25 per cent of the capital reserve set aside for the next three years. He’s also spent 40 per cent of the day-to-day reserve.

“This is categorically not what the reserves are for. Drawing down reserves is another way the government has reduced its buffer against future shocks.”

#965. Russia, Nigeria sign customs cooperation agreement to ease business, trade

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

NAIROBI, October 29. /TASS/. Russia and Nigeria have concluded an agreement on cooperation and mutual assistance in customs matters, aimed at facilitating business operations in both countries, the Trade Representation of the Russian Federation in Nigeria, Togo, Mali, and Burkina Faso announced on its Telegram channel.

The document was signed on the sidelines of the third Russia-Africa Summit in Moscow. According to the Trade Representation, the agreement will strengthen collaboration between the two nations' customs authorities and foster a more favorable environment for bilateral trade.

Valery Pikalev, Head of the Federal Customs Service of Russia, and Bashir Adewale Adeniyi, Comptroller-General of the Nigeria Customs Service, inked the document. This marks the first such agreement between the two states, with drafting efforts underway since 2023.

The agreement provides for information exchange required for customs control, joint efforts to combat customs offenses, and cooperation in professional training and development for specialists.

Furthermore, the sides discussed tariff preferences during the meeting. Nigeria benefits from the Eurasian Economic Union’s (EAEU) unified system of tariff preferences, meaning import duty rates on certain Nigerian goods are 25% lower than those established by the EAEU’s Common Customs Tariff.

#398. European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde will resign February 7, nine months early

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Published - October 29, 2026 04:56 pm IST - FRANKFURT, Germany

President of the European Central Bank, Christine Lagarde. File | Photo Credit: AP

European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde will resign nine months ahead of the end of her term, a move that opens the way for European leaders to choose a successor ahead of France’s presidential election next year.

Also read | Why have Central banks around the world started hiking rates? | Explained

Ms. Lagarde will leave February 7, the ECB said Thursday (October 29, 2026). Her eight-year term was to end October 31, 2027.

The resignation opens the way for complex political horse trading over her successor and to fill the posts of two other top officials who are leaving next year.

The announcement was not a surprise since there has been speculation that Lagarde would leave early to enable EU leaders to choose a successor while pro-EU French President Emmanuel Macron is still in office. That would guard against the possibility that right-wing presidential candidate Marine Le Pen might win the election and influence the choice.

“That she leaves early is no surprise and does not change the outlook for ECB policies,” said Holger Schmieding, chief economist at Berenberg bank.

European Council President Antonio Costa said that the selection process “will be initiated today and will proceed without delay.”

The term of another top ECB official — chief economist Philip Lane — is to end May 31, while another member of the ECB’s executive board, Isabel Schnabel, is resigning at year end to move to the International Monetary Fund. European leaders are expected to work out a package deal to apportion the three vacancies among member countries in a complex round of political bargaining.

EU leaders would likely seek to reach agreement on all three posts before a summit meeting on December 17-18, said Holger Schmieding, chief economist at Berenberg bank.

He said that leading candidates for Lagarde’s job appeared to be former Netherlands central bank head Klaas Knot and Spain’s Pablo Hernandez de Cos, the general manager of the Bank for International Settlements.

“The European Union is trying to guard itself at least somewhat against the risk that right-wing Marine Le Pen may win the French presidential election in April-May 2027,” Mr. Schmieding wrote in an email. “Lagarde’s successor will still be appointed while the staunchly pro-European centrist Emmanuel Macron still resides in the Elysee Palace.”

MS. Lagarde, the former head of the International Monetary Fund, was appointed in 2011 to succeed Mario Draghi.

#979. Africa seeks expanded Russian investment, industrial partnerships — AU Chairman

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

MOSCOW, October 29. /TASS/. African states are interested in expanding investments by Russian companies and establishing joint industrial production facilities on the continent, African Union (AU) Chairperson and President of Burundi Evariste Ndayishimiye announced at the plenary session of the third Russia-Africa Summit.

"We invite Russian enterprises to invest more in Africa, form partnerships within the industrial sector, develop projects with our local partners, and facilitate the transfer of expertise," he said.

According to the AU Chairperson, African countries seek to move away from an economic model where the continent primarily exports raw materials and imports finished goods.

"We need to develop our own industrial capacity, modernize our agriculture, strengthen our energy infrastructure and logistics networks, and invest more in research and technological development," Ndayishimiye stressed.

He noted that Africa possesses significant natural resources, a growing market, and a young population, while Russia can offer technologies, industrial capabilities, and expert knowledge--a combination that opens up broad prospects for mutually beneficial cooperation.

The AU Chairperson also underscored the need to establish domestic value-added production chains on the continent.

"Our continent must become a genuine hub for production and sales, rather than remaining merely a consumer market," he declared.

Ndayishimiye added that developing productive investments and industrial partnerships, alongside facilitating mutual market access for Russian and African enterprises, should be a priority for the Russia-Africa Partnership Forum’s 2027-2029 action plan.

#699. Fact check: Disposable incomes set to rise each year after 2026

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Responding to the Budget on Wednesday, Opposition leader Kemi Badenoch claimed the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) has forecast “real household disposable income: lower every year”.

Robert Jenrick, Reform UK’s Treasury spokesman, made a similar claim on GB News on Thursday. Speaking of the Chancellor, Mr Jenrick said: “By his own figures he’s saying that living standards for people in this country will decline every single year for the next five years.”

The OBR’s forecast sees real household disposable income (RHDI) – which is likely the measure both politicians are referring to – fall this year, but rise in each subsequent year until 2031.

Compared with its previous forecast in March, RHDI annual growth has been downgraded in some years by the OBR and upgraded in others. However, thanks to the fall in 2026, RHDI is lower in each year of the forecast than the OBR had previously estimated. It appears this is what Mrs Badenoch was referring to.

It is unclear what Mr Jenrick was referring to as there does not appear to be any measure in the OBR forecast by which living standards decline every single year.

The OBR calls real household disposable income “a useful though incomplete measure of living standards”.

According to the OBR’s report, RHDI is forecast to fall this year, but then rise in each of the following years in the forecast. It falls by 0.6% in 2026, then rises by 0.2% in 2027, 0.9% in 2028, 0.7% in 2029, 1.1% in 2030, and 1.0% in 2031.

Compared with its March 2026 forecast, RHDI annual growth was downgraded by 1.3 percentage points this year and 0.5 points in 2027. It was upgraded by 0.2 percentage points in 2028 and 2030, while there was no change to the 2029 forecast. There was no previous forecast for 2031.

Chart 2.6 in the OBR report shows that compared with March’s forecast, RHDI in this forecast is lower each fiscal year than what was previously expected. This is because of the falls early on in the forecast. This is what the Conservative Party pointed to when asked to clarify Mrs Badenoch’s comment.

RHDI falls in 2026, but rises each of the next five years – growing by £766 to 2031. However, compared with the March forecast, RHDI will be £356 lower in 2026 than previously thought, and £250 lower in 2030. These figures are all in constant 2023 prices.

What other forecasts could measure living standards?

The OBR calls real GDP per person “an indicator of average living standards”. However, this increases in every year of the forecast. The lowest rise over the period is 0.9% in 2027, while the highest is 1.6% in 2029.

Average real earnings – both weekly and hourly – could also be seen as a way of measuring how well off people in employment are. However, these also rise in every year of the OBR’s forecast, apart from a dip in 2027.

Reform UK has been contacted to clarify Mr Jenrick’s comments.

#736. Bird flu cases are spiking again in the US. Here is what you need to know about the spread

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The nation’s biggest outbreak in Illinois has sickened close to 1 million chickens

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

H5N1 bird flu activity is spiking at poultry farms across the U.S., with 1.32 million birds affected over the past month, according to the latest federal data.

Illinois’s Clinton County has seen the biggest outbreak of highly contagious virus, with 934,600 chickens reported sick.

There are also large outbreaks in Minnesota, Wisconsin and North Dakota. A turkey farm in Minnesota’s Roseau County has seen 57,000 birds affected, another in North Dakota has had 60,600 birds fall ill and one in Wisconsin has seen 41,400 infections, the Department of Agriculture’s tracker shows.

Past outbreaks at egg-laying farms have resulted in major surge in egg prices for Americans after sick birds were killed. Bird flu has also led to the deaths of farm workers and people exposed to sick animals.

Smaller bird flu outbreaks have been reported in California, Montana, South Dakota, Utah, Ohio, Idaho, Wyoming, Kansas, Kentucky and Washington state since October 5. These outbreaks came from both backyard flocks and commercial farms.

But bird flu is spreading in wild birds, as well. There have been reports of bird flu in Canadian geese, mallards, an American white pelican, a turkey vulture and an American black duck this month. Jackie, the famous California bald eagle, died of bird flu in August, a recent necropsy found.

Birds aren’t the only animals that have been impacted, either. There’s reportedly been concern about bird flu spreading at a mink farm in Utah and Washington just confirmed its first-ever case of bird flu in cattle at two farms a couple of weeks ago.

One unidentified resident exposed to the cattle tested positive for the flu, but has not experienced severe illness and is isolating at home, KING 5 reported.

Between September 2025 and last month, at least 5,000 people have been monitored and more than 150 were tested for bird flu. There have been two deaths tied to bird flu and 72 cases since the outbreak began in 2024, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Bird flu typically spreads through the infected saliva, and feces of infected birds or other animals.

Humans are infected when the virus enters the eyes, nose, mouth or is inhaled. Infections range in severity, but can lead to acute disease that results in death. Symptoms include pneumonia fever, cough, sore throat, runny nose, muscle or body aches, fatigue, trouble breathing and headaches.

Bird flu has yet to spread from human to human - but experts fear it’s only a matter of time.

Spreading cases in mink could help the virus mutate enough to make that transition. Mink have similar lungs and airways to people and past research has shown they are susceptible to spreading human viruses, including bird flu.

“As long as it continues to go mink to mink, then it becomes more and more likely that it could jump to a human,” Gail Hansen, a former Kansas state public health veterinarian and epidemiologist, told ProPublica last week.

The Department of Agriculture told The Independent its Animal & Plant Health Inspection Service does not have a disease control program for mink and that Congress has not appropriate funding for such a program.

“For HPAI, APHIS’ depopulation and indemnity authorities apply only to domestic poultry,” Tanya Espinosa, acting assistant director of Public Affairs, explained in a Thursday email.

“In this case, influenza investigations in mink are led by State animal health officials, with USDA providing confirmatory testing through the National Veterinary Services Laboratories (NVSL),” she said.

Regardless, the service encourages prompt reporting of suspected disease events, Espinosa added.

Another big fear? The return of sky-high egg prices. As animals, including chickens, are slaughtered to slow the spread of bird flu, supplies can be affected. Fewer chickens mean fewer eggs are produced. That hurts supply and can drive prices up.

That is what happened in 2024 when egg prices rose to $8-$10 per dozen. The cost became a major campaign issue with President Donald Trump promising to bring down prices. It also became a symbol of the rising food costs Americans have faced in post-Covid inflation.

Prices have dropped since their high and currently sit at $1.32 per dozen, the USDA says.

#773. Inside Trump’s killing of USAID and where we stand now: ‘A farce to stroke Elon Musk’s ego’

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Nicholas Enrich tried to warn that shutting down America’s primary aid agency would lead to preventable deaths – he tells Liz Cookman that the destruction of USAID has seen the US ‘walk away from our role as a leader in global health’

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

For 42 days, Nicholas Enrich tried to save the life-saving work of the US government’s global health agency from the Trump administration as they set about dismantling it.

Having worked at the United States Agency for International Development (USAID) since 2013, Enrich was appointed head of the Global Health Bureau shortly after President Donald Trump was inaugurated in January 2025.

He had been tasked with restarting vital international health programmes after Secretary of State Marco Rubio announced a waiver to an aid funding freeze. A week before, and hours after taking office, Trump had ordered a 90-day freeze on foreign assistance from the US, the world’s largest aid donor.

Enrich spent the next few weeks “banging my head against a wall trying to get that done and being thwarted at every turn by DOGE, the political appointees and by the restrictions and disruption put in place by the administration,” he told The Independent.

DOGE, the Department of Government Efficiency, was an initiative led by billionaire Elon Musk – CEO of Tesla and Space X – that spearheaded efforts to slash federal spending, including by cutting USAID’s workforce.

“By late February, it was clear that I was not going to be able to implement that waiver and restart programmes, and that it was actually kind of a farce,” he says from his home in the US.

“Meanwhile, Elon Musk, the secretary of state [Marco Rubio], and others were publicly saying that our programme had restarted,” he adds.

Enrich began documenting the obstacles in internal memos, detailing how the administration’s shifting guidance, blocked payment systems and mass contract cancellations had made it impossible to restart life-saving programmes.

In a memo dated February 28 and circulated to colleagues on March 2, he warned that the failure to deliver aid would lead to preventable deaths. About 20 minutes after sending it, he informed staff that he had been placed on administrative leave.

His contract was later terminated when, in July, USAID officially closed, with the State Department taking over its remaining programmes. The agency, set up by President John F Kennedy during the Cold War, had been the backbone of US development assistance for more than six decades.

Enrich believes its closure saw the country “walk away from our role as a leader in global health”.

From the fastest and widest-spreading Ebola outbreak ever, first reported in the Democratic Republic of Congo in May, to tuberculosis, malaria and malnutrition, “the kinds of support USAID used to provide is now the exact areas where we are seeing a breakdown,” he says. “This is mostly a really, really bad story.”

“During the first year after the dismantling of USAID, we saw destruction, suffering, and death on a massive scale. All of which is preventable. And for what?” said Enrich. Estimates have suggested that USAID cuts could have caused between 500,000 and 700,000 global deaths in the first year. Rubio and the State Department have disputed that the aid cuts have directly killed anyone.

Now, a little short of two years after the initial halt to USAID operations, the devastating global consequences are becoming ever clearer, including widespread severe hunger, increasing maternal complications, shortages of lifesaving medicine and spikes in child marriage.

The speed and lack of transition left those on the ground with little time to find alternative funding – the agency had spent billions of dollars a year on disease prevention programmes, food aid, clean water, education, agriculture and emergency relief.

Despite the insistence of Musk and the US government that no one has died as a result of the agency’s dissolution, our reporting has shown otherwise.

The Independent has documented newborn deaths in sub-Saharan Africa, deaths of pregnant women who could no longer access hospitals in Afghanistan, and heavily underresourced humanitarian responses.

Funding for HIV responses in low- and middle-income countries fell by $2.1 billion (£1.6bn) last year, putting decades of progress to combat HIV at risk. The reductions are likely to lead to a resurgence of the AIDS epidemic, United Nations agencies have warned.

In Uganda and Zimbabwe people are dying of HIV because of a lack of access medication, Chief International Correspondent Bel Trew reported for Independent TV. In the Central African Republic, and beyond, hard-won successes in efforts to stop women and babies dying in childbirth have faced a serious setback due to cuts to aid.

The Ebola epidemic, the second deadliest ever, in the DR Congo, Uganda, Kenya and Nigeria has killed almost 5,000 people so far. Health workers have warned repeatedly that the response effort had been hampered by aid cuts.

The true measurable impact is hard to calculate as the eventual dissolution of USAID was compounded by cuts to international aid by the UK and elsewhere. Experts say the repercussions will also take time to manifest, with thousands of front line workers laid off – some of whom collected mortality data.

Enrich has documented his experience at the agency in his book, Into the Wood Chipper: A Whistleblower's Account of How the Trump Administration Shredded USAID. He believes that the dissolution of the agency was not about saving money – “there was no money saved” – or because the agency was wasteful or inefficient. “It was not even about realigning priorities of foreign aid, or [Trump’s] ‘America First’ agenda,” he says.

“It was just destroyed because there were a bunch of people whose job it was to stroke the ego of Elon Musk, who got upset when he wasn't able to do whatever he wanted inside of a federal agency … Then they spread a bunch of lies about what was actually happening there,” he adds.

The Independent has contacted the State Department for further comment.

However, Enrich believes that If USAID had built more sustainable systems over the last 65 years, vulnerable countries would have been made more resilient and we would not have seen the “destruction” that he says he have thanks to aid cuts.

“The idea of transitioning to a place where countries don't need to rely on American aid should, frankly, be the goal of foreign assistance,” Enrich says.

He hopes that the US can now use the opportunity created “from the wreckage that we're seeing now” to build a system in the future that does not come at the cost of dependency. He also has faith in the American people, who are “embarrassed about the way we have broken promises on the global stage and moved away from demonstrating American generosity on kind of a mass scale”.

“Americans are angry at what has been destroyed and what has been lost,” Enrich says, adding that he predicts the US will now decide to reinvest in global health development.

“It's in our national interest because it makes the world a safer and healthier place for people to live,” he says.

This article has been produced as part of The Independent’s Rethinking Global Aid project
