#791. Overweight offshore workers could lose job under new rules, industry body warns

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Hundreds of offshore workers could be at risk of losing their jobs when a new weight limit comes into effect next week, an industry body has warned.

Trade body Offshore Energies UK (OEUK) is bringing in guidelines from November which will require all those working offshore to weigh less than 124kg (19.5st).

OEUK said this is required on safety grounds, as it is the most a person can weigh when they are being winched from a platform into a helicopter during an emergency evacuation.

Since announcing the policy in 2025, the body said it has helped operators identify and implement measures to slim down their offshore workforces.

OEUK health and safety leader Graham Skinner said this included the “wide uptake” of GLP-1 weight-loss jabs, with some employers installing additional fridges to store the medication.

However he said weight-loss jabs are just one of the measures workers are using to trim the pounds, and they are not being treated as a “blanket solution” to the problem.

The number of workers over the 124kg limit has fallen from about 2,200 in early 2025 to 840 earlier this year, with the OEUK saying it is “delighted” so many have achieved it.

However an estimated 241-300 workers are projected to still be above the weight limit when the policy comes into effect, meaning they could face losing their jobs.

Mr Skinner said it is possible some of these people no longer work offshore in the UK, or that they will be moved to onshore roles by their employers.

“We are hoping that the vast majority of workers will have found a way through this,” he said on Thursday.

“But the reality is also that if somebody has chosen not to lose weight (or) doesn’t particularly want to comply with the policy for any reason, then there will be a route out of employment as well.

“Ultimately, it’s a safety policy, and it is related to capability.

“If somebody cannot mobilise for their work because they’re over the weight limit, then they will not be able to work.

“In the worst cases, that would mean somebody will lose their job.”

In the past, the industry made changes to safety equipment to compensate for the growing waistlines of offshore workers.

This included making lifeboats larger and more robust, and ensuring larger workers were seated next to the largest windows when travelling on helicopters.

However Mr Skinner said this was a case of only “addressing certain symptoms” of the problem, and the industry had needed to find a solution.

He explained that while the 124kg limit is based on helicopter winches, capping the weight of workers will benefit “all aspects” of life-saving equipment on offshore platforms.

He also said it could reduce the risk of medical evacuations for health-related reasons – including things like heart attacks and strokes – which in 2025 accounted for 78% of all medivacs.

He said those with the most weight to lose have had the “greatest concerns” about the policy since its announcement last year.

“It’s fair to say that everyone’s gone through that psychological process, that change curve of feeling that initial disbelief, anger, and all those emotions that go on when you’re faced with change,” he said.

“That really landed with those that are greatest affected. Those that have had to face a challenge of losing a significant amount of weight have obviously had the greatest concerns.

“However, the vast majority of workers who are already under 124kg and really unaffected by this are very pleased with the introduction of the weight limit.”

The policy has met with some pushback, with the RMT union saying it is concerned it could provide employers with a “convenient mechanism” to sack workers without proper redundancy procedures or financial compensation.

RMT general secretary Eddie Dempsey said: “Nobody disputes that safety must be the overriding priority when working offshore.

“However, safety policies must be proportionate, evidence-based and developed through meaningful consultation with the workers they affect.

“RMT is calling on OEUK to withdraw the policy and engage in meaningful consultation with trade unions to establish a fair, proportionate and evidence-based approach to offshore safety.”

#409. U.K. PM Andy Burnham’s trip to India could take place soon: British Envoy

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Updated - October 29, 2026 04:39 pm IST - New Delhi

Britain's Prime Minister Andy Burnham. File | Photo Credit: Reuters

British Prime Minister Andy Burnham is keen to visit India and a trip could take place soon, a top U.K. diplomat said on Thursday (October 29, 2026), highlighting the growing partnership between the two countries.

Speaking to PTI, Acting British High Commissioner to India Ben Mellor said Mr. Burnham had conveyed his desire to visit India during a conversation with Prime Minister Narendra Modi after taking office. “I know that Prime Minister Andy Burnham is very keen to come to India," Mr. Mellor said.

"When he and Prime Minister Modi spoke just after he took office as prime minister, he expressed that directly to the prime minister, and I'm sure we'll be able to see that happen soon,” he added.

Mr. Burnham was appointed as prime minister on July 20 this year.

On the India-U.K. economic ties, Mr. Mellor expressed confidence that the two countries would surpass their target of doubling bilateral trade as their economies continue to grow.

The Free Trade Agreement (FTA) came into force 100 days ago. But actually it was signed just over a year ago. Since then we've already seen new deals worth 12 billion pounds, he said.

Comment | An anchor for India-U.K. ties, their economic partnership

“I have every confidence that as the UK and the Indian economies grow, we will surpass the target of doubling trade between our two countries,” Mr. Mellor added.

India and the U.K. are aiming to double bilateral trade to around $120 billion by 2030.

Mr. Mellor also said the U.K. remained strongly welcoming to Indian businesses and investors, noting that Indian investors had been the second-largest source of new investment projects in the country over the past several years.

He said the India-U.K. Free Trade Agreement (FTA) had further improved the environment for businesses seeking to invest, trade and work across the two countries. “Just two weeks ago, seven mayors from the UK and one local authority brought a large business delegation of over 200 businesses to India to come and look for partners that they could work with,” Mr. Mellor said.

He said the measures announced in Wednesday's U.K. Budget had reinforced that approach by seeking to make the British economy predictable and attractive for investment.

Asked how the Budget would help ordinary British households facing cost-of-living pressures, Mr. Mellor said Chancellor John Healey had announced measures aimed at supporting households, particularly by addressing high energy costs.

“Most households in the UK will benefit from 100 pounds off their energy bills next year,” he said.

Mr. Mellor said the Budget also sought to put the British economy on a stronger footing through tight fiscal rules while addressing economic headwinds arising from the conflict in West Asia.

Asked whether Britain had sufficient fiscal space to withstand another global economic shock, Mr Mellor said the government's plans to reduce borrowing would help lower borrowing costs and increase the economy's capacity to respond to future crises.

"There is also a ready headroom of some 15 billion pounds to deal with any crisis that may come along in the future," he said.

"I think we can be very confident that the steps the Chancellor announced yesterday have put the UK economy on very stable footing whatever the future holds," Mr. Mellor added.

#221. Europe’s central bank chief, Christine Lagarde, steps down for ‘freedom of speech’

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Possible candidate to run World Economic Forum departs ECB nine months early, saying she wants to speak her mind about world affairs

Christine Lagarde, the eurozone’s top central banker, has said she is stepping down nine months early to speak her mind about world affairs and confront the “misrepresentation of the truth”.

Ending months of speculation about her future at the European Central Bank, Lagarde said she would quit her role as president in March rather than October next year because the rules of neutrality that governed her job prevented her from influencing debate about financial and economics affairs.

She spoke at the end of a press conference to explain the ECB’s decision to hold interest rates – maintaining its key deposit rate at 2.5% – despite inflation climbing to 3.8% last month, its highest since September 2023.

Lagarde said: “I ‌want to have the freedom of speech, the freedom of expression and the freedom ‌to engage on matters that are not monetary policy matters.”

The former managing director of the International Monetary Fund has been tipped as a candidate to take over the running of the World Economic Forum, which holds its annual meeting in Davos each January. The WEF founder, Klaus Schwab, stepped down last year after being accused of financial and ethical misconduct, which he denies.

Lagarde’s early departure fires the starting gun on the race to replace her as ECB chief, at a time when the eurozone is struggling with weak growth and above-target inflation. Potential candidates include the former Dutch central bank president Klaas Knot, the Spanish banker Pablo Hernández de Cos, the German central bank president, Joachim Nagel, and Nadia Calviño, the president of the European Investment Bank.

The European Council made up of EU heads of state appoints the head of the ECB, which sets monetary policy across the eurozone.

There has been speculation that Lagarde, a former finance minister of France, has been encouraged to quit early by the French president, Emmanuel Macron, so her successor can be appointed before the possibility of a far-right or far-left presidential candidate being elected to the Élysée Palace. Lagarde will also be able to participate in next April’s elections.

She noted that ECB officials were bound by strict rules on not appearing partisan. “We have an obligation of neutrality; we comment on the situation with the focus on monetary policy,” Lagarde said, adding that the mandate was “the price we pay for being independent”.

Lagarde said she no longer ‌felt comfortable staying silent on broader global issues. “I’m interested in the truth, and I see the truth being very often damaged, contradicted by misrepresentation,” she ​said, without elaborating.

Drawing on more than two decades in international economic and financial roles, she said she had more to contribute. “There are things that I can say – whether people will listen or not is another story. But I feel that I ⁠should say those things and I cannot do so in my current ​position.”

To reassure markets and staff ​that her early departure would not disrupt the central bank’s operations, she added: “A process ​has already started ‌to identify my ​successor.” She said ​there were “plenty of excellent candidates.”

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Macron said on Thursday ⁠that the ​succession at the ECB must ⁠be prepared in “an orderly ​and ‌balanced way” ‌with European partners, and also expressed his deep gratitude to Lagarde for her action ‌during her tenure.

Holger Schmieding, the chief economist at Berenberg bank, said: “The European Union is trying to guard itself at least somewhat against the risk that rightwing Marine Le Pen may win the French presidential election in April/May 2027.

“Lagarde’s successor will still be appointed while the staunchly pro-European centrist Emmanuel Macron still resides in the Élysée Palace. He is a long-term political adversary of Le Pen.”

Lagarde joined the ECB in late 2019, shortly before the start of the Covid pandemic. During her tenure the central bank has also faced the economic consequences of the Russia-Ukraine war, Donald Trump’s trade war and his war in the Middle East.

Lagarde was wearing two plasters on her face in her video message, which she said was because of “a small surgery earlier in the week”. She added: “Follow doctors’ orders and get your skin checked regularly.”
